# Rheumatology
== SLE & lupus nephritis  (1/2)
q: A lady with SLE was put on 60mg steroid dose and instead of tapering she took it daily for 6 weeks, she was later found dead in her bathtub. What is the likely cause of death? | B. Hypertensive intracerebral hemorrhage @SWR-0311
q: {{rep:×2}} 30 something female Medically free complained of fever, arthralgia, oral ulcer and skin rash for 1 week. P/E skin eruption over the face and trunk. Immunology? Testing is ordered - erythrocytes 30 and protein positive - ESR is high - no mention of ANA or anti Ds DNA What is the diagnosis ? | B. SLE @SWR-0771
q: A 30-year-old woman with stable systemic lupus erythematosus presents to the clinic with active polyarthritis for 1 week. Which of the following is the most appropriate management? | A. Prednisolone and methotrexate @SWR-0979
q: A 40-year-old woman presents with pain and swelling of the hands with early morning stiffness for 3 months. She has photosensitivity, hair loss, difficulty climbing stairs, and Raynaud's phenomenon. Examination reveals active arthritis at the second and third proximal interphalangeal joints, malar rash, and healed ulcers at the tip of the second finger. Proximal muscle strength is 3/5 (see lab results). Antinuclear antibody (ANA titer 1: 2540) Which of the following antibodies will most likely confirm the diagnosis? | B. Anti-ribonucleoprotein (RNP) @SWR-1027
q: A 34-year-old woman presented with 2-months history of jaundice and upper abdominal discomfort. She is known case of SLE on hydroxycholorquine. She also gave history of pruritis and fatigue. Clinical examination revealed obesity and hepatomegaly (see lab results and reports), Test Result Normal Values: Direct bilirubin 32 1.5-6.5 mol/L; Total bilirubin 43 3.5-16.5 mol/L; Aspartate aminotransferase 654 12-40 IU/L; Alanine aminotransferase 734 5-40 IU/L; Alkaline phosphatase 129 39-117 IU/L; Gamma glutamyltransferase 60 6 to 37 IU/L; Random Glucose 7 3.9-5.5 mmol/L; Globulin 43 21-36 g/L; US Abdomen: Showed enlarged liver with bright coarse echogenicity. Viral Hepatitis: Negative. ANA: 1:640. Liver Biopsy: Inter phase hepatitis with plasma cells. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? | A. Prednisolone @SWR-1128
q: {{rep:×2}} A patient with a history of alcohol and tobacco use, but no diabetes or skin bronzing, presents for evaluation. Laboratory results show: Marked elevation of liver transaminases; Mildly elevated bilirubin; Positive ANA; Negative anti-dsDNA; Mildly elevated serum ferritin. What is the most likely diagnosis? | C. Autoimmune hepatitis @SWR-1265
q: A patient presents with a positive ANA, negative anti-dsDNA, elevated serum iron, mildly elevated serum ferritin, and normal-to-mildly elevated AST and ALT levels. What is the most likely diagnosis? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-1273
q: Patient presents with active neuropsychiatric lupus. What you should start | C. Cyclophosphamide + cortosteroids @SWR-1378
q: 28-year-old woman with active neuropsychiatric lupus based on clinical manifestations and her workups. With neurosyptoms Which of the following is the most appropriate first-line therapy? | A. Corticosteroids and cyclophosphamide @SWR-1379
q: A patient presents with a skin rash and low platelet count (thrombocytopenia). The patient has a history of hypothyroidism and is currently taking thyroxine (levothyroxine). What is the most likely diagnosis? | C. Immune thrombocytopenic purpura (ITP) @SWR-1879
idea:
- [[0]] "Found dead suddenly" asks for the fastest killer; among steroid complications that is a hypertensive bleed, not a perforation that announces itself with pain.
- [[1]] Oral ulcers + facial rash + proteinuria in a young woman = SLE; oral plus genital ulcers with uveitis = Behcet.
- [[2]] Kidney involvement flips the answer from methotrexate to mycophenolate; joints only = methotrexate.
- [[3]] When lupus features come with Raynaud ulcers and proximal weakness, think overlap and pick anti-RNP.
- [[4]] Interface hepatitis with plasma cells on biopsy means steroids, whatever the obesity and glucose suggest.
- [[5]] In the original version of this question (SWR-1266) with an AST:ALT about 2:1 in a drinker, the answer becomes alcoholic hepatitis.
- [[6]] If the exam stem says "marked" transaminase elevation, go with autoimmune hepatitis; if it says normal to mild, weigh the iron studies.
- [[7]] Single agent options are traps; severe organ SLE always pairs steroids with an immunosuppressant.
- [[8]] If the stem shows seizures with MRI lesions and an antiphospholipid picture (prolonged APTT, positive lupus anticoagulant), anticoagulation becomes the answer instead.
- [[9]] Rash described as malar or photosensitive with thrombocytopenia suggests SLE; petechiae and bruising suggest ITP.
== SLE & lupus nephritis  (2/2)
q: {{rep:×2}} A 38 yeara old right handed woman, 1 week post partum, presents with severe headache and generalized tonic clonic seizures. No fever and focal issues. The headache doesn't improve with paracetamol. Which of the following investigations has the highest diagnostic value? | D. CT brain with CT venogram @SWR-2306
q: 65 women presents with arthritis, fever, weight loss, she had hand synovitis but no rash or nail hemorrhage, presents with SOB or chest pain I'm not sure Echo was normal CXR showed mild plural effusion Labs Normocytuc Anemia Thrombocytopenia High ferritin High ESR/CRP HIGH ANA What's the most likely diagnosis: | C. SLE @SWR-3354
q: A 53-year-old woman presents to the Outpatient Department. She has been unwell for 6 months with arthralgia, fever, weight loss, and more recently has developed pleuritic chest pains. She also has synovitis of the small joints of the hands. There were no rashes or nail fold infarcts. Heart sounds are normal (see lab results and reports). Blood pressure 110/70 mmHg Heart rate 70 /min Respiratory rate 18/min Test Result Normal Values Hb 89 120-160 g/L WBC 3.2 4-10.5 x 109/L Platelet count 99 150-400 x 109/L Neutrophils 80 40-60% Lymphocytes 17 20-40 % MCV 90 80-95 f1 C-reactive peptide 99 <8.2 mg/L ESR 56 3-15 mm/h Clotting time 7 5-10 min Ferritin 920 22-340 pg/L ANA 1:320 =1:40 Pulmonary Angiography CT: Confirms a small left pleural effusion. There is no consolidation, pulmonary embolus, and no lymphadenopathy. ECG: Sinus tachycardia. Which of the following the most likely diagnosis? | C. Systemic lupus erythematosus @SWR-3355
q: SLE pt ( the Diagnosis not mentioned in the case) with poly arthritis w/o CNS symptoms not pregnant | B. hydroxychloroquine @SWR-3431
q: 26 year old female presented with painless left neck lump associated with fatigue and weight loss for 3 months Labs : sorry I couldn't memorise the exact numbers but from what I remember High Uris acid High Cr Slightly low Ca High K What is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Tumor lysis syndrome @SWR-4898
q: Female patient with SLE presented with lower edema. Cr: 150 Albumin: 25 C3 C4 slightly low AST/ALT high MCQ: | A. Constrictive pericarditis @SWR-4962
idea:
- [[0]] If MRI with MRV appears among the options, it beats CT venogram; if only plain CT and CT venogram are offered and the question asks for diagnostic value, pick the venogram.
- [[1]] High ferritin tempts Still's disease, but low counts plus a high ANA make it SLE.
- [[2]] Do not let the ferritin of 920 pull you to Still's disease: WBC 3.2 and ANA 1:320 mean SLE.
- [[3]] If the lupus arthritis persists despite hydroxychloroquine, the add-on is methotrexate, not azathioprine or cyclophosphamide.
- [[4]] Low calcium points to TLS; high calcium with lymphadenopathy points to sarcoidosis or malignancy-related hypercalcemia.
- [[5]] Do not be pulled to renal failure by the creatinine and low complement; raised JVP that worsens on inspiration points to the pericardium.
== Rheumatoid arthritis
q: A woman has dry eyes, morning stiffness lasting more than 1 hour, and involvement of the PIP and MCP joints with elevated inflammatory markers. What is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Rheumatoid arthritis @SWR-0693
q: A 50-year-old man is complaining of pain affecting the wrist joints bilaterally, second and third proximal interphalangeal joints, and right knee joint for the last 4 months. His examination revealed active arthritis at both wrists and second and third proximal interphalangeal joints (see lab results). Which of the following is the most appropriate treatment? | D. Methotrexate, hydroxychloroquine, and prednisone @SWR-0978
q: A 60-year-old woman with a history of rheumatoid arthritis is treated with prednisolone 60 mg orally once daily for an acute exacerbation. She is instructed to continue the treatment for a short period and then gradually taper the dose. However, the patient fails to follow the tapering instructions and continues taking prednisolone 60 mg daily for several days. She is subsequently found dead at home. Which of the following is the most likely cause of death? | C. Hemorrhagic cerebrovascular accident @SWR-1144
q: A 35-year-old man with rheumatoid arthritis for 6 months on methotrexate 20 mg weekly and Prednisolone daily presented to the clinic with increasing joint pain in both hands and feet associated with morning stiffness for 2-hours. On examination, he shows signs of active arthritis at both wrists. Which of the following is the most appropriate additional treatment? | A. Infliximab @SWR-2078
q: methotrexate 20 mg weekly and ibuprofen daily presented to the clinic with increasing joint pain in both hands and feet associated with morning stiffness for 2-hours. On examination, he shows signs of active arthritis at both wrists. Hand X-rays show erosions at multiple joints (see lab results). Test Result Normal Values Hb 120 130-170 g/L (Male) 120-160 g/L (Female) Platelets count 450 150-400 x 109/L WBC 8 4.5-10.5 x 109/L ESR 60 2-10 mm/h (Male) 3-15 mm/h (Female) C-reactive peptide 18 Rheumatoid factor 500Which of the following is the most appropriate additional treatment? | A. Adalimumab not in the choices it was infliximab @SWR-2991
q: A woman presents with joint pain, associated with morning stiffness that lasts one hour. History only thing remarkable was DM. Her physical examination is otherwise unremarkable. Laboratory investigations are significant for elevated ESR and CRP (I’m not sure about other labs but I believe all was normal). Her HbA1c is mildly elevated (i think 7%). Rheumatoid factor was not mentioned. What is the most likely diagnosis? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3099
q: A T2DM woman presents with joint pain, associated with morning stiffness that lasts one hour. She also complained of eye dryness. Her physical examination is otherwise unremarkable. ESR = elevated CRP = elevated A1C = 7 Anti-SSA = negative Rheumatoid factor was not mentioned. What is the most likely diagnosis? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3169
idea:
- [[0]] If the stem stresses dry mouth and eyes with positive anti-Ro/La and only mild joint pain, Sjogren becomes the answer.
- [[1]] When the stem stresses ACTIVE arthritis, pick the option that includes the steroid bridge.
- [[2]] Found dead while STILL taking high-dose steroid = hemorrhagic CVA; collapse after STOPPING steroid = adrenal crisis.
- [[3]] Before starting any anti TNF, screen for latent TB; reactivation is the classic exam complication.
- [[4]] Erosions on X-ray while on methotrexate = add a TNF inhibitor, not another weak or non-RA immunosuppressant.
- [[5]] Mild diabetes in the history is a distractor unless the hands are stiff, waxy and painless with normal markers.
- [[6]] Dry eyes occur both in primary Sjögren and as secondary Sjögren in RA; the stiffness duration, inflammatory markers and serology (anti-SSA/SSB vs RF/anti-CCP) are what separate the options.
== Gout & pseudogout  (1/4)
q: Adult patient came with features of ALL confirmed by BM bx. WBC 110, lactate 2770. Which of the following medications would reduce likelihood of tumor lysis syndrome. | B. Rasburicase @SWR-0154
q: 50 yo male newly diagnosed with BULKY diffuse large B cell lymphoma (DLBCL), came to ER c/o N& V, decreased urine output for 24 h after first cycle of chemotherapy (R CHOP) K 6 P04-3 Ca 1.2 UA 570 What is the drug that help in PREVENT such presentation? | C. Rasburicase @SWR-0155
q: All other labs normal except uric acid of 450-490 (exact value not remembered). What is the best next action? | B. Hydration @SWR-0263
q: A 40-year-old man with a history of recurrent painful attacks affecting the right big toe, was diagnosed with gout and started on allopurinol. He is also a known case of psoriasis for several years. He presented to the clinic with recurrent joint pain affecting the right big toe, left ankle, and bilateral knees. He is using allopurinol regularly as prescribed but has no improvement in his joint pain. Examinations revealed he has active arthritis at the left ankle and knee joints bilaterally uric acid was above than 400 CRP i think was 11 Which of the following is the most likely cause for his joint pain? | C. Psoriatic arthritis. @SWR-0571
q: dult patient came with features of ALL confirmed by BM bx. WBC 110, lactate 2770. Which of the following medications would reduce liklihood of tumor lysis syndrome. | B. Rasburicase @SWR-0593
q: TLS case with very high uric acid what to give? | B. iv fluid, @SWR-0769
q: middle-aged man with diabetes mellitus is on metformin and aspirin. His labs show elevated creatinine and high uric acid. He is planning to travel and asks about prophylaxis for traveler’s diarrhea. Which of the following is the most appropriate option? Creatinine was 120 | B. Fluoroquinolone @SWR-1176
q: elderly patient with a known history of hypertension, who is non-compliant with medications and refuses hospital care, presents to the primary care clinic with complaints of fatigue and extensive pruritus. She was told by her doctors that her renal functions are high Laboratory investigations reveal deteriorating kidney function, while other parameters are within normal limits. Physical examination shows widespread scratch marks. Which of the following is the most likely complication associated with his condition? | B. Gouty arthritis @SWR-1234
q: A patient with Burkit Lymphoma (DLBCL In Another Recall) is receiving B-CHOP chemotherapy. Laboratory results show hyperkalemia (6) and hypocalcemia. (Unable to urinate) What is the most appropriate management (in addition to hydration)? | A. Lasix @SWR-1306
q: A patient with DLBCL/Burkit lymphoma is receiving B-CHOP chemotherapy. After one day he was unable to urinate and additional symptoms (I couldn’t recall it) Laboratory results show: hyperkalemia (6) hypocalcemia Hyperphosphatemia Uric Acid was Not Mentioned What is the next most appropriate step in management in addition to hydration? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-1307
idea:
- [[0]] Every number matters: WBC ≥100 or LDH ≥2x ULN turns allopurinol into rasburicase.
- [[1]] The words BULKY and PREVENT are the clues: high risk tumor = rasburicase even for prophylaxis.
- [[2]] When a TLS question offers hydration, it beats allopurinol and diuretics as the first measure.
- [[3]] Known psoriasis + new asymmetric large-joint arthritis = psoriatic arthritis until proven otherwise.
- [[4]] Rasburicase lowers uric acid already formed; allopurinol only stops new formation; avoid rasburicase in G6PD deficiency.
- [[5]] Allopurinol prevents, rasburicase treats, fluids always come first; dialysis only if fluids fail.
- [[6]] If rifaximin appears among the options it replaces fluoroquinolone; if the patient is healthy, the answer becomes no medication.
- [[6]] ⚠ Current travel medicine guidance discourages routine prophylaxis and no longer favours fluoroquinolones for prophylaxis (adverse effects, resistance; also dysglycemia risk with diabetes); rifaximin is preferred when prophylaxis is justified, and some would key 'No medication'.
- [[7]] Pruritus with normal liver tests and high creatinine is uraemic, so think of CKD complications, not liver disease.
- [[8]] If rasburicase were listed with a high uric acid, the debate shifts (see the undecided version of this recall).
- [[9]] Uric acid mentioned high → rasburicase; uric acid absent or labs normal with poor urine output → Lasix (the sister version is keyed Lasix).
== Gout & pseudogout  (2/4)
q: Ptn with non-hodgkin lymphoma and he passed away before going through chemotherapy by 1 week wil he died before his chemotherapy session and he is known case of uncontrolled DM2 for 20 years and HTN. Labs: high uric acid, high potassium What's the cause of his death? | B. Tumor lysis syndrome @SWR-1498
q: Patient with ALL presented with puffiness and increased fluid in lower limb and abdomen. She has decreased urine output. Urologist said something about it being obstructed. Labs showed TLS pattern: What is the cause of her decreased urine output? | B. Uric Acid Nephropathy @SWR-1524
q: 40s yo lady diagnosed with breast cancer underwent neoadjuvant chemotherapythen mastectomy surgery, now started radiotherapy sessions, after 2 days comes with this labs -Potassium 6.7 -Creatinine 730 -Uric acid (it was high) What's the cause of her labs | A. radiotherapy induced tumor lysis @SWR-1593
q: {{rep:×2}} Patient came with 1-Day attack Of Gout , what is the appropate management | B. Meloxicam @SWR-2268
q: {{rep:×2}} Patient came with 1-Day attack Of Gout , what is the appropate management Creatinine was HIGH | C. Steroids @SWR-2269
q: A man (sorry forgot age) presented to the clinic for follow up. He was previously informed of his worsening kidney function. He was told to control his hypertension but he doesn't care and doesn't take his medications at all. On physical examination, he has bilateral lower limb edema all the way up to his knees (or thighs). Forgot blood pressure but at least 150/xx Labs showed very high creatinine and BUN, forgot the rest. Which complication is expected to occur in this patient? (something like this) | B. Gouty Arthritis @SWR-2283
q: Patient with ALL presented with puffiness and increased fluid in lower limb and abdomen. She has decreased urine output. Urologist said something about it being obstructed. Labs showed TLS pattern: What is the cause of her decreased urine output? | D. Tumour lysis syndrome ⚠ @SWR-2463
q: Patient with ALL presented with puffiness and increased fluid in lower limb and abdomen. She has decreased urine output. Urologist said something about it being obstructed. Labs showed TLS pattern: What is the cause of her decreased urine output? | D. Tumor lysis syndrome ⚠ @SWR-2535
q: Non-Hodgkin Lymphoma Elderly Patient With Severe Hypertension And Diabetes Mellitus, Taking Potassium-Sparing Diuretics, Scheduled For Chemotherapy. He Presented A Week Before His Chemotherapy Session With Sudden Cardiac Arrest And Death Was Reported. Labs: Potassium: 7.7 High Uric Acid High Creatinine Level No Other Labs Mentioned. What Is The Cause Of His Hyperkalemia? | B. TLS @SWR-2638
q: A patient with lymphoma presents with fatigue and generalized weakness. Laboratory investigations show an elevated uric acid and elevated urea nitrogene (9.5 sure ) . What is the most appropriate initial management? | A. IV hydration @SWR-2656
idea:
- [[0]] Lymphoma + high potassium + high uric acid = TLS, even if chemotherapy has not started yet.
- [[1]] AKI in a leukaemia patient just after starting chemotherapy = uric acid nephropathy from tumour lysis.
- [[2]] Hyperkalemia plus high uric acid plus AKI soon after starting cancer therapy is TLS until proven otherwise; the associated calcium is LOW.
- [[2]] ⚠ TLS after radiotherapy for a solid tumor that has already been resected (mastectomy after neoadjuvant chemotherapy) is very rare because little tumor remains; the key is defensible only as the option that best fits the lab triad and timing.
- [[3]] Add "creatinine high" to the stem and the NSAID drops out: the answer becomes steroids.
- [[4]] Same stem, two answers: normal creatinine = meloxicam; high creatinine = steroids.
- [[5]] If anemia, hyperkalemia or bone disease are not offered, pick the urate complication: falling GFR means rising uric acid.
- [[6]] Narrow mechanism ("crystals obstructing") favours uric acid nephropathy; whole-picture wording favours TLS.
- [[7]] Hypocalcemia in TLS is secondary to phosphate binding calcium; giving calcium when phosphate is high worsens renal calcium phosphate deposition, so reserve it for symptomatic cases.
- [[8]] A medication that raises potassium is the distractor; the extra clue of high uric acid in a lymphoma patient points to TLS.
- [[9]] Initial management = IV hydration; if the question asks for the drug that rapidly lowers an already high uric acid, pick rasburicase, not allopurinol.

A lymph node biopsy is consistent with non-Hodgkin lymphoma. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? Vital signs: Blood pressure: 110/70 mmHg, Heart rate: 76 beats/min, Temperature: 36.3C, Oxygen saturation: 97%. Laboratory results: Sodium 136, Potassium 4.3, Calcium 2.14, Phosphate 0.9, Blood urea nitrogen 9.5, Creatinine 120, Uric acid 490. | D. Intravenous hydration @SWR-2657
q: A 20s year-old girl, K/C of Sickle Cell Disease, presented to the ER with shortness of breath. She was admitted a week ago for respiratory shortness of breath and respiratory problems. She is a non-smoker. Vitals are within normal levels EXCEPT for temperature 38 and respiratory rate of 22. Otherwise, she is stable. Which of the following you should give? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-2885

Labs: Uric acid: 500 Platelets: 60 or 30 They initiated aggressive hydration, allopurinol, and something else. What is the target labs before restarting chemotherapy? | B. Uric acid: 435, something in the middle - I can't recall, but I think the number was 141; pH &lt;= 7 @SWR-3013
q: When to resume chemotherapy after tumor lysis syndrome? | B. Uric acid &lt;475.83 Creatinine &lt;141 Ph>7 @SWR-3014
q: A female was diagnosed with DLBCL. She was started her first cycle of chemotherapy but presented to the ER with a history of fever and decreased urine output for 24 hrs . Labs: Uric acid: 500 Platelets: 60 or 30 They initiated aggressive hydration, allopurinol, and Sodium Bicarbonate. What is the target labs before restarting chemotherapy? | B. Uric acid: 435 , serum creatinine141; pH &lt;= 7 @SWR-3015
q: A female was diagnosed with DLBCL. She was started her first cycle of chemotherapy but presented to the ER with a history of fever and decreased urine output for 24 hrs . Labs: Uric acid: 500 Platelets: 60 or 30 They initiated aggressive hydration, allopurinol, and Sodium Bicarbonate. What is the target labs before restarting chemotherapy? | B. Uric acid &lt;475.83 Creatinine &lt;141 Ph>7 @SWR-3016
q: Old lady with fatigue diagnosed with high grade b cell lymphoma started on chemotherapy came after with more fatigue and decreased urine output K 6.6 Ca 1.5 P 2.7 CR 190 Uric acid 680 What is the initial management? | A. IV normal saline @SWR-3112
q: 30 year old male came to the OPD due to fatigue, fever ( and other one or 2 symptoms) , no lymphadenopathy he received IV fluid . Bobe marrow biopsy showing ALL. Labs: WBCs count the only one was written but forget the number. What also is given to prevent TLS? | B. rasburicase @SWR-3311
q: 34 y/o male. Pressents with right knee swelling and pain. No other symptoms. No other joint involvement Urea 400 Normal other RFT | A. Gout @SWR-3632
q: 30-year-old man presents to clinic with right knee pain. He previously had a gout flare 3 months ago. Currently, he complains of right knee pain. There is no fever, swelling, effusion, or systemic manifestations, only tenderness on palpation. No recent illness. Labs: WBC: normal Serum uric acid: 400 umol/L (high-normal, normal <480) What is the diagnosis? | A. Gout @SWR-3633
idea:
- [[0]] Hypercalcemia also starts with IV fluids, but here the abnormal value is uric acid, and calcium is mildly low, so calcitonin is the trap.
- [[1]] SCD patients are functionally asplenic: fever is treated as bacterial infection until proven otherwise.
- [[2]] Between near identical options, eliminate the one with a still high creatinine and the one with pH 8; then pick the recalled uric acid (435).
- [[3]] Restart only when uric acid is below the TLS threshold, creatinine has recovered and pH is about 7, not 8.
- [[4]] Same uric acid in B, C and D: the creatinine (141 vs 245) and the pH (≤ 7 vs ≥ 8) decide the answer.
- [[5]] Established TLS with high uric acid favours rasburicase (avoid in G6PD deficiency); urine alkalinization with bicarbonate is no longer routinely recommended because it promotes calcium phosphate deposition.
- [[6]] Even when rasburicase or dialysis is listed, the INITIAL management of TLS is IV normal saline; reserve dialysis for failure of medical treatment.
- [[7]] Allopurinol prevents NEW uric acid; rasburicase destroys EXISTING uric acid, so high risk patients get rasburicase (check G6PD first).
- [[8]] Same knee monoarthritis: young man = gout, elderly with chondrocalcinosis = pseudogout; fever and a hot joint = aspirate for septic arthritis first.
- [[9]] A normal or high-normal uric acid does not exclude gout during a flare; the history of a previous attack is the stronger clue.

q: 50 yo male newly diagnosed with [[[[BULKY]]]] diffuse large B cell lymphoma (DLBCL), came to ER c/o N& V, decreased urine output for 24 h after first cycle of chemotherapy (R CHOP) K 6 P04-3 Ca 1.2 UA 570 What is the drug that help in prevent such presentation? | B. Allopurinol @SWR-3977
q: Patient was presented with fatigue and multiple enlarged lymph nodes On labs Everything was normal Expcept for uric acid :480 Lymph node biopsy was : Non-Hodgkin What is the best management | A. IV hydration @SWR-4092
q: Patient came with 1-Day attack Of Gout , what is the appropate management creatinine 200 | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-4135
q: A patient who is a known case of non-Hodgkin lymphoma was started on chemotherapy. A few days later, he presented to the emergency department with decreased urine output. Laboratory investigations showed a serum uric acid level of 850 µmol/L. What is the most appropriate management? | A. IV hydration @SWR-4318
q: 50 yo male newly diagnosed with BULKY diffuse large B cell lymphoma (DLBCL), came to ER c/o N& V, decreased urine output for 24 h after first cycle of chemotherapy (R CHOP) K 6 P04-3 Ca 1.2 UA 570 What is the drug that help in PREVENT such presentation? | B. Allopurinol @SWR-4803
q: 9- 50 yo male newly diagnosed with diffuse large B cell lvmphoma (DLBCL) , came to ER c/o N&V, decreased urine output for 24 h after first cycle of chemotherapy (R CHOP) K6 P04-3 Ca 1.2 UA 570 What is the best initial management in addtion to HYDRATION? | C. RASBIRUCASE @SWR-4816
q: Adult patient diagnosed with ALL presented with TLS, lactate 2267. Which of the following medications would reduce likelihood of tumor lysis syndrome in addition to hydration? | B. Rasburicase @SWR-4885
idea:
- [[0]] The version with rasburicase in the options (SWR-0155) is keyed to rasburicase, because bulky DLBCL is high risk; without it, allopurinol.
- [[1]] If rasburicase is offered for an already raised uric acid in high-risk lymphoma it may be keyed; without it, IV hydration is the answer.
- [[2]] Same gout stem: normal creatinine = NSAID; high creatinine = steroids.
- [[3]] If the question asks what lowers an already high uric acid, rasburicase, not allopurinol; if it asks the first step, hydration.
- [[3]] ⚠ IV hydration is the first step and the key, but current TLS guidance gives rasburicase together with hydration for established TLS with marked hyperuricemia and AKI; some recall versions may key rasburicase.
- [[4]] Read the verb and the options: PREVENT with no rasburicase listed = allopurinol; initial management of established high-risk TLS with rasburicase listed = rasburicase.
- [[5]] Allopurinol stops new urate, rasburicase removes urate already made; the version of this recall keyed to allopurinol tests prophylaxis, while existing hyperuricemia with AKI points to rasburicase.
- [[6]] High risk or established TLS = rasburicase; allopurinol only prevents, it does not treat.
== Spondyloarthritis
q: case of reactive arthritis symptoms during x ray there is no effusion , and also i think the wbc was normal not increased what is the next step ? | A. Ibuprofen @SWR-0329
q: A 40-year-old man presents with fever and a painful swollen left knee for 2 days. Two weeks earlier he had watery non-bloody diarrhea for 5 days after eating at a restaurant. The knee is warm with an effusion, the right wrist is swollen, and the left Achilles tendon is tender. What is the most likely diagnosis? | C. Reactive arthritis @SWR-1372
idea:
- [[0]] Add a hot effusion with fever or high WBC and the next step becomes joint aspiration to exclude septic arthritis.
- [[1]] Diarrhea or urethritis weeks before plus asymmetric arthritis and Achilles tenderness = reactive arthritis.
== Vasculitis
q: Patient with URTI 2 days ago and now has edema and hematuria, labs show normal c3 and c4, negative ANCA, cause? | A. IgA nephropathy @SWR-0818
q: Patient with sore throat( no mention duration) and fever BP 110/70 ( not sureof number but sure normal Bp) but has urinalysis with erythrocytes | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-5053
idea:
- [[0]] Count the days: hematuria within days of the URTI = IgA; weeks later with low C3 = PSGN.
- [[1]] The time gap is the key: "few days" after the throat infection = IgA nephropathy, "1 to 3 weeks" = PSGN.
== Scleroderma, myositis & Sjogren  (1/2)
q: Patient with diabetes, hypertension and renal failure. Blood pressure is around 130/80 and glucose around 7-8. What is the cause of the renal failure? | B. Hypertensive nephrosclerosis @SWR-1322
q: Case: An elderly man with no significant past medical history presented with a hemorrhagic stroke and was treated accordingly. One week later, he developed facial asymmetry and dysarthria. Brain imaging showed a left cerebral infarction. What is the most likely mechanism of the cerebral infarction? | D. Cerebral artery spasm or stenosis @SWR-1635
q: Pt diagnosed by extradural haematoma before 4 week and treated successfully now present by weakness diagnosed by ischemic stroke, no atrial fib no DM or HTN What is cause of ischemia?? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-1741
q: A patient with known multiple sclerosis presents with diplopia. On examination, when asked to look to the right, the left eye fails to look to the right (adduct), while the right(not sure right or left) eye abducts with nystagmus. Where is the most likely lesion? | C. Medial longitudinal fasciculus @SWR-1846
q: Harsh crescendo-decrescendo systolic ejection murmur Radiates to the carotids? | B. aortic stenosis @SWR-2031
q: A 23-year-old woman is brought to the neurology clinic by her family due to episodes of altered behavior occurring for the past 3 months. During these episodes, she becomes unresponsive to her surroundings, stares blankly, and performs repetitive movements such as lip smacking and facial grimacing. Each episode lasts about 1-2 minutes, followed by brief confusion and then return to normal baseline. No history of generalized tonic-clonic seizures. Neurological exam normal. MRI shows hippocampal sclerosis. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Temporal lobe epilepsy @SWR-2055
q: {{rep:×2}} A 25-year-old woman with a known diagnosis of multiple sclerosis (MS) presents to the clinic for follow-up. She is in remission and on teriflunomide. She reports transient worsening of her symptoms when exposed to heat, such as during hot weather or after a warm shower. no neurological manifestations, no fever, and no other symptoms. What is the explanation for her symptoms? | C. Uhthoff phenomenon @SWR-2060
q: Liver Function Abnormalities Findings: Patient came I forgot the symptoms but no UC in the history here are the labs: Liver function in the hundreds (400-700), ANA : positive, ASMA : negative, Ferritin : high, Everything related to iron was high. What was the diagnosis? No autoimmune hepatitis. | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-2297
q: 13- A 52-year-old woman with a 4-year history of diffuse systemic sclerosis presents to the Emergency Department with a severe headache, blurred vision, and new-onset shortness of breath over the past 24 hours. Her blood pressure is 190/110 mmHg. Laboratory results reveal a rising creatinine (from a baseline of 0.9 mg/dL to 2.8 mg/dL). Urinalysis shows mild proteinuria with few red blood cells. What is the most appropriate next step in management? | A. Captopril @SWR-2495
q: A 36-year-old previously healthy man presents with black stool and yellowish sclera. He is a smoker and drinks alcohol. He has no fever, recent travel, or sick contacts. Viral serology is negative. Liver enzymes are elevated. ANA is positive and ferritin is around 400 ng/mL. What is the most likely cause? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-2708
idea:
- [[0]] Flip the durations (long diabetes, recent hypertension) and the answer becomes diabetic nephropathy.
- [[1]] A new deficit days after SAH is vasospasm; a sudden new headache within the first day is rebleeding.
- [[2]] Stroke without risk factors after trauma: look for dissection before blaming atherosclerosis.
- [[3]] Adduction failure with nystagmus in the OTHER eye = MLF; abduction failure of the same eye = 6th nerve.
- [[4]] Same murmur shape in both: carotid radiation, a slow rising pulse or a soft S2 means stenosis.
- [[5]] Staring with automatisms and postictal confusion = temporal lobe (focal impaired awareness); the same staring for seconds with no confusion = absence.
- [[6]] Before calling a relapse, exclude heat and infection; both can bring back old symptoms without new disease activity.
- [[7]] Ferritin, serum iron and even transferrin saturation can all rise in any acute hepatitis as hepatocytes break down; transaminases in the hundreds point to active inflammation, not iron overload.
- [[8]] Steroids precipitate scleroderma renal crisis; ACE inhibitors treat it.
- [[9]] A ferritin in the hundreds in a drinker should not push you to hemochromatosis without a high transferrin saturation.

q: Multiple patient 26 came with worsening symptoms after a fever but no new neurological symptoms. | B. Uhthoff syndrome @SWR-2815

q: A 30 years old female known case of Multiple Sclerosis presented to the ER with diplopia and right upper limb weakness and parasthesia. On neurological exam, patient had diplopia in the right eye when looking to the right, left eye failed to move medially, also there was nystagmus in the right abducted eye. Where is the lesion? | D. Medial longitudinal fasiculus @SWR-3304
q: A female patient with relapsing-remitting multiple sclerosis (RRMS) is receiving treatment with dimethyl fumarate. She has chronic weakness in the right upper limb, with muscle power graded as 4/5. MRI: 5-cm hyperintense new active lesion, while a previously identified T5 spinal cord lesion remains stable. What is the most appropriate next step in management? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3345
q: A case of a female with multiple sclerosis she is doing well. But sometimes her symptoms worsen with sun exposure. (I don't remember what the symptoms are exactly) | A. Uhthoff's sign @SWR-4228
q: Patient in her 60 known case of DM,HT, on insulin and valsartan presents with LL edema and others symptoms. BP: 130/90 Creatinine (High) Proteinuria (high). Imaging showed normal kidney size. What is the diagnosis? | A. Diabetic nephropathy @SWR-4374
q: A male patient in his 50 presents with an episode of left-hand weakness that resolved spontaneously, associated with diplopia and an abnormal gait. Brain MRI performed 1 year ago showed periventricular lesions. A repeat MRI now demonstrates multiple lesions, including a gadolinium-enhancing lesion. Which of the following findings most strongly confirms the diagnosis of multiple sclerosis? | A. Development of new lesions on repeat MRI @SWR-4785
idea:
- [[0]] A new deficit lasting over 24 hours without fever is a relapse; the same symptoms during a fever are Uhthoff.
- [[1]] Post-chemotherapy AKI with high urate: ask for phosphate and potassium; if they are up, it is TLS.
- [[2]] Dry eyes in a patient with true inflammatory MCP/PIP arthritis is secondary Sjogren; the joint disease (RA) is still the diagnosis.
- [[3]] Failure of adduction with intact abduction and convergence is MLF, not a nerve palsy; failure of abduction is the sixth nerve.
- [[4]] Steroids treat the relapse (new symptoms); switching DMT treats breakthrough disease activity.
- [[5]] Before calling a relapse, exclude heat and infection; if the trigger is heat and symptoms settle with cooling, it is Uhthoff.
- [[6]] Small kidneys = chronic disease of almost any cause; normal-sized kidneys in a CKD patient with proteinuria = think diabetes (or amyloid).
- [[7]] A comparison with a prior scan showing new lesions beats a single enhancing lesion for proving time.
== Osteoarthritis & joint/back pain
q: Patient present with lower limb weakness, back pain and urine incontinence T10 sensory level. What is the most appropriate test to diagnose? | A. MRI spine @SWR-0096
q: Pa Patient in 60s diagnosed with prostate cancer presenting with severe back pain and lower limb weakness Most Appropriate Step (NOT INITIAL): | C. IV Dexamethasone @SWR-1121
q: 20's woman presents to the clinic with back pain that is worse in the morning that keeps her not moving from the bed . With hx of playing football On examination there is localized tenderness over the left side and lumbar region (L2-L5) unremarkable What is the most appropriate next step in management? | A. MRI @SWR-2112
q: elderly with osteoarthritis that limit his daily activity, his creatinjne was high (180), most appropriate treatment? | B. paracetamol @SWR-2270
q: elderly with osteoarthritis that limit his daily activity, his creatinjne was high (180), most appropriate treatment? | B. paracetamol @SWR-2315
idea:
- [[0]] A sensory level puts the lesion in the cord, so brain imaging or a urine culture is a trap.
- [[1]] Do not wait for the MRI to give dexamethasone; outpatient imaging is always wrong once there is weakness.
- [[2]] If the stem describes an ordinary sports strain with no morning stiffness or red flags, the answer flips to NSAIDs.
- [[2]] ⚠ Debatable key. Without a duration of 3 months or more or other red flags, a young footballer with localized lumbar tenderness fits mechanical back pain, where NSAIDs without imaging is standard. If axial spondyloarthritis is suspected, a pelvic (sacroiliac) X-ray is usually done first, with MRI if it is negative. The compiler says the full recall is needed.
- [[3]] If the stem says paracetamol was already tried with partial relief and creatinine is high, the answer moves to tramadol.
- [[4]] If the stem says paracetamol was already tried with partial relief and creatinine is high, the answer moves to tramadol.

== Pulmonary embolism

idea:
- [[0]] Confusion without focal signs after immobilization is PE until proven otherwise, not a stroke.

What is next management? | B. Intubation and ventilation @SWR-2863
q: Patient age 33 known case of asthma came to clinic complaining of recurrent attack of asthma She is on slabutamol MDI with multiple use for rescue Which of the following is next step/added to her management? | B. Budesonide MDI @SWR-3039
q: {{rep:×2}} 30-years old woman with asthma. She is adherent on inhaled corticosteroids. However, she gets intermittent asthma exacerbations, especially at night. Her husband smokes in her existence. What is the most appropriate next step? | D. Smoking cessation for household members @SWR-3479
q: Patient transferred from ICU to general ward after management of asthma exacerbation. 2 Days after transfer he became fatigued with less wheezing on chest auscultation (Same wording less wheez not silent chest), Urgent ABG obtained revealed High PCO2 and Low O2. What is the most appropriate next step | B. Intubation with mechanical ventilation @SWR-3771
idea:
- [[0]] Read the current drugs: LABA alone means add ICS; ICS plus LABA already means LTRA.
- [[1]] Oral steroid is tempting after a recent admission, but it comes only after the LTRA step.
- [[2]] Tachypnea and tachycardia are 'severe'; the asthmatic who is tiring, silent or slowing down is 'life-threatening'.
- [[3]] Diminished gag or reduced consciousness flips NIV to intubation.
- [[4]] LABA is never the first add on and never given without an ICS; if the stem already says she is on ICS, then the answer becomes adding a LABA.
- [[5]] If the stem mentions a steroid inhaler with poor adherence or bad technique, the answer flips to inhaler teaching.
- [[5]] ⚠ Debatable key: GINA puts checking inhaler technique and adherence first when asthma is poorly controlled, so B is a strong alternative if the stem implies a controller inhaler; the card follows the author's printed answer A.
- [[6]] Less wheeze in a tiring asthmatic is a silent chest, not recovery; normal or high CO2 = intubate.
- [[7]] Frequent SABA use is a marker of uncontrolled asthma: the answer is a controller (ICS), not a stronger reliever.
- [[8]] If the stem mentions adherence plus a trigger exposure, the answer is the trigger, not the next drug.
- [[9]] Less wheeze in a tiring asthmatic is not improvement; with a rising PCO2 it means imminent arrest.

q: A 22 year old male moved from the ICU to the general floor after being admitted for severe bronchial asthma exacerbation. On day 2 upon examination, he was fatigued and less wheezing on chest auscultation. He is on 10L of oxygen to maintain O2 sat of 90%. An urgent ABG is taken: PCO2 6.7 kPa, pH 7.30, O2 7.8 kPa. Which of the following is the best next step: | C. Intubation and mechanical ventilation @SWR-4673
q: 26 year old male present with mild intermittent asthma and takes herbal supplement to improve his symptoms , which of the following is the most appropriate? | B. Check for adherence to medication @SWR-4804
q: A patient with mild intermittent asthma complains that his asthma is not controlled. He is on inhaled steroids and a long-acting beta agonist. He started a herbal medication, to which he had some response. What is the appropriate next step? | B. Check inhaler technique @SWR-4805
idea:
- [[0]] Same ABG, but an alert patient breathing fast gets NIV; drowsy with RR 10 gets intubated.
- [[1]] In asthma a 'normal' or high CO2 is ominous, unlike COPD where hypercapnia first goes to NIV.
- [[2]] When a patient brings herbals, the exam answer is education about the proven therapy, not approval of the herbal.
- [[3]] "Best oxygen delivery" in COPD = Venturi; switch to BiPAP only when the ABG shows hypercapnic acidosis.
- [[4]] "Less wheeze" in a tiring asthmatic is a silent chest, not recovery; the ABG with high CO2 confirms it.
- [[5]] Less wheeze in a tiring asthmatic is not improvement; with a rising PCO2 it means imminent arrest.
- [[6]] Same ABG, but an alert patient breathing fast gets NIV; drowsy with RR 10 gets intubated.
- [[7]] "Less wheezing" in a tired asthmatic with rising CO2 is a silent chest, not improvement: intubate.
- [[8]] Any stem where the patient self-treats or reports poor control on prescribed therapy: the answer is adherence and technique, not a new drug.
- [[9]] 'Not controlled on current inhalers' is a technique and adherence question until proven otherwise.

== COPD  (1/4)
q: 55y known case of COPD complain of fatigue most appropriate diagnostic step | A. Spirometery @SWR-0212

Positive pressure ventilation @SWR-1221
q: New Recall: (Received On Private After Investigation) A Patient With Acute exacerbation COPD they literally gave him everything for management of COPD (Nebs , SABA , SAMA , ABX) but no O2 given. He does not have confusion or any CNS symptoms or absent gag Or Any Indication For Intubation. ABG: CO2 65 mmhg O2 55 mmhg Ph 7.18 O2 Sat: 70% | D. Intubation and mechanical ventilation @SWR-1222
q: COPD patient mechanically ventilated, shows signs of pulmonary infection, how to prevent this? | A. Elevate head rest 30-45 degrees @SWR-1518
q: copd shortness of breath and respiratory distress, ph :7.24 | B. Mechanical ventilation @SWR-1563
q: COPD patient presented with drowness somolness shortness of breath RR 10/min o2 84% ph 7.25 what is the most appropriate next step | A. endotracheal intubation/mechanical ventilation @SWR-1641
q: it was long case patient with COPD presented with 2 days worsening of symptoms difficulty breathing, cyanosis. PH 7.25 (sure) now is drowsy with SOB how to mange? | A. intubate and ventilation @SWR-1841
q: COPD patient with high flow oxygen pH 7.18 | A. Decrease oxygen flow @SWR-2457
q: 72 y/o old with copd hospitalized due to pneumonia . On lisonopril ,?, and lorazepam .Worsening of sx became agitated and tried to remove iv line. Vitally stable written exactly like this Then o2 89-91% on room air What is the most appropriate initial .... ? | C. review meds, oxygen , and something @SWR-2697
idea:
- [[0]] If the stem describes acute worsening of dyspnea or sputum, switch to chest X-ray.
- [[1]] The same pH and pCO2 give opposite answers: high oxygen means turn it down, low oxygen with drowsiness means intubate.
- [[2]] Same story in the newer version with pH 7.18: the author intubates. pH 7.27 = NIV, pH 7.18 = intubation.
- [[3]] pH 7.27 in the older version = NIV; pH 7.18 in this version = intubation. Read the pH.
- [[3]] ⚠ Current COPD guidance does not set an absolute pH cut off for NIV: an alert, cooperative patient with severe acidosis may still get a monitored NIV trial (in HDU/ICU with a low threshold to intubate). Because the stem denies any intubation indication, B (NIV) is a defensible answer; the author himself calls his cut off arbitrary.
- [[4]] Routine or frequent changes (tube, circuit, scheduled suctioning) are always wrong in VAP prevention; positioning is right.
- [[5]] CPAP is never the ventilation answer in hypercapnic COPD; if BiPAP is offered with an alert patient, weigh it carefully.
- [[6]] Same acidotic gas: alert patient = NIV; drowsy patient with slowing breathing = intubate.
- [[7]] Alert = NIV; drowsy or cannot protect airway = intubate. Same pH, different consciousness, different answer.
- [[8]] Low pH alone does not mean intubate: if the patient is over-oxygenated, turn the oxygen down first.
- [[9]] Benzodiazepines in an elderly patient are a cause of delirium, not its treatment; avoid adding more.

q: A 65-year-old man with COPD is on LABA + LAMA but continues to have frequent exacerbations despite good adherence.. What medication should be added? | B. Inhaled corticosteroid (ICS) @SWR-2739

q: A patient diagnosed with COPD is currently using inhaled salbutamol. He presents complaining of persistent cough that is worse at night despite treatment. What is the most appropriate medication to add? | A. Long acting B2 agonist @SWR-3559
q: A patient with COPD has a pH of 7.24. What is the appropriate management? | A. Mechanical ventilation @SWR-3835
q: COPD patient presented with drowness somolness shortness of breath RR 10/min o2 84% ph 7.25 what is the most appropriate next step | A. endotracheal intubation/mechanical ventilation @SWR-3989
q: COPD needs oxygen was not drowsy what type of ventilation? | A. Non invasive positive pressure ventilation @SWR-4000
q: COPD exacerbation, after viral infection and runny nose After stabilizing the patient and giving steroid salbutamol and sending septic screen, Bp140/80s O2:70% Co2 high 9 Ph:7.2 X ray given ( showed loss of sharpness in costophrenic angle looks overloaded ) What is the most appropriate next step in management? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-4001
q: COPD exacerbation 2ry to pneumonia venturi mask 28% FiO2 Which of the following is the best oxygen delivery system in a pa;ent with Chronic Obstruc8ve Pulmonary Disease? | D. Venturi mask @SWR-4050
idea:
- [[0]] Oral steroids belong to the acute exacerbation, never to long-term maintenance.
- [[1]] Low pH tells you acute vs chronic; high CO2 tells you type 2. Do not mix the two questions.
- [[2]] Acidosis alone pushes to NIV; acidosis plus a lost gag reflex or drowsiness pushes to intubation.
- [[3]] If PO2 is above normal, the answer is less oxygen; if SpO2 is acceptable and the patient is still acidotic, the answer becomes NIV.
- [[4]] Read the diagnosis first: the same "night cough on salbutamol" stem is keyed to ICS if it says asthma and to a long acting bronchodilator if it says COPD.
- [[5]] Read the letters: BiPAP can be right in COPD, CPAP never is for hypercapnia.
- [[6]] Drowsiness plus a LOW respiratory rate is the clue that rules out NIV.
- [[7]] Same blood gas, but the patient becomes drowsy or loses the gag reflex: the answer flips from NIV to intubation.
- [[8]] "Non invasive" alone is not enough: only positive pressure NIV is correct; negative pressure is a distractor.
- [[9]] COPD patient who becomes drowsy on nasal O2 at 4 L/min: the fix is to reduce inspired oxygen, and the device of choice is a Venturi mask.
== COPD  (3/4)
q: Copd pt 69 age came with progressive sob for 3 year with cough and sputum production | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-4219

O2 was the 80ish% Chest X-ray: no infiltrate. What is the most appropriate management? | B. Oxygen + antibiotics + prednisone @SWR-4494
q: Old woman had herpes zoster infection 3 years ago and has l copd and is concerned abt vaccine what should you say to her ? | B. give her vaccine @SWR-4536

q: A 60 year old has symptoms suggestive of COPD(productive cough for 2y i think). He has a 30 year history of smoking but stopped smoking 5 years ago. fev1/fvc <70 and fvc can't remember but was in 60s What is the ( can't remember if best or next )step in management? | C. Alpha-1 antitrypsin level @SWR-5061
q: COPD patient presented with exacerbation he was fully conscious, he was started on high flow oxygen then he started to deteriorate with decreased level of consciousness. PH:7.18 PaO2:15 kpa PaCO2 was high what is the initial thing you would do? | B. Decrease oxygen flow @SWR-5067
idea:
- [[0]] Clubbing is not a feature of COPD; if it appears, think bronchiectasis, ILD or lung cancer.
- [[1]] Every listed pair is part of correct care; the deciding clue is whether the stem signals bacterial infection (purulent sputum, fever) and whether bronchodilator is assumed.
- [[2]] SpO2 88 to 92% without cor pulmonale or polycythaemia is not an LTOT indication; vaccinate instead.
- [[3]] Normal-looking saturation does not exclude CO2 narcosis; a confused COPD patient needs a gas, not a sedative.
- [[4]] The antibiotic decision turns on sputum purulence; a change in sputum colour or volume tips you to add antibiotics, a dry viral flare may not need them.
- [[5]] "She already had shingles" is the trap: zoster recurs, so vaccinate anyway.
- [[6]] Read the letters: BiPAP can be right in COPD, CPAP never is for hypercapnia.
- [[7]] HRCT is not a screening test for lung cancer; screening uses low dose CT.
- [[8]] If spirometry is an option and has not been done, choose spirometry; once obstruction is shown, AAT level is the add-on test.
- [[9]] A high PaO2 in a drowsy hypercapnic COPD patient points to the oxygen, not the lungs, as the problem.
== COPD  (4/4)
q: A 65yo male kc of SEVERE COPD presents with exertional dypnea, drowsiness. On examination he is alert Vitals No fever Bp 100/60 PH 7.1 Which of the following is the most appropriate next step | A. invasive @SWR-5140
idea:
- [[0]] Same COPD stem flips on one word: alert = NIV, drowsy/unconscious or lost gag = intubation.
- [[0]] ⚠ BTS/NICE still recommend an NIV trial for pH below 7.35 (pH below 7.26 in a high dependency setting with intubation ready) unless NIV is contraindicated; the stem also says 'alert', so many would choose NIV (C). Invasive ventilation is favoured only if reduced consciousness is genuine.
== Pneumonia & sepsis  (1/3)
q: Patient with pneumonia (+ yellow sputum), lower patchy opacities, high KFT, what's the causative organism? | B. Legionella @SWR-0418
q: {{rep:×2}} A prisoner presents with fever, productive foul-smelling green sputum, weight loss, and shortness of breath. He appears cachectic.Chest X-ray shows a mass with thick wall and irregular borders and an air-fluid level in the left lower lobe. What is the most appropriate next step in management? | A. Aspiration and antibiotics @SWR-0658
q: 30 years old man teacher medically free present to ER with fever, cough for 3 day. No night sweating. CXR show right lobe consolidation and 25 mm pleural effusion decubitus. What is initial management : | A. Diagnostic thoracocentesis @SWR-0712
q: {{rep:×3}} A Black patient presents with chronic respiratory symptoms, cervical lymphadenopathy, and hepatomegaly. Chest X-ray: bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy with an area of consolidation in the right middle lobe. Which biopsy would help establish the diagnosis? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-1770
q: {{rep:×2}} A Black FEMALE patient presents with chronic respiratory symptoms, cervical lymphadenopathy, and hepatomegaly. Chest X-ray: bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy with an area of consolidation in the right middle lobe. Which biopsy would help establish the diagnosis? | D. Lymph node biopsy @SWR-2229
q: Patient known case of cystic fibrosis has clubbing, presented with signs of pneumonia and its seen on X-ray. Sputum culture showed Staphylococcus aureus which is susceptible to the antibiotic he is already taking. | A. continue the same antibiotic @SWR-2271
q: Case of pneumonia the patient was on o2 mask then became drowsy , SPO2 less than 89 ( i forget ) , they mention its severe to moderate respiratory distress : | A. mechanical ventilation @SWR-2874
q: young pt symptoms of pneumonia on examination crepitations best next investigation to diagnose? | A. CXR @SWR-3254
q: A 40-year-old patient presents with fever, cough, and crackles on lung auscultation, suggestive of pneumonia. What is the initial diagnostic investigation? | C. Chest xray @SWR-3256
q: Patient with cough and sob. on PE there is stony dullness. What is the diagnosis | B. pleural effusion @SWR-3295
idea:
- [[0]] Pneumonia plus kidney, liver, gut or sodium abnormalities = Legionella.
- [[1]] Putrid sputum means anaerobes, so treat the abscess; switch to bronchoscopy when it fails to resolve or when there is no infectious picture.
- [[1]] ⚠ Debatable recall: a thick, irregular-walled cavity with cachexia and weight loss also raises cavitating carcinoma (or TB in a prisoner), for which bronchoscopy with sampling (D) is favoured; and standard care of lung abscess is antibiotics alone first, with drainage only for non-responders.
- [[2]] Pneumonia plus a tappable effusion: the tap, not the antibiotic, is the step being tested.
- [[3]] Bilateral hilar nodes plus lung opacity: bronchoscopic transbronchial biopsy before any open procedure.
- [[3]] ⚠ Current practice biopsies the safest, most accessible involved site; with palpable cervical lymphadenopathy, a peripheral lymph node biopsy (the listed D) is a defensible first choice, and EBUS-guided node sampling is often preferred over transbronchial lung biopsy for hilar disease. Transbronchial biopsy remains the classic exam answer for pulmonary sarcoidosis.
- [[4]] If a palpable node is in the stem, biopsy the node; another version of this recall without it was keyed to transbronchial biopsy.
- [[5]] "Susceptible to the current antibiotic" = keep it; if the stem adds persistent fever despite a sensitive drug, the answer becomes imaging to look for a complication.
- [[6]] Trap: COPD patient on high-flow oxygen who becomes drowsy = CO2 retention, reduce oxygen first; pneumonia without COPD on a mask who becomes drowsy = intubate.
- [[7]] When the lead-in says diagnose or confirm pneumonia, the answer is CXR; culture only wins when the question asks for the causative organism.
- [[8]] Only move to CT when the stem gives a reason (chronic course, suspected bronchiectasis, abscess or normal CXR with ongoing doubt).
- [[9]] Dullness alone is not enough; the word "stony" is what makes it effusion.
== Pneumonia & sepsis  (2/3)
q: Patient known heart disease with pink sputum mucus BP 80/60 HR 125 What is the associated with this shock? | B. increase vascular resistance @SWR-3327
q: Case of septic shock and was given 6 L in fluids but still hypotension (they provided CVP and BP but I can't recall) what to give next: | B. Norepinephrine @SWR-3359
q: Elderly patient (50s-60s), admitted with urosepsis. After ~48 hours, he becomes confused, agitated and disoriented. What is the appropriate management? | B. Regular sleep cycle @SWR-3400
q: Creatinine 60 Crp 120 What type of shock? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3428
q: A patient presents with symptoms of pneumonia (productive cough, fever, SOB). He was admitted and treated with ceftriaxone, azithromycin, tramadol, and paracetamol. His clinical condition subsequently improves. Which medication should be discontinued? | A. Ceftriaxone @SWR-3442
q: Old patient came with urosepsis then developed agitation and irritability I forgot the labs | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3450
q: A patient presented to the clinic with a 3-year history of dyspnea and cough with sputum, the patient quit smoking 5 years ago "It was a long case"+ temperature was normal The pulmonary function test showed an FEV1/FVC ratio <0.6 and FEV1 around 55% (not sure about the FEV1 value but around this number ) What is the next or most appropriate investigation? | C. Alpha-1 antitrypsin ⚠ @SWR-3700
q: 69 year old man k/c of dm came with cough and yellow sputum, oriented to time and place, decrease breath sound in the right lower lung. Tem: 38c RR: 23 Urea: 5 Culture: is pending What is the most appropriate management? | A. Admit IV antibiotics @SWR-3803
q: 20-year-old girl with fatigue and jaundice, chest infection 3 weeks ago. MCV and MCH normal, Hb 92, reticulocytes 7, LDH high, indirect bilirubin high. Blood smear: polychromasia, spherocytes. Most likely diagnosis? | A. Atypical pneumonia ⚠ @SWR-3830
q: 60y old mild productive cough for several weeks Dec breath sounds, Dec tactile fretums, decrese voice transions, dullness on percussion, normal chest exam, temp: 37.5 Initial? | A. Thoracocentesis @SWR-3832
idea:
- [[0]] Cardiogenic shock is the cold, wet pattern: low CO, high PCWP, high SVR; a HIGH CO with LOW SVR is septic (distributive) shock.
- [[1]] Persistent hypotension after adequate fluid means start norepinephrine; another fluid bolus is the trap.
- [[2]] Restraints and sedatives prolong delirium; the exam answer is environmental and sleep-cycle measures plus treating the cause.
- [[3]] Warm peripheries + tachycardia + infection markers = septic; cold + raised JVP = cardiogenic or obstructive.
- [[4]] When an improving pneumonia question asks which drug to stop, think IV to oral switch: the IV-only antibiotic goes first.
- [[4]] ⚠ The stem does not say whether it means IV to oral step down or stopping a drug that is no longer needed; tramadol (an opioid no longer needed once pain settles) is a plausible competing answer. The printed key (ceftriaxone) is defensible only as the IV to oral switch.
- [[5]] In delirium questions, restraints are almost never the answer and sedation is not first line unless the patient is a danger.
- [[6]] Fever or a recent change in sputum flips the answer to sputum culture; a stable, afebrile picture points to alpha-1 antitrypsin.
- [[7]] Same picture in a younger diabetic with CURB-65 of 0 flips to outpatient beta lactam plus macrolide; "culture pending" is never a reason to wait.
- [[7]] ⚠ Strict CURB-65 here is 1 (age only; urea 5, RR 23, oriented), which points to home treatment. Admission is defensible on clinical judgment (elderly with diabetes), and the author calls it a pattern to memorise, but the scoring itself favours outpatient care.
- [[8]] The word "chest infection" points to Mycoplasma; a sore throat with lymph nodes and splenomegaly would point to EBV instead.
- [[9]] If the stem gives increased tactile fremitus with dullness, it is pneumonia, and sputum culture or antibiotics becomes the answer.
== Pneumonia & sepsis  (3/3)
q: 20-year-old girl with fatigue and jaundice, chest infection 3 weeks ago. MCV and MCH normal, Hb 92, reticulocytes 7, LDH high, indirect bilirubin high. Blood smear: polychromasia, spherocytes. Most likely diagnosis? | A. Atypical pneumonia @SWR-3883
q: {{rep:×2}} Prisoner with fever, productive cough, weight loss, and cavitary lesion A 22-year-old prisoner presented with fever, productive cough, and weight loss. On examination, he appeared cachectic and ill. Chest X-ray showed a cavitary mass with an air-fluid level in the left lower lobe. What is the most appropriate management? | C. Aspiration and antibiotics @SWR-4174
q: chronic cough, fever, I am not sure about whole scenario was admitted to ward to be given IV hydration and antibiotic, what is the best prevention or advice I don't remember to be given at the discharge time? but was pneumonia obvious | A. Take pneumococcal vaccine @SWR-4288
q: A 36-year-old heavy smoker presents for lung cancer screening. Which of the following is the most appropriate screening method? | D. Low-dose CT scan @SWR-4497
q: A patient in the ICU has been intubated and mechanically ventilated for 5 days. He develops a fever and is suspected of having ventilator-associated pneumonia , something like this. Which of the following is an appropriate measure to prevent the complication ? ( not same order of answers ) | B. Elevate the head of the bed to 30-45 degrees @SWR-4895
q: A 45-year-old male, smoker for around 15 years, approximately 20 pack-years, came to the clinic concerned because his father had lung cancer at around the age of 60. What is the most appropriate management? | B. Reassure and inform him there is no need for screening now @SWR-4910
q: 65 or 67 patient came with s&s of pneumonia ( diagnosis wasn’t mentioned in case but there was x ray report showing right lobe infiltration he ask about best way to prevent this condition | C. give pneumococcal vaccine @SWR-4922
q: Patient 22 year old prisoner come with fever , greenish sputum cough and weight loss , on physical examination there is clubbing on nails . Chest x-ray shows [recaller: there was a lot of text, honestly forgot it, but after it], left lower lobe MASS. What is your management? | C. antibiotics with aspiration @SWR-5054
idea:
- [[0]] Young patient + hemolysis + chest infection a few weeks ago = Mycoplasma cold agglutinins; swap the chest infection for pharyngitis and lymphadenopathy and the answer becomes mononucleosis.
- [[1]] Do not let "prisoner" auto-trigger TB: the air-fluid level in a lower lobe makes it an abscess.
- [[2]] Age 65 plus pneumonia: the prevention answer is the pneumococcal vaccine, not a mask or hand washing.
- [[3]] If a smoker below 50 is offered screening with a 'no screening' option, that becomes the answer; LDCT is the method only for eligible patients.
- [[3]] ⚠ LDCT is the correct screening method, but a 36-year-old is below current eligibility (age 50 to 80, ≥20 pack-years); the recalled age is likely wrong, and if 'no screening needed' were an option it would be favoured.
- [[4]] Any option that is routine or scheduled (antibiotics, suction, tube changes) is wrong; positioning and early weaning are right.
- [[5]] The trap is the father's lung cancer: unlike colon or breast cancer, family history does not bring lung screening forward; age 50 is the gate.
- [[6]] 'Best way to prevent' pneumonia in an elderly patient = pneumococcal (and influenza) vaccination; hand hygiene is the answer only for spread of hospital contact organisms.
- [[7]] Upper lobe cavity with night sweats in a prisoner = TB and isolation; lower lobe cavity with air fluid level and foul sputum = abscess and antibiotics.

q: A 58-year-old man presents with —— (forget sorry) and weight loss. His BMI is 18 kg/m2. He has a pleural effusion and has experienced similar recurrent episodes over the past few months. Thoracentesis made ( they put result ) Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? | B. Indwelling pleural catheter @SWR-3317
q: A 58-year-old man presents with (chronic cough or hemoptesis) one of them not sure, and weight loss. His BMI is 18 kg/m2. He has a pleural effusion and has experienced similar recurrent episodes over the past few months. Thoracentesis made Pleural fluid analysis shows: Pleural fluid/serum LDH > 0.6 Pleural fluid/serum protein > 0.5 Pleural fluid pH: 7.35 Glucose: not remembered Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? | B. Indwelling pleural catheter @SWR-3318
idea:
- [[0]] If the option says AFB culture and the lead-in says confirm, culture wins; if it says most sensitive, ADA wins.
- [[1]] The phrase "not the confirmatory" is the whole question: culture is confirmatory, so ADA is the answer.
- [[2]] Exudate + weight loss + recurrence means malignant effusion: catheter or pleurodesis, never decortication.
- [[3]] Pleurodesis needs a lung that re-expands and time to benefit; trapped lung or short prognosis flips it to an indwelling catheter.
- [[4]] Do not confuse decortication with pleurodesis: only pleurodesis or a catheter treats a recurrent malignant effusion.
- [[5]] pH 7.35 removes empyema, weight loss makes it malignant, and recurrence calls for a catheter rather than another tap.
- [[6]] If chemical (talc) pleurodesis is listed instead of a catheter, it is an equally valid durable option; never pick decortication or diuretics for a malignant exudate.
- [[7]] A pH near normal (7.35) removes the empyema pathway; the weight loss and recurrence point to a malignant exudate needing long-term drainage.
== Interstitial lung disease & sarcoidosis
q: 68-years old known adenocarcinoma of lung came with SOB and clubbing vital signs normal CT chest: showed apical spiculated mass Hormone receptors negative PET-CT : positive uptake What is the most appropriate management? | D. surgical resection @SWR-3325
q: Known case cystic fibrosis on respiratory habilitation came with fever, culture shows MSSA, what is the next step? | B. Abx @SWR-3691
q: A 55-year-old man, a plumber, presented with a 1-year history of dry cough and progressive exertional breathlessness. Examination shows finger clubbing and bilateral basal crackles (see reports). Chest X-ray: Bilateral reticular shadowing at the lower zone of the lungs. Which of the following has the highest diagnostic value? | C. High-resolution CT scan of the chest @SWR-4096
idea:
- [[0]] Apical (superior sulcus) tumours usually get chemoradiation before resection; if PET shows distant uptake, surgery is no longer the answer.
- [[1]] A sick CF patient with a positive culture gets antibiotics; physiotherapy is an add-on, never the treatment of an infection.
- [[2]] Lower-zone fibrosis = asbestosis or IPF; upper-zone = silicosis, sarcoidosis, TB. For suspected pulmonary fibrosis the test of highest diagnostic value is HRCT, not biopsy, DLCO or serum ACE.

q: A male patient known case of small cell lung cancer came with complain of lethargy and fatigue (no change in LOC or any sign of that). He has no complaints of polydipsia or LL edema. q: {{rep:×2}} 35 y.o male heavy smoker is currently ASYMPTOMATIC found to have a pulmonary nodule on CT, TB culture pending. Which of the following features is most suggestive of malignancy? | B. Nodule size more 2.5 cm @SWR-2785
q: (<50 years, exact age forgotten) asymptomatic smoker presents asking about lung cancer screening. His father died from lung cancer. Which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation? | C. No routine lung cancer screening @SWR-3542
q: -Female patient presents with bilateral recurrent axillary nodules with discharge for one year, what is the diagnosis? | A. Hidradenitis suppurativa @SWR-5118
idea:
- [[0]] Proximal weakness or low sodium = small cell; high calcium = squamous; clubbing with periostitis = adenocarcinoma.
- [[1]] The same SCLC patient flips to hypertonic saline the moment the stem adds confusion or seizures.
- [[2]] Same SIADH, different answer: Na 115 with altered sensorium gets hypertonic saline; Na 126 with fatigue only gets fluid restriction.
- [[3]] Apical location is a distractor because TB also likes the apex; size beats location.
- [[4]] A young smoker with TB culture pending: apex points to TB, age argues against cancer, so size is the only real malignancy clue.
- [[5]] Age and pack-years decide eligibility; a father with lung cancer does not start screening earlier.
- [[6]] One acute boil = furuncle; recurrent bilateral axillary or groin nodules with sinuses = hidradenitis suppurativa.
== Sleep apnea & chronic cough

q: 55 y/o man, complaining of non productive chronic cough since 18 months, 30 year smoking history. [Arabic: weight loss? probably not; B symptoms and exam literally all negative] Most appropriate investigation? | B. B Spirometry @SWR-2698
q: Patient with daytime dreaming, wife says he snores, decreased distance between..? I forgot honestly, ABG: Ph: 7.31 Pco2: 6.6 HCO3: 18 Sure 100% | B. Bipap during sleep at night @SWR-2808
q: A patient presents with poor- sleep and loud snoring and sleep all day , On examination, the sternal notch-chin distance is increased. On auscultation, crepitations were heard. What is the most appropriate treatment? | B. BiPAP @SWR-4482
q: 53 years ago old woman with chronic prod cough that has been going on for 3 years is worse during winter is smoker for 35 years what is the best method to confirm diagnosis : | C. spirometry @SWR-4530
q: A 45-year-old male with a 15-year history of ( GERD symptoms were mentioned) , sleeping with multiple pillows, and non-responsive to maximum-dose antacids, now presents with dysphagia. What is the next investigation? | A. Endoscopy @SWR-5134
idea:
- [[0]] Read the minutes: 'avoid eating 30 minutes before bed' is a trap; the correct gap is 2 to 3 hours.
- [[1]] Add hemoptysis, clubbing or weight loss to this same smoker and the answer flips to CT chest.
- [[2]] If home CPAP is listed, it is usually keyed even with compensated hypercapnia (obesity hypoventilation with OSA); BiPAP is chosen when CPAP is not offered or fails, and intubation only for acute failure with drowsiness.
- [[3]] If CPAP is among the options for plain OSA, CPAP is the best treatment; BiPAP wins when hypoventilation/hypercapnia is the issue or CPAP is not offered.
- [[4]] "Confirm diagnosis" of COPD or asthma = spirometry; imaging is for excluding alternatives, not confirming obstruction.
- [[5]] Dysphagia in a long-standing reflux patient is never treated empirically: scope to exclude stricture, Barrett or cancer.
# Cardiology
== Pericardial & myocardial disease
q: A woman was incidentally found to have high blood pressure. She reports being prescribed antihypertensive medications previously but failed to comply. What is the side effect if her condition is left untreated? | B. Heart failure @SWR-0139
q: Heart failure with reduced ef and dilated cardiomyopathy On Valsartan , statin , metoprolol , spironolactone and furosemide What to add ? | A. Ivabradine @SWR-0385
q: {{rep:×2}} Variceal hemorrhage. Failed octreotide and banding. What is the next step? | B. TIPS @SWR-1804
q: 50 yo man knows case of cardiomyopathy present with cardiogenic shock, he is on metoprolol for chronic heart failure BP 80/50 HR 110 Lactate 4.5 0.5-2.2 What is the appropriate approach ? | C. Discontinue metoprolol immediately @SWR-4195
q: A patient with dilated cardiomyopathy on metoprolol develops cardiogenic shock. What is the most appropriate management? | A. Stop metoprolol immediately ⚠ @SWR-4196
idea:
- [[0]] Pericarditis comes from uremia, not hypertension; if CKD were the stem, uremic pericarditis would fit.
- [[1]] Ivabradine needs sinus rhythm and a maximal beta blocker first; if the stem says AF, ivabradine is wrong and digoxin is the rate drug.
- [[2]] Balloon tamponade buys hours; TIPS is the answer when the question asks for the next definitive step after failed octreotide plus banding.
- [[2]] ⚠ Some sources list balloon tamponade as the immediate step when banding fails (as a temporary bridge), with TIPS after rebleeding; TIPS is the definitive rescue in current guidance, so the key is defensible but a 'temporizing measure' wording would favour D.
- [[3]] Tachycardia rules out beta blocker toxicity, so glucagon is the trap; the answer is to stop the drug.
- [[4]] Glucagon is for a slow heart from beta blocker poisoning; in shock without bradycardia, simply stop the drug.

idea:
- [[0]] Skin GAS infection causes post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis but not rheumatic fever; only pharyngitis triggers rheumatic fever.
- [[1]] A prosthetic valve does not mean prophylaxis for every procedure; a hernia repair or colonoscopy in the same patient gets none.
- [[2]] Oral amoxicillin unless she cannot swallow (ampicillin IV) or is penicillin allergic.
- [[3]] Slow-rising pulse goes with AS; a collapsing pulse goes with AR, even though both lesions sit at the aortic valve.
- [[4]] MVP and HOCM are the two murmurs that get louder on standing; the click points to MVP.
- [[5]] Benzathine penicillin = rheumatic fever prevention; amoxicillin or ampicillin = endocarditis prophylaxis.
- [[6]] In MR, an EF of 55 to 60% already signals LV dysfunction because the leaking valve flatters the EF.
- [[7]] Native valve disease (rheumatic MR, MS) without a prosthesis does not need prophylaxis; it is the prosthesis that makes this patient high risk.
- [[8]] Add the words 'prosthetic' or 'previous endocarditis' and the answer flips to amoxicillin or ampicillin.
- [[9]] MVP and HOCM are the two murmurs that get louder on standing; the click points to MVP.

- [[0]] ⚠ Cardiogenic shock needs hypotension with hypoperfusion, but the recall says vitals were "approximately stable"; the real complication is acute mitral regurgitation, which is missing from the options.
- [[1]] Cardiac catheterization is only for discordant echo findings or to assess coronaries before surgery; ECG and CXR are supportive only.
- [[2]] Inferior MI tempts you to papillary muscle rupture; the sternal border location flips it to septal rupture.
- [[3]] Inferior MI tempts you to papillary muscle rupture; the sternal border location flips it to septal rupture.
- [[4]] "Monthly" penicillin always means prophylaxis; the acute symptomatic treatment is aspirin.
- [[5]] A murmur or regurgitation alone does not qualify; only prosthetic material, prior IE, cyanotic CHD or transplant valvulopathy flip the answer to giving antibiotics.
- [[6]] 'Health maintenance' wording tempts dental prophylaxis; native rheumatic valve disease alone does not need it, get the echo.
- [[7]] New murmur + rheumatic history = echo first; smoking in the stem is a distractor toward CXR.
- [[8]] Diastolic murmurs are never innocent: always echo.
- [[9]] Radiation to the axilla means MR; an apical diastolic rumble that stays put means MS.
- [[9]] ⚠ As worded ("diastolic"), mitral regurgitation cannot be right; an apical diastolic murmur is mitral stenosis. The key MR holds only if the murmur was actually systolic, as the compiler suspects.

q: (Paraphrased, aimed at vasovagal syncope) An 18 year old girl came complaining of an episode of fainting that resolved spontaneously. The episode occurred after seeing a spot of blood. idea:
- [[0]] A gradient of 40 can already be "severe", but without symptoms or LV dysfunction the answer is still follow-up.
- [[1]] Rheumatic valve disease without a prosthetic valve or previous endocarditis no longer gets dental antibiotic prophylaxis.
- [[1]] ⚠ ECG 'immediately' in an asymptomatic patient is not standard; echocardiography is the test to evaluate an AS murmur, and the other version of this recall (SWR-2853) is keyed 'Echo immediately', which is more likely the true option.
- [[2]] Dramatic response of the arthritis to aspirin supports ARF; no response should make you rethink the diagnosis.
- [[3]] 'Preventive advice' tempts you to pick dental antibiotics, but that is outdated for native valve disease; evaluate the murmur.
- [[4]] If the stem says initial step and lifestyle is offered (e.g. pregnancy, no prior treatment), pick lifestyle; once antacids or lifestyle have failed, pick PPI.
- [[5]] 'Treats the arthritis' = aspirin; 'prevents recurrence' = monthly IM benzathine penicillin; a dramatic response to salicylates supports the diagnosis.
- [[6]] Rheumatic heart disease tempts "antibiotics before dental work", but without a prosthetic valve or prior endocarditis it is no longer indicated.
- [[6]] ⚠ The other version of this recall is keyed Echo immediately, which is the test that actually evaluates an aortic stenosis murmur; ECG is acceptable only because echo is not listed here and may be a recall distortion.
- [[7]] Diagnose before you treat: a new murmur gets ECG plus echo before any prophylaxis or surgery decision.
- [[8]] Syncope during exercise in a young person is not vasovagal: think HOCM or arrhythmia and investigate.
- [[8]] ⚠ Current syncope guidelines advise a 12 lead ECG for every patient with syncope; if the full stem did not state a normal ECG, ECG would be favoured. The key of reassurance assumes cardiac causes are already excluded (normal exam, unremarkable echo).

idea:
- [[0]] COPD with right heart strain can raise BNP, so a high BNP supports but a normal BNP is what best excludes heart failure.
- [[1]] Other versions of this recall are keyed to endoscopy; when no investigation option is offered, treat with a PPI.
- [[2]] The primary lipid target is always LDL; HDL is never a treatment target.
- [[3]] Normal ECG does not rule out MI; troponin decides between unstable angina (normal) and NSTEMI (raised).
- [[4]] ST elevation that comes and goes with normal coronaries = spasm; the beta blocker option is the trap.
- [[5]] Dysphagia to liquids more than solids points to motility, so barium swallow then manometry; burning pain worse lying flat points to reflux, so pH monitoring.
- [[6]] A raised BNP in an elderly patient with CKD or COPD may not mean left ventricular failure; obesity can falsely lower it.
- [[7]] After MI the LDL value does not decide whether to start a statin; it is always high intensity.
- [[8]] If angiography/PCI is not among the options (or the setting has no cath lab), the answer becomes thrombolysis.
- [[8]] ⚠ A reference version of this scenario (options without angiography) is keyed to thrombolytic therapy; it notes PCI is preferred when available, so the card's angiography key is consistent with guidelines when both are offered. The recall's option D label "change!" suggests the key may have changed between versions.
- [[9]] Read the verb: 'confirm' means tissue (biopsy); 'next step' or a 'blind' biopsy option means CT first.
