idea:
- [[0]] Bloody diarrhoea, tenesmus and proctitis would flip the answer to UC treatment (mesalamine, steroids for flares).
- [[1]] A single raised BP during acute chest pain is not a diagnosis of hypertension; watch how the stem frames the BP.
- [[1]] ⚠ Debatable key: a single BP of 150/92 taken during acute pain does not establish hypertension, while active smoking is an established major risk factor even at low pack-years, so many would favour smoking (A).
- [[2]] Read the options for the beta-lactam plus macrolide pair: if the outpatient combination is offered (original version), it beats admission.
- [[2]] ⚠ The full version of this question is keyed to outpatient cefuroxime plus azithromycin (CURB-65 0, so no admission). In this modified version that option is missing, so A is the only regimen with correct coverage, but the patient does not strictly need admission.
- [[3]] Abdominal pain in any cirrhotic with ascites = tap the ascites; the neutrophil count, not the fever, makes the diagnosis.
- [[4]] If the real paper gives a stage 2 BP and offers ACEi + CCB, the combination may be the intended answer; with no BP and no combination option, the ACE inhibitor is the safe pick.
- [[4]] ⚠ A version of this exact stem with the same four options is keyed to D (lisinopril and amlodipine, initial dual therapy), while noting the question lacks BP values and labs. The recaller says D was not on the real paper; if it is offered with a markedly raised BP, combination therapy is favoured.
- [[5]] Dyspnea plus crackles without chest pain = think heart failure → BNP; switch to troponin only if the stem describes ischemic chest pain.
- [[6]] Positive occult blood rules out a functional diagnosis (IBS, abdominal migraine).
- [[7]] Thrombolysis needs ST elevation or a STEMI equivalent (new LBBB, posterior MI); never give it for NSTE-ACS.
- [[8]] Long-standing reflux plus new dysphagia is stricture or cancer until endoscopy proves otherwise.
- [[9]] If LDL < 70 mg/dL is among the options for a CHD patient, standard teaching keys it as the primary goal.
- [[9]] ⚠ Standard guidelines make LDL cholesterol the primary target in CHD (option B, LDL < 70 mg/dL), as the Part 1 version of this recall was keyed; triglycerides are only a secondary target.

ECG: ST -segment depression in II, III, and aVE. Which of the following is the most considerable risk factor? | C. Patient's age @SWR-4398
q: A 40-year-old male smoker presents to the Emergency Room with a sudden onset of retrosternal chest pain radiating to the neck. His systemic examination is unremarkable. ECG: ST -segment depression in II, III, and aVF. Which of the following is the most considerable risk factor? | A. Smoking @SWR-4399
q: A 65-year-old man with cardiac disease presents with cough/pink frothy sputum, BP 80/40 mmHg and HR 125/min. Which hemodynamic statement is true? | B. Increased systemic vascular resistance @SWR-4437
idea:
- [[0]] HR clearly low (e.g. 40) with shock = atropine; normal rate with hypotension, raised JVP and clear lungs = RV infarct, fluids.
- [[0]] ⚠ HR 59 is above the usual bradycardia algorithm threshold (below 50), and hypotension in inferior STEMI is classically RV infarction, treated with a fluid bolus; raised JVP with clear lungs is part of that RV infarct pattern, not proof of adequate preload. IV fluid is arguably the better first step unless the full recall shows a clearly low rate or AV block.
- [[1]] "Highest diagnostic value" for fibrosis = HRCT; only a lead-in explicitly asking for histology or definitive tissue diagnosis moves you to biopsy.
- [[2]] Raised JVP with clear lungs in inferior MI means give fluids; a truly slow heart rate means atropine.
- [[2]] ⚠ HR 59 is barely bradycardic, and the recalled picture (inferior MI, BP 70/40, raised JVP) is the classic RV infarction triad; the reference material favours an IV fluid bolus (option A), with atropine only if there is clear symptomatic bradycardia or AV block.
- [[3]] Clear lungs with raised JVP in inferior MI means RV failure, not volume overload; giving nitrates or diuretics drops the BP further.
- [[3]] ⚠ Most guidelines and the reference explanations treat hypotension in RV infarction with an IV fluid bolus first (option A), adding dobutamine only if hypotension persists after volume loading; the key here rests on the compiler's view that 1 to 2 L is excessive.
- [[4]] Only septic has high CO, only cardiogenic has high PCWP, only neurogenic has bradycardia.
- [[5]] Of the four classic types, only cardiogenic raises PCWP, only septic raises CO, only neurogenic lowers HR; SVR is high in both cardiogenic and hypovolaemic.
- [[6]] Raised JVP alone does not mean cardiogenic: look for the obstructive clue; raised cardiac enzymes with no obstructive clue = cardiogenic.
- [[7]] Check the age: 55 gives 'age', the 40-year-old version of the same stem gives 'smoking'.
- [[8]] A young smoker with ACS: smoking is the culprit factor; change the age to 55 and the answer becomes age.
- [[9]] Both cardiogenic and hypovolemic shock have high SVR and low CO; the wedge pressure separates them (high in cardiogenic, low in hypovolemic).

q: A 68-year-old man with a known history of COPD presents to the emergency department with severe shortness of breath and increased work of breathing. On examination, he is conscious, oriented, and cooperative. There are decreased breath sounds at both lung bases. Vital signs: BP: 165/95 mmHg HR: 110/min RR: 30/min SpO2: 84% on room air Temperature: 37.0C ABG: pH: 7.28 PaCO2: 68 mmHg PaO2: 55 mmHg Chest X-ray shows bilateral pleural effusions with pulmonary vascular congestion. What is the most appropriate initial management? | A. IV furosemide + non-invasive ventilation (BiPAP) @SWR-5068
idea:
- [[0]] COPD itself (via right sided failure) can raise BNP, so a raised value is supportive but a normal one is the most useful for ruling heart failure out.
- [[1]] The words 'post NSTEMI' point to cardiogenic shock, where the missing ideas are inotropes and revascularization; vasopressin and hydrocortisone are septic shock add-ons.
- [[2]] Troponin separates a new ACS from old ST changes: normal troponin plus congestion means heart failure / pulmonary edema; a rising troponin flips the answer to acute coronary disease.
- [[2]] ⚠ Debatable key: new ST elevation in contiguous inferolateral leads with acute pulmonary edema in an elderly diabetic is a silent STEMI until proven otherwise (troponin can be normal early, and a value of 0.2 is raised on many assays), which would favour acute coronary disease as the cause. Pulmonary edema is the answer only if the troponin is truly normal and the ST changes are old; compare with old ECGs and repeat troponin.
- [[3]] A pre-renal looking ratio does not mean give fluid; the raised JVP overrides it and points to furosemide.
- [[4]] "Conscious and cooperative" decides NIV over intubation; congestion and effusions add the diuretic.

idea:
- [[0]] The word 'rate control' or AF on the ECG flips the answer to digoxin; sinus rhythm in stable HFrEF keeps it on the ACE inhibitor.
- [[1]] Pump problem = add an inotrope (dobutamine); vessel problem = add or increase a vasopressor.
- [[2]] Normal EF does not stop you giving furosemide; it only stops the HFrEF drug ladder from being the answer.
- [[3]] Classic algorithm threshold is HR below 50; with HR 59 the answer rests on the heart failure signs and the MI context.
- [[4]] Diuretics treat symptoms, not survival: any option that pairs them with a long wait is wrong for new HFrEF.
- [[5]] Wet and warm (congested, BP fine) means more diuretic, not less; stopping furosemide is the trap.
- [[6]] Shock from a failing heart needs an inotrope; shock from dilated vessels needs more vasopressor.
- [[7]] Edema plus dyspnea plus hypotension means congested low output: never give a big fluid bolus.
- [[8]] Inotropes are for the hypotensive, poorly perfused patient; congestion with normal BP gets diuretics.

idea:
- [[0]] In the other version of this recall creatinine is high and V/Q scan replaces Doppler: then the answer is V/Q scan because contrast is avoided.
- [[1]] The stem changes mid-way: 'initially conscious' then hypotensive and drowsy; the LATEST state (unstable) decides the answer.
- [[2]] If an ejection systolic murmur radiating to the carotids is added, think aortic stenosis instead of hypertension.
- [[3]] "Control the rate" = beta blocker/CCB/digoxin; "maintain sinus rhythm" = antiarrhythmic.
- [[4]] Ivabradine is a trap: it is an HFrEF drug but works only in sinus rhythm; propranolol is a trap too, as it is not an HFrEF beta blocker.
- [[5]] Cardioversion is for fast rhythms; slow rhythms get atropine, then pacing.
- [[6]] Fever does not mean septic shock: if the SVR is high, the patient is vasoconstricted, so look at the filling pressure next.
- [[7]] Rate alone rarely decides it: an irregular rhythm points to AF, a fixed rate near 150 to flutter, and a regular very fast narrow rhythm (especially in a child) to SVT.
- [[8]] Confusion or drowsiness in a COPD patient is CO2 retention until proven otherwise; check an ABG and the oxygen flow.
- [[9]] 'Controls the bleeding' = octreotide; 'reduces mortality' = ceftriaxone.

She presented to the ER with sudden change in mental status and confusion, Vital signs: All normal and no fever Which of the following is the best indicator? | B. the most common cause is medications @SWR-1196
q: An asymptomatic patient undergoing employment screening is found to have evidence of left ventricular hypertrophy with strain. Which additional finding is most likely? | A. Blood pressure 170/80 mmHg @SWR-1325

q: An obese woman provided her waist measurement (125 cm) and hip measurement 108cm . Laboratory results show normal HDL and elevated LDL (4.3 mmol/L). Among the following options, Which of the following is the most significant biochemical indicator of health risk? | A. High LDL @SWR-1629
idea:
- [[0]] Read the negatives: no jaundice removes liver, no edema removes overload states, so dehydration with AMS = electrolytes.
- [[1]] When DASH is among the options for "best lifestyle measure to lower BP", it beats exercise, salt restriction and alcohol reduction.
- [[2]] 'Normal lipids' is the distractor: ASCVD needs a statin whatever the LDL.
- [[3]] Muscle pain on a statin: check CK and TSH; hypothyroidism both mimics and worsens statin myopathy.
- [[4]] 'Most accurate' for early ischaemia is MRI; 'next step' in the ED is always non-contrast CT.
- [[5]] Beta blockers for dissection apply only when BP allows; a dissection at 85/55 needs fluids first.
- [[6]] An afebrile elderly patient can still be septic; never accept 'no fever, no infection'.
- [[7]] In an asymptomatic patient, any option describing acute distress (tachypnea, tachycardia, hypoxia) is a distractor; pick the chronic cause.
- [[8]] 'Initial' = Doppler ultrasound; 'confirm' = CT/MR angiography; 'gold standard' = conventional angiography.
- [[9]] Drop the word 'biochemical' and the answer flips from LDL to waist-hip ratio.

q: {{rep:×2}} A 24 years old female, presented with blood pressure of 156-85 (around that), that was confirmed on multiple visits and home reading, she does not take any over the counter medication nor supplements, she does not complain of any medical condition other than obesity, examination revealed a right sided abdominal bruit Which of the following have the highest diagnostic test: | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-2263
q: A young patient with hypertension, I think his BP around 140/90 Abdominal US: shows right renal artery stenosis of 85-80% and left artery stenosis of 75-70% What is the Mx (don't remember next or appropriate) | A. a surgical intervention @SWR-2264
q: () years old male i think, known case of hypertension, his hypertension is resistant to multi-drug therapy, high serum renin On US: Rt renal artery 85%stenosis , Lt 77% ( not sure pf the numbers but the same concept) What is the most appropriate management? | A. renal artery stenting @SWR-2265
idea:
- [[0]] Gastric varices with a normal liver and normal portal vein = splenic vein thrombosis, and the cure is splenectomy, not band ligation.
- [[1]] Never thrombolyse a stroke before a non-contrast CT has excluded haemorrhage.
- [[2]] On ARB + CCB, the missing letter is D: add a thiazide like diuretic.
- [[3]] Calculate the TC:HDL ratio first; reading total cholesterol 6.1 straight onto a ratio axis only works here because HDL is 1.
- [[4]] After an MI, a statin is never optional: 'controlled by diet' is the trap.
- [[5]] Fever and leukocytosis tempt 'septic', but low CO with high SVR and low filling pressures is volume loss.
- [[6]] Once the stem says diabetic, age and ethnicity no longer change the first choice: start an ACE inhibitor or ARB.
- [[7]] Plain US and plain CT see the kidney, not the renal artery; renovascular disease needs a vascular study (Doppler or angiography).
- [[7]] ⚠ If the exam wording is "most accurate/gold standard", catheter angiography (or CT angiography, if option B was in fact CTA) is favoured over Doppler, which is the initial noninvasive test.
- [[8]] Young age plus renal artery stenosis points to fibromuscular dysplasia, where angioplasty (not more drugs) is the treatment.
- [[9]] High renin points to RAS; low renin with hypokalemia points to primary aldosteronism (spironolactone).

q: 58-YO man. Diabetic hypertensive and dyslipidemia, 2 day cough fever vomiting Admitted to the ICU and right heart catheter showed, Low CVP PCWP and cardiac output, High SVR and low venous saturation? Temp. 38 BP. 128/89 HR. 120 RR. 18 Hg 132 Wbc 19 Platelets.. | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3438
q: It was a long case patient with a 10-year history of diabetes mellitus on insulin, recently diagnosed with hypertension. Her serum creatinine is 80 umol/L. What is the most appropriate management? | A. Start an ACE inhibitor @SWR-3502
idea:
- [[0]] Pregnancy-safe (methyldopa) and resistant-HTN add-on (spironolactone) are distractors; a fixable bilateral stenosis is treated with angioplasty.
- [[1]] Pregnancy-safe (methyldopa) and resistant-HTN add-on (spironolactone) are distractors; a fixable bilateral stenosis is treated with angioplasty.
- [[2]] Do not read total cholesterol (6.1) straight off the chart: divide by HDL to get the ratio, then locate the cell.
- [[3]] Any established vascular event (stroke, TIA, MI) means the strictest LDL target offered; do not be tempted by primary prevention numbers.
- [[4]] Controlled hypertension does not make a traveler high-risk; an immunocompromised or IBD patient would flip the answer toward prophylaxis.
- [[5]] Check age and confirmation: unconfirmed reading = confirm first; age ≥60 with office SBP below 150 under JNC 8 = lifestyle; confirmed ≥140/90 under current guidelines = drug plus lifestyle.
- [[6]] Make the same patient younger than 60 (as in the 50-year-old sister recall) or add LVH, proteinuria or raised creatinine and the answer flips to starting a drug.
- [[6]] ⚠ Current ACC/AHA (≥140/90 = stage 2) and ESC 2024 guidance recommend drug therapy plus lifestyle for confirmed BP ≥140/90 in a 69-year-old; lifestyle alone reflects older stage 1 / JNC 8 (age ≥60, threshold 150/90) teaching.
- [[7]] A near identical stem in a 69-year-old with BP 147/86 is keyed to lifestyle: age 60 or older moves the drug threshold to 150/90.
- [[8]] Fever does not make it septic shock: if SVR is high and cardiac output low, the profile is hypovolemic (or cardiogenic if PCWP is high).
- [[9]] Diabetic + hypertension = ACE inhibitor first, even with normal creatinine; normal creatinine is what makes it safe, not unnecessary.

q: A female patient presents with hypertension that is not responding adequately to antihypertensive medications. She also has renal bruit on examination. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic test? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-4151
q: Patient smokes 20 cigarettes and vital signs show BP 140/92 Which of the following is the most considerable risk factor for MI ? | B. Smoking @SWR-4397
q: A 60-year-old patient, known case of diabetes mellitus and hypertension, presents to the clinic worried about peripheral arterial disease. He has no complaints, but his older brother had a left leg amputation. What is the initial investigation? | B. ABI @SWR-4578
q: A female patient presents with hypertension ( 150/90 ) that is not responding adequately to antihypertensive medications. She also has renal bruit on examination. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic test? | A. CT scan of the kidneys @SWR-4603
q: A male patient has blood pressure measurements of 158/95 mmHg on two separate occasions in the hospital. Twenty-four-hour ambulatory blood pressure monitoring shows an average daytime blood pressure of 145/90 mmHg and nighttime blood pressure of 135/85 mmHg. (Labs provided and all normal). What is the most likely diagnosis? | B. Primary hypertension @SWR-4696
idea:
- [[0]] Do not apply the permissive ischemic target (220/120) to a bleed; in ICH you actively lower the BP.
- [[1]] Always convert kPa to mmHg (x 7.5): 7.2 kPa is about 54 mmHg, just below the oxygen cut-off.
- [[2]] If ambulatory BP monitoring is among the options for a single elevated reading, it beats repeat office measurement; if readings are already repeatedly high, lifestyle (or drugs) beats repeating.
- [[2]] ⚠ Confirming before treating is correct, but a reference question with a single elevated reading is keyed to ambulatory BP monitoring, and for a reading of 150/99 guidelines advise confirmation within weeks rather than waiting 3 months; the compiler also leaves this card pending revision.
- [[3]] Aspirin is a secondary prevention drug; for an asymptomatic hypertensive with high LDL the answer is the statin.
- [[4]] Do not trust the recall's 'normal' label on lab values: TG 2.1 mmol/L is high and adds risk.
- [[5]] Look for the word 'Doppler' or 'angiography': imaging the kidney itself (plain US, plain CT, IVP) does not diagnose a stenosed artery.
- [[6]] A borderline BP (140) loses to a heavy smoking history; a clearly raised BP (150 or more) flips the answer to hypertension.
- [[7]] Diabetics can have falsely high ABI from calcified arteries; a value above 1.4 is not 'normal'.
- [[8]] A bruit points to the vessel, so choose the test that images the vessel (CT angiography), not one that only images the kidney or stones.
- [[9]] If the ABPM daytime average had been below 135/85, the same office readings would make this white-coat hypertension.

idea:
- [[0]] Budd-Chiari also causes portal hypertension, but a heavy drinker with varices is cirrhosis until proven otherwise.
- [[1]] Any proteinuria in a hypertensive patient makes an ACE inhibitor (or ARB) the answer, whatever the age.

q: A patient undergoes viral hepatitis serological screening with the following results: Anti-HBs: Positive Anti-HBc IgM: Negative Anti-HBc IgG: Negative Anti-HAV IgM: Positive Anti-HAV IgG: Positive What is the most likely diagnosis? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-1275
q: {{rep:×2}} 50s patient came with hematemesis then developed altered level of consciousness and he is drowsy but arousable There are a lots of labs and vitals (I believe he was unstable) (from my understanding to this case that it is a liver cirrhosis that started as hematoemsis then progressed to heptic encephalopathy) Most appropriate next step? | D. IV octreotide and blood type and cross match @SWR-1540
idea:
- [[0]] Normal amylase rules out nothing in a chronic drinker with recurrent epigastric pain; it is expected in chronic pancreatitis.
- [[1]] Alcohol + long history of epigastric pain + normal amylase = chronic pancreatitis, not 'no pancreatitis'.
- [[2]] Failed drug plus endoscopy = TIPS; tamponade only buys time.
- [[3]] Screening = ultrasound; a nodule found = triphasic CT or MRI.
- [[4]] Obesity and diabetes are the NAFLD distractor; a high-titer ANA with transaminases in the hundreds makes it autoimmune hepatitis.
- [[5]] Antibody positive but RNA negative with no risk exposure flips the answer to no further testing.
- [[6]] The trap is 'cured HCV = reassure'; the cirrhosis, not the virus, decides surveillance.
- [[7]] Normalised viral load never cancels HCC surveillance once cirrhosis is established.
- [[8]] Anti-HBc is the only marker that proves past HBV contact; anti-HBs alone means vaccine.
- [[9]] Haematemesis in cirrhosis = varices until proven otherwise: octreotide, not PPI, is the specific drug; ceftriaxone is what cuts mortality.

q: An asymptomatic patient who go infected with hepatitis B 10 years ago due to blood transfusion presents with routine serology: HBs Ag: positive Hbs IgG positive Hbs IgM negative Hbe anting is positive Hbe IgG is negative Hbc IgG is positive Hbc antigen is negative What does the patient have? | B. Immunization form previous infection @SWR-2507
q: There was a case about, I don't remember if it was a man or a woman, coming for a check-up or follow-up. There were labs for hepatitis serology. Everything was negative. The only one that was positive was anti-hepatitis B surface antigen. What is the condition, diagnosis, or something like that? | A. Previous immunization against hepatitis B @SWR-2510
idea:
- [[0]] Anti-HBe positive does not mean recovery; as long as HBsAg is positive the patient is still infected.
- [[1]] Drowsy plus massive haematemesis = intubate first; in an alert patient, the 'most reduces mortality' answer is antibiotics.
- [[2]] Autoantibodies suggest autoimmune hepatitis, but 'confirm' or 'gold standard' always means biopsy.
- [[3]] Do not let the word "abscess" pull you toward drainage: for amoebic disease the drug is the treatment, and drainage is for complications or failure.
- [[4]] Size alone does not make an amoebic abscess a drainage case; a right lobe abscess on "initial management" is still metronidazole.
- [[5]] An alert, hypotensive bleeder needs fluids first; the same bleeder who is drowsy with blood in the mouth needs intubation first.
- [[6]] Look for 'drowsy', 'low saturation' or 'blood in the mouth': any one of these turns the answer to intubation.
- [[7]] Pruritus and obesity are red herrings for PBC and NAFLD; the biopsy word "plasma cells" plus ANA settles it as autoimmune hepatitis → steroids.
- [[8]] Anti-HBc separates infection from vaccination; HBsAg separates current infection from recovery.
- [[8]] ⚠ As recalled (HBsAg positive, HBeAg positive, anti-HBc IgG positive, IgM negative), the pattern is chronic HBeAg positive hepatitis B, not immunity; the key B is correct only if HBsAg was actually negative, which the recall likely got wrong.
- [[9]] The vaccine contains only surface antigen, so it can never produce anti-HBc.

q: 67-year-old man a known case of HCV cirrhosis admitted to Emergency Room with massive hematemesis... variceal bleeding is suspected. On examination on, he is drowsy with tense ascites (see lab results). pressure 110/70 mmHg heart rate 86/min en saturation on 95 % Result Normal Values 130-170g/L (Male) 120-160 g/L (Female) 1.3 0.8-1.2 INR 1.3 Which of following management options has a great benefit in reducing this patient mortality ? | B. q: a question about a person has jandice, symptoms suggesting of hepatitis ast, alt in 600 i guess, but importantly has low ceroplasmin want definitive investigation ? | A. liver biopsy @SWR-3245
q: A young patient diagnosed with bipolar disorder using valproic acid for mood stabilization Presents with jaundice?? I don't know Labs ALT and AST markedly elevated but not in thousands Negative ANA Hepatic serology is negative for hepatitis A,C and B Low ceruplasmin What is the most appropriate next step in diagnosis | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3276
idea:
- [[0]] IgM = now, IgG or anti-HBs = before; read each virus separately before combining.
- [[1]] Anti-HBs alone = vaccine; anti-HBs plus anti-HBc = recovered infection. Anti-HBc is the marker that proves the virus was ever there.
- [[2]] Do not let the hepatitis B serology distract you: the history of excess paracetamol plus enzymes in the thousands points to NAC.
- [[3]] IgM anti-HBc is the switch: positive = acute, negative with HBsAg positive = chronic.
- [[4]] Positive anti-HBs is protective, not infection; the trap is reading every "positive" result as disease.
- [[5]] Background hepatitis B is a distractor: a clear paracetamol ingestion with AST and ALT in the thousands = NAC, not antivirals.
- [[6]] Normal amylase does not exclude pancreatic disease: in recurrent alcoholic attacks it points to chronic, burnt out pancreatitis.
- [[7]] "Reduces mortality" in a cirrhotic GI bleed = antibiotics (ceftriaxone); "stops the bleeding" = octreotide then band ligation.
- [[8]] "Best initial" in suspected Wilson = ceruloplasmin; "definitive" = liver biopsy with copper content.
- [[9]] Low ceruloplasmin in a sick liver does not equal Wilson; a drug history of valproate plus enzymes in the hundreds points to drug-induced injury first.

q: patient with chronic liver disease presents with the following laboratory results: HCV antibody: positive HCV RNA: 1.5 million IU/mL HBsAg: negative HBcAb: positive Hepatitis A IgG: positive Which type of chronic hepatitis does this patient have? | C. C @SWR-4103
q: A patient complains of abdominal pain, jaundice I think??? I can't remember but there are symptoms and they did an abdominal US and showed a lesion in the liver, what is the most appropriate next step?? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-4291
q: Someone came back from India I think and he has fever, jaundice, couple of symptoms. What is the route of infection? Something like that | A. Drink and eat contaminated food or water @SWR-4294
q: A nurse sustains a needlestick injury from a patient known Hepatitis B. Lab results: Positive HBsAg Positive anti-HBc IgM Positive total anti-HBc Positive HBeAg Elevated liver enzymes Which of the following findings would confirm infection? | C. Positive anti-HBc IgM + positive HBsAg @SWR-4311
idea:
- [[0]] Acute bleed = octreotide; long term prevention = beta blocker; if the lead-in asks what reduces MORTALITY, the answer flips to ceftriaxone.
- [[1]] Anti-HBc is the marker that separates vaccine immunity (negative) from immunity after infection (positive).
- [[2]] Anti-HBe positive does not mean recovery; as long as HBsAg is positive and anti-HBs negative, she is infected.
- [[3]] Antibodies suggest the diagnosis; the word "confirm" points to biopsy.
- [[4]] DAAs can reactivate hidden hepatitis B, so HBV status is checked before HCV treatment.
- [[5]] The vaccine gives anti-HBs only; if anti-HBc is also positive, the answer flips to past (resolved) infection.
- [[6]] HCV antibody stays positive for life; only HCV RNA tells you the infection is active.
- [[7]] Do not biopsy a likely HCC first: contrast imaging comes before tissue.
- [[8]] Travel plus jaundice equals hepatitis A or E equals contaminated food or water; blood and sex point to B and C.
- [[9]] HBsAg says "infected"; only IgM anti-HBc says "acute"; HBeAg says "infectious".

q: A 30-year-old man presents to the ED with right upper quadrant pain and jaundice for 3 days. Acute hepatitis A @SWR-5021
idea:
- [[0]] When the stem gives chronic vomiting and "no labs", the answer that connects everything is the electrolyte disturbance.
- [[1]] Anti-HBc is the marker of real exposure: anti-HBs with anti-HBc = recovered infection; anti-HBs alone = vaccine.
- [[2]] Positive antibody is a trap; a negative RNA with normal enzymes and no exposure means nothing more to do.
- [[3]] Do not let the HBV serology distract you; the paracetamol history plus AST over 2000 is the decisive clue.
- [[4]] Anti-HBc is the switch: positive means real virus exposure, negative with anti-HBs means vaccine.
- [[5]] To split vaccine from natural immunity to HBV you need anti-HBc: positive = past infection, negative = vaccinated.
- [[6]] Ultrasound tells you ascites is there; only the tap tells you why.
- [[7]] If "transfusion" is offered for a hypotensive variceal bleed choose it; if not, IV fluids before octreotide or endoscopy.
- [[7]] ⚠ A similar reviewed question with postural hypotension keys adequate IV fluid resuscitation as the first step, with transfusion reserved for Hb below 7; on this card, if Transfusion is not offered, IV fluid (the printed answer) is the expected choice.
- [[8]] A positive HCV antibody alone does not mean active disease; the RNA (viral load) does. Here the RNA is high, so it is active HCV.
- [[9]] Anti-HBc is only made after real exposure to the virus, so a positive anti-HBs with a negative anti-HBc means vaccination.

q: A 27-year-old woman with Crohn’s disease presents with bloody diarrhea and left lower-quadrant abdominal pain. She is currently taking prednisone. Sigmoidoscopy reveals persistent patchy colitis. Which of the following should be added to her treatment? | A. Anti-TNF therapy @SWR-4666

idea:
- [[0]] A vertical family history (mother AND brother) is the clue for a dominant polyposis syndrome; without it, the same symptoms in a young adult would point to UC.
- [[1]] If the stem adds a dilated colon (toxic megacolon) after failed medical therapy, the answer switches to colectomy, not more steroids.
- [[2]] If the stem specifies "no vomiting", the examiner wants the diarrhea fluid: Ringer lactate.
- [[3]] "Already on high dose steroids" is the phrase that moves the answer from more steroids to anti-TNF.
- [[4]] Same stem with ulcerative colitis and mild disease flips the answer to 5-ASA; Crohn's failing steroids = biologic.
- [[5]] "Already on steroids" plus ongoing activity is the cue to escalate to anti-TNF, not to repeat steroids.
- [[6]] PSC is the exception that moves UC surveillance to the time of diagnosis.
- [[7]] Active colitis on steroids with infection excluded = refractory disease, so escalate to biologics rather than add a weaker drug.
- [[8]] "Crohn's on steroids, still active" = anti-TNF; 5-ASA is the UC drug.
- [[9]] Bloody diarrhea is still diarrhea (RL), but a lower GI bleed without diarrhea takes NS.

q: {{rep:×4}} Female with melena and epigastric pain. She didn't use NSAIDs. She did upper endoscopy Urease test of antral biopsies: negative Most appropriate next diagnostic test? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-2142
q: Female with melena and epigastric pain. She didn't use NSAIDs. She did upper endoscopy Urease test of antral biopsies: negative Most appropriate next diagnostic test? | B. Urea breath test ⚠ @SWR-2146
idea:
- [[0]] Antibiotics in an ulcer question are never given alone; the PPI is the backbone.
- [[1]] NSAID history + ulcer = PPI; save antibiotics for a positive H. pylori test.
- [[2]] Night symptoms flip the answer to bed-head elevation or avoiding late meals, provided the interval is 2 to 3 hours.
- [[3]] NSAIDs are an ulcer cause, not a cancer cause; salt is the dietary answer.
- [[4]] "Metaplasia" on biopsy = Barrett; "after vomiting" = Mallory-Weiss.
- [[5]] Add weight loss, anaemia or older age to the same stem and the answer flips to endoscopy.
- [[6]] If Heller myotomy is among the options for a fit patient, many keys prefer it; when it is absent, pick pneumatic dilatation, never botox.
- [[7]] Decide by context: already scoped means histology, not scoped means breath test, PPI cannot be stopped means serology.
- [[8]] Look for active-bleed clues (tachycardia, ED setting, ongoing melena, blood in the stomach at endoscopy): present means biopsy for histology, absent or unclear means urea breath test.
- [[9]] Read for signs of ongoing bleeding: present, choose biopsy for histology; absent or unclear, choose urea breath test.

Fecal occult blood is positive. Colonoscopy is negative. What is the appropriate next step? | A. Esophagogastroduodenoscopy @SWR-3643

q: 35man has epigastric discomfort and burning that increases 3-4hrs after eating, but partially relieved after eating sure 99.1%. It also increases late night and when lying down. Hb is low What is the diagnosis? (Sure was mixed symptoms of GERD and PUD | B. PUD @SWR-4688
q: A patient presents with typical symptoms of GERD that are relieved by calcium carbonate. A fecal occult blood test is positive. Colonoscopy is performed but shows no abnormality. What is the most appropriate next step? | B. Esophagogastroduodenoscopy @SWR-4697
idea:
- [[0]] Decide by where the clues point: a mucosal red flag (anemia, age 60 or more, weight loss, dysphagia) favours endoscopy; pain suggesting a cause outside the lumen favours CT; free air or obstruction favours X ray; biliary pain favours ultrasound.
- [[1]] The negatives (no mass, no family history, no vomiting) are distractors; age alone is enough to mandate endoscopy.
- [[2]] If the stem adds an alarm symptom (dysphagia, weight loss, GI bleeding), the answer flips to upper endoscopy.
- [[3]] The same symptoms in a 25-year-old with no red flags would be answered by H. pylori testing or lifestyle and PPI, not endoscopy.
- [[4]] If the classic clarithromycin triple therapy is not in the options, pick the bismuth quadruple regimen.
- [[5]] Never pick a regimen with one antibiotic or a non-antimicrobial add-on (sucralfate, H2 blocker): standard eradication regimens pair the PPI with at least two antimicrobials (bismuth counts as one).
- [[6]] Occult blood with a clean colonoscopy is not the end of the workup: look upstairs with EGD before any empiric PPI.
- [[7]] Positive FOBT with a normal colonoscopy means scope the other end: EGD.
- [[8]] Pain RELIEVED by eating plus nocturnal pain = duodenal ulcer; positional heartburn without anemia = GERD.
- [[9]] Without the positive FOBT, typical GERD relieved by antacids in a patient with no red flags would simply be treated with a PPI.

q: Patient complains of intermittent diarrhea with no blood or mucus. She is diagnosed with IDA, other than that her history and physical exam is unremarkable. Which food should she avoid? | D. Wheat @SWR-3122
idea:
- [[0]] Neutropenic patient + RLQ pain: think typhlitis and never scope; CT is the answer.
- [[1]] Neutropenic patient + RLQ pain: think typhlitis and never scope; CT is the answer.
- [[2]] The joint pattern decides here: small joints point to tTG (celiac), large joints point to colonoscopy (IBD).
- [[3]] Bloody diarrhoea with rectal involvement points to UC and the steroid plus mesalamine answer; skin lesions and RLQ signs point to Crohn's and anti-TNF.
- [[3]] ⚠ Stem gives no diagnosis or severity; another recall version keys steroids and mesalamine, and for a first flare steroids to induce remission are reasonable. Anti-TNF is defensible only if this is moderate to severe Crohn's disease.
- [[4]] Erythema nodosum does not need its own therapy; it settles when the IBD flare is controlled, and markers of severe disease push the answer from steroids toward a biologic.
- [[5]] Watch the word 'bloody': non-bloody chronic diarrhea leans to Crohn's (anti-TNF), bloody diarrhea leans to UC (mesalamine plus steroids).
- [[6]] Mesalamine belongs to ulcerative colitis; in Crohn's with extra-intestinal or severe features, think anti-TNF.
- [[7]] New urinary retention in an older man right after a new drug: think anticholinergic (TCA, antihistamine, antispasmodic) before blaming disease progression.
- [[8]] Read whether steroids FAILED: failed = cyclosporine; responded = azathioprine.
- [[9]] Unexplained iron deficiency anemia with non-bloody diarrhea = think celiac: the grain to avoid is wheat, barley or rye.

HG: (I think 10) AST: 121 Alt: 79 Upper endoscopy: Erosion 1st 2nd part of the duodenum Ileocolonoscopy: normal duodenal Biopsy: shows villous atrophy What is the Best Management? | C. Gluten free @SWR-3751
q: Pt presented to ED with Hematemesis and melena. Pt is pale and DRE confirmed melena What is the most Diagnostic thing to do? | A. Upper Endoscopy @SWR-3926
q: A 31-year-old presents with 1 day of melena and dizziness. DRE shows maroon/red stool. BP is about 90/60 mmHg, HR 120/min, and Hb 8.7 g/dL. What is the most appropriate next step? | A. Normal saline @SWR-4463
q: Patient present with hematemesis and melena , he is known to be alcoholic and has mild abdominal pain Vitals: BP 90/55 Which of the following is the most important next step ? | B. IV fluid and blood transfusions @SWR-4808
q: female in 30s colonoscopy there's some normal areas (skip lesions) | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-5058
idea:
- [[0]] Black stool alone with normal vitals in a patient on bismuth or iron may not be a bleed at all; hematemesis plus NSAID makes it a peptic ulcer until proven otherwise.
- [[1]] Isolated high urea with normal creatinine in a bleeding patient points UPSTAIRS: even heavy red stools can come from an upper source.
- [[2]] Size beats histology: a completely removed tubular adenoma larger than 1 cm moves the interval to 3 years.
- [[3]] The relative's age at diagnosis (under 60) is what upgrades him to colonoscopy every 5 years.
- [[4]] Dermatitis herpetiformis rash responds to dapsone quickly, but the definitive treatment of the disease is still a gluten free diet.
- [[5]] Hematemesis or coffee ground vomit always means start with EGD; angiography only comes after a failed or impossible endoscopy.
- [[6]] Early Hb underestimates acute blood loss; if the stem says the patient stays hypotensive after fluids or describes massive bleeding, the answer becomes blood transfusion.
- [[7]] Hypotension or orthostatic change = resuscitate first; endoscopy, octreotide and labs are never ahead of IV access and volume.
- [[8]] "Normal areas between diseased segments" = skip lesions = Crohn's; a continuous lesion from the rectum = UC.
# Nephrology & Electrolytes
== Potassium & acid-base  (1/3)
q: Patient known case of lymphoma took R-CHOP treatment, laboratory results showed hyperkalaemia and hypocalcaemia. how to prevent? | C. Rasburicase @SWR-0156
q: A patient with heart failure had previously been symptomatic with pulmonary edema/congestion and was started on guideline-directed therapy. He is currently taking: Sacubitril/valsartan (maybe this medication or similar) Furosemide Spironolactone possibly another heart-failure medication not sure He now presents for follow-up and is completely asymptomatic. Laboratory investigations are normal except for: Potassium ~ 5.8 mmol/L What is the most appropriate next step in management? | A. Decrease the dose of spironolactone @SWR-0354
q: A patient admitted with heart failure The Doctors put him on sacubatril/valsartan, Spirono, Metoprolol and discharge, on follow up he's fully normal, asymptomatic, but the lab showed HYPERKALEMIA (it was mild elevation MAYBE 6 [handwritten correction: 5.8]) What to do | C. reduced dose of spironolactone @SWR-0355
q: Case about female who have sedintry lifestyle, high cholestrol, bmi:29 She is afraid because of cardiovascular risk... How to counsel her ? | B. Mediterranean diet @SWR-0396
q: Senna syrup is used to treat chronic constipation, which of the following is considered a serious adverse effect / side effect ? | C. Hypokalemia @SWR-0484
q: Pt with TLS presents with muscle weakness, fatigue, nausea, palpitations K:6.2, Ca: 1.9 (I think) Which of the following is attributed to his symptoms? | A. Hyperkalemia @SWR-1313
q: A man dies following an arrhythmia associated with hyperkalemia. He has diabetes, hypertension, and cancer, but has not yet started chemotherapy. He may be taking a potassium-sparing diuretic, and elevated creatinine and urea were also recalled. What is the most likely cause of the hyperkalemia? | A. Tumor lysis syndrome; @SWR-1497
q: Patient (not remember age) come comatose by ambulance Na K8 | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-1653
q: Female with severe community acquired pneumonia. pH 7.1 (can't remember the rest but were abnormal HCO3, PO2) | B. Elective intubation @SWR-2107
q: A female patient, known to have chronic hepatitis, presents with lower-limb edema, a flapping tremor, and spider nevi. Her potassium level was slightly elevated, and her sodium level was also mildly elevated, but neither abnormality was significant. What is the most appropriate management? | B. Furosemide @SWR-2410
idea:
- [[0]] If hydration is an option it beats any drug; diuretics (loop or thiazide) are never the preventive answer.
- [[1]] The threshold is 6: below 6 reduce spironolactone, above 6 stop it.
- [[2]] Mild hyperkalemia on HF drugs: touch the spironolactone first, not the ARNI.
- [[3]] If DASH appears in the options it is usually the best answer; without DASH, the Mediterranean diet is the evidence-based choice for CV risk, and "salt below 2 g" is a trap because sodium and salt are not interchangeable.
- [[4]] Read the adjective: most common side effect = cramps; serious side effect = hypokalaemia.
- [[5]] Same TLS stem with muscle aches or cramps flips the answer to hypocalcemia.
- [[6]] 'Before chemotherapy' does not exclude TLS: spontaneous TLS occurs in bulky, fast growing tumours.
- [[6]] ⚠ This version is incompletely recalled: without lymphoma or uric acid in the stem, and with a potassium-sparing diuretic, diabetes and renal impairment, a drug-induced cause (or type 4 RTA) would compete; TLS fits best when the fuller version (non-Hodgkin lymphoma, high uric acid) applies.
- [[7]] Calcium does not lower the potassium level; it buys time, so shifting and removal must follow.
- [[8]] BiPAP is the COPD answer; in severe pneumonia with pH 7.1 (or a drowsy patient) the answer flips to intubation.
- [[9]] Same cirrhotic patient, different potassium: K high gives furosemide, K normal or low gives spironolactone.
- [[9]] ⚠ The stem calls the K and Na rises insignificant; with mild, non-significant hyperkalemia, cirrhosis guidance still starts spironolactone (often with furosemide), so the key holds only if hyperkalemia is real; the compiler also notes the potassium value is missing.
== Potassium & acid-base  (2/3)
q: {{rep:×2}} COPD patient came with worsening SOB and cough Vitally stable pH 7.20 Co2 8.6 O2 6 | C. acute COPD exacerbation with acute type 2 respiratory failure @SWR-2843
q: A 50-year-old man with diabetes, hypertension and chronic kidney disease presents to the Nephrology Clinic for follow-up. Blood pressure 160/98 mmHg, Heart rate 80 /min Potassium 6.5 mmol/L (Normal: 3.5-5.1) Creatinine 440 umol/L (Normal: 44-115) Which of the following is the best immediate step in management? | A. Intravenous calcium gluconate @SWR-2918
q: Patient using NGT CO2 within normal range PH : 7.52 HCO3 : 40 What is the abnormality here ? | A. Metabolic Alkalosis @SWR-3291
q: Female with severe community acquired pneumonia. pH 7.1 (can't remember the rest but were abnormal HCO3, PO2) | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3421
q: Patient found comatose. K+: 8 mmol/L. ECG: peaked T waves, widened QRS complexes, and absent P waves. What should be done? | B. IV calcium gluconate @SWR-3474
q: Patient known case of COPD and CKD. Came with confusion. In PE there is astrexis. Lab (Not exactly) : PCO2 : 9.2 PO2 : 6.8 PH : 7.16 ? I don't remember exactly but it's acidosis High creatinine Which of following is mostly causing patient condition ? | B. respiration failure type 2 @SWR-3537
q: Which of the following electrolyte and acid base disturbance is expected to happen with diarrhea ? | D. Metabolic acidosis with hypokalemia @SWR-3912
q: 75 Female HTN and HF. Daughter mentioned she is compliant with medication regularly. Came with confusion for 3 days. Lisinopril, thiazides, amlodipine. Na = 120 K 5.8 Ca 2.7 Glucose 8 What is the cause of her symptom? | A. Hyponatremia @SWR-4187
q: A patient presents with hyperkalemia. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial treatment? | B. IV insulin with dextrose @SWR-4471
q: A patient presents with vomiting and other symptoms. Laboratory investigations reveal a serum potassium level of 2.2 mmol/L. ECG shows flattened T waves, idk U waves, idk QT interval. What is the most appropriate management(not sure) ? | B. Intravenous potassium replacement @SWR-4702
idea:
- [[0]] High CO2 in COPD is chronic only if the pH is near normal; once pH falls to 7.2 it is acute.
- [[1]] Calcium treats the ECG, not the potassium level: with ECG changes give calcium first; with a normal ECG start with insulin-dextrose.
- [[1]] ⚠ This recall omits the ECG; if the ECG truly showed no changes, insulin with dextrose would be favoured as the first treatment, as in similar questions with a normal ECG. The original version includes peaked T waves, which supports calcium gluconate.
- [[2]] NG tube or vomiting = loss of acid = alkalosis; diarrhea = loss of base = acidosis.
- [[3]] Ask "is the patient awake?": awake and cooperative favours BIPAP, drowsy means intubate.
- [[4]] Calcium does not lower potassium; it protects the heart while you lower it.
- [[5]] Asterixis is not only hepatic or uremic: in a CO2 retainer it signals hypercapnic encephalopathy, so check the PCO2.
- [[6]] Do not reason 'acidosis therefore hyperkalemia': diarrhea is the classic exception with acidosis plus hypokalemia.
- [[7]] A mildly high potassium or calcium in the same panel is a distractor; Na 120 in an elderly patient on a thiazide is the classic cause of confusion.
- [[8]] Peaked T waves in the stem flip the answer to calcium gluconate; without ECG changes, insulin with dextrose is the initial treatment.
- [[9]] Do not confuse with hyperkalemia: peaked T waves call for IV calcium gluconate first; flattened T waves with low K call for IV potassium.
== Potassium & acid-base  (3/3)
q: Heart-failure patient previously symptomatic with congestion, now asymptomatic on sacubitril/valsartan, furosemide, spironolactone, etc. Potassium ~5.8 mmol/L. What next? | A. Decrease spironolactone dose @SWR-4710
q: 50 year old male presented to the ER with lethargy, ECG normal, labs all normal except potassium 6.6 and HCO3 15. What's the initial management? | B. Insulin dextrose infusion @SWR-4797
q: Q11. A patient presents with hypokalemia and is started on potassium replacement therapy. Despite treatment, the serum potassium level remains below the normal range. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? | C. Check serum magnesium level @SWR-4985
q: A man newly diagnosed with diabetes, has hypertension was started on candesartan, added to his meds which are nifedipine, and hydrochlorothiazide , with normal baseline renal function 2 weeks ago. He now has headache and fatigue. Electrolytes are normal and potassium is 5.0 mmol/L, but urea and creatinine are elevated. What is the most appropriate next step? | A. Stop candesartan @SWR-5020
idea:
- [[0]] Stopping furosemide sounds safe but raises potassium further; the MRA is the drug to cut.
- [[1]] ECG changes present = calcium gluconate first; normal ECG = start with insulin and dextrose.
- [[2]] Calcium gluconate and dialysis are hyperkalemia tools; when K or Ca will not rise, the answer is magnesium.
- [[3]] ACE inhibitors and ARBs are renoprotective long term, but a sharp creatinine rise soon after starting one means stop it, then look for renal artery stenosis.

Labs: hyperglycemia, sodium of 132 ( N > 134 ), calcium: 1.9 mmol, her Bp is 150 ( I mean she is not hypotensive) Brain MRI shows cortical atrophy.( it was very long case ) What to do to correct her neurological symptoms? | B. q: Patient with a cold, treated for pneumonia, now agitated; takes albuterol and lorazepam; 02 89% with mild hyponatremia. Most appropriate action? | C. Review medications, oxygen, and infection @SWR-3002
q: Q5. Patient came with signs and symptoms of pleural effusion (I don't remember the exact case), CXR show pleural effusion (unilateral? bilateral?). Fluid analysis (with numbers) shows ↑ LDH (sure), other not sure if protein (↑), glucose (↓), pH (↓) | D. TB @SWR-4981
q: A male patient with known small-cell lung cancer presents with lethargy and fatigue. He has no altered level of consciousness, seizures, or other significant neurological symptoms. He has no polydipsia and no lower-limb edema. Laboratory investigations are largely unremarkable except for serum sodium of approximately 124-126 mmol/L. Urea/BUN and creatinine are slightly above the reference range. No serum osmolality, urine osmolality, or urine sodium is provided. What is the most appropriate management? | B. Fluid restriction @SWR-5073
idea:
- [[0]] Hypotension trumps the sodium value: resuscitate with isotonic saline first, even when sodium is high.
- [[1]] Borderline labs placed in a seizure stem are a distractor; the answer is the primary seizure disorder.
- [[2]] Mild abnormal labs in a septic elderly patient are distractors; the infection is the cause to treat.
- [[3]] In delirium, a sedative is never the first answer unless the stem says alcohol withdrawal.
- [[4]] One positive Light's criterion is enough for an exudate, even if the protein ratio is borderline.
- [[5]] The same SCLC patient flips to hypertonic saline the moment he becomes confused or seizes; "lethargy and fatigue" alone means fluid restriction.

q: A 29 year old man came to the emergency room after falling in a 50-kilometer race. He had dark urine that was dipstick positive for large blood and on microscopic analysis there was no RBC. Which of the following is the most appropriate management step? | A. Hydration @SWR-1229
q: A 60-Year-Old Male Presents With Persistent Back Pain For 3 Months, Unresponsive To NSAIDs. Laboratory Findings: Electrolytes: Normal, Creatinine: Elevated. Bone Marrow Biopsy: 20% Plasma Cells. What Is The Most Likely Cause Of This Patient's Deterioration In Kidney Function? | C. Accumulation Of Monoclonal Light Chains @SWR-3074
idea:
- [[0]] Adrenal crisis comes from stopping steroids; continuing a high dose kills through its side effects (here hypertensive bleed).
- [[1]] "Large blood, no RBCs" is not haematuria; it is myoglobinuria, so treat with fluids, not a urology workup.
- [[2]] Back pain + renal failure + marrow plasma cells = myeloma kidney from light chain casts, not ATN.

Prepare For Transplant @SWR-0596
q: 20-year-old man with sickle cell anemia presents with right sided chest pain, shortness of breath, leg/back pain. Crackles in right middle/lower zones. BP 125/75, HR 92, RR 25, Temp 37.9, SpO2 91%. Most effective drug to reduce incidence? | B. Hydroxyurea @SWR-1426
q: A 50-year-old male with a 3-year history of chronic kidney disease presents with numbness in his hands and legs. On physical examination: bilateral lower limb loss/diminished sensation up to the knees and absent reflexes. Lab results: K= 6.3 Creatinine and urea: very high Hb = 9 RBC =4 What is the management? | C. Initiate dialysis @SWR-1670
q: Patient on Kidney dialysis Came to the ER complaining , chest pain, dyspnea orthopnea with laying down chest is clear on examination. Echo shows pericardial effusion best management | C. initiate dialysis @SWR-1743
q: 41 years old female, BMI 31, BP 140/ something, HgA1c is 8, BUN and creatinine are mildly elevated. What is the most important thing to prevent renal failure? | D. glycemic control @SWR-2000
q: Pt with eng stage kidney disease. Long case but at the end Echo shows moderate pericardial effusion and was unstable. | A. Pericardiocentesis @SWR-2103
q: 4-A patient with CKD has DVT and received unfractionated heparin develops thrombocytopenia (16) and left leg pain and absent ARTERIAl pulses. What to do next? | A. Stop unfractionated heparin @SWR-2176
q: جاني سؤال كيس ماذكر التفاصيل بس الزبده كان البوتاسيوم قليل ومع العلاج او شي كذا نسيت ، ستل مو جاي يتعدل وكان البوتاسيوم اقل من الرينج الطبيعي | C. Check serum magnesium @SWR-2468
idea:
- [[0]] Match the answer to the lab given: low Na points to electrolyte imbalance, not organ failure.
- [[1]] Cirrhosis does not stop HCV treatment; it makes it more urgent, and surveillance continues after cure.
- [[2]] Add pulmonary edema, refractory hyperkalemia or uremic signs and the answer flips to initiating dialysis.
- [[3]] 'Reduce incidence/recurrence' = hydroxyurea; 'treat this episode' = oxygen, fluids, analgesia, antibiotics, transfusion.
- [[4]] A CKD patient with new neuropathy or confusion has uremia until proven otherwise: dialyze.
- [[5]] Add hypotension to the same stem and the answer flips to pericardiocentesis.
- [[6]] Diabetic with rising creatinine: treat the cause (glucose) and protect the kidney (ACE inhibitor or ARB); lifestyle options are supportive.
- [[7]] Same ESRD effusion but stable: the answer flips to hemodialysis.
- [[8]] Thrombocytopenia on heparin with a NEW clot is HIT: stop heparin; never switch to LMWH and never reverse with protamine.
- [[9]] If potassium or calcium refuses to rise with replacement, the missing step is magnesium.
== CKD, anemia of CKD & dialysis  (2/4)
q: A patient with CKD has DVT and received unfractionated heparin develops thrombocytopenia (16) and left leg pain and absent ARTERIAL pulses. What to do next? | A. Stop unfractionated heparin @SWR-2515
q: Patient presents with 10 years history of HTN, was diagnosed 5 months ago with DM and Dyslipidemia, and takes NSAIDs with for recurrent headache He has CKD Whats the cause of his worsening CKD? | A. Hypertension @SWR-2595
q: Patient with 5-6 bouts of diarrhea for 5 days. She visited her family medicine doctor and he prescribed anti-spasmodic with no relief. She was dehydrated. Her creatinine in the first visit was normal. Current labs: BUN: 11 Creatine: 160 Urine Na: 60 Urine osmolality: 220 What is the cause of her current presentation? | B. ATN @SWR-2642
q: A 60 years old man with poorly controlled diabetes mellitus presented to the ER with headache and elevated blood pressure. He had normal RFTs and was discharged on candesartan, hydrochlorothiazide, and nifedipine. Two weeks later, he presented again with reduced urine output and fatigue. His laboratory results showed: K: 5 Urea: 24 Creatinine: 160 What is the most appropriate management? | A. Stop candesartan @SWR-2672
q: An elderly woman presents with dyspnea and lower limp edema and Bilateral Basal crackles with other symptoms suggestive of heart failure for 6 months. She is a known diabetic and has chronic kidney disease (CKD), currently on medications for hypertension and diabetes. What is the best secondary prevention strategy for this patient? | A. Early treatment of heart failure symptoms @SWR-2727
q: Patient with stage 3 CKD or ESRD Found to have atrial fibrillation What to give? | A. Apixaban @SWR-2811
q: Known CKD recently given postoperative fentanyl now altered mental status and visual hallucinations what is the diagnosis? | A. Opioid-induced encephalopathy @SWR-2860
q: {{rep:×3}} 65year old patient with chronic kidney diseases developed acute confusion. He was receiving fentanyl as post operative care. He developed tremor and myoclonus. + hallucinations What is the cause of encephalopathy? | B. Opioid-induced neurotoxicity @SWR-2861
q: {{rep:×2}} 65 year old patient with chronic kidney diseases developed acute confusion. He was receiving fentanyl as post operative care. He developed tremor and myoclonus. What is the cause of encephalopathy? | B. Opioid-induced neurotoxicity @SWR-2862
q: A patient with known chronic kidney disease is being treated with gentamicin and furosemide. He is vitally stable. Echocardiography shows a moderate pericardial effusion. His creatinine is elevated, while his BUN is 11.8 mmol/L. What is the most appropriate next step? | D. Stop aminoglycosides @SWR-2873
idea:
- [[0]] Low platelets in HIT tempt you to give platelets or protamine; both are wrong, because the danger is clotting.
- [[1]] The durations (10 years versus 5 months) are the decisive clues; if the stem tells you NSAIDs were used for years or the drop was sudden, medication induced becomes the answer.
- [[1]] ⚠ Debatable: the lead in asks about "worsening" CKD in a patient taking NSAIDs for recurrent headache, which many would key as medication induced (NSAIDs are a classic, reversible cause of CKD worsening). The key rests on HTN as the long standing cause; the compiler also questions NSAID duration.
- [[2]] Dehydration in the stem tempts "prerenal"; the urine sodium and osmolality decide, and here they say the tubules are damaged.
- [[3]] A small, stable rise in creatinine after an ARB is acceptable; a jump with falling urine output means stop it.
- [[4]] The word "prevent" in an option does not make it primary; ask whether the disease already exists.
- [[5]] If the stem adds a mechanical valve or mitral stenosis, the answer becomes warfarin regardless of kidney function.
- [[6]] New delirium with hallucinations or myoclonus soon after starting an opioid in CKD = opioid neurotoxicity, not uremia.
- [[7]] Myoclonus is the clue that separates opioid neurotoxicity from simple postoperative delirium or sepsis.
- [[8]] Do not blame the kidney when a new drug was just started: CKD is the risk factor, the opioid is the cause.
- [[9]] Stable vitals take pericardiocentesis off the table; look for the reversible cause in the stem, here the gentamicin.

q: A 72 year old knows case of HTN ,DM CKD sage4 asymptomatic Labs : Createnin 380 DEX scan T score 3 at femoral neck Asked about Initial therapy | C. Subcutaneous denosumab @SWR-4371
q: A 55-year-old man with a history of hypertension, type 2 diabetes mellitus, and chronic kidney disease stage 3 is on metformin, lisinopril, and a statin. He presented with signs of fluid overload raised JVP, bilateral lower limb edema, bibasilar crackles, and hepatomegaly, complaining of shortness of breath. On examination, a grade 2 systolic ejection murmur at left sternal border was heard. Where should this patient be referred? | C. Cardiology to assess the possibility of heart failure and valvular pathology @SWR-4788
q: A 20-something-year-old male college student, known Hepatitis B case, presented to ER with decreased consciousness and confusion. He ingested a LARGE AMOUNT (20 tablets of 500mg) of paracetamol for knee pain. Upon examination, NO stigmata of chronic hepatitis B infection. LFT: AST 3000, ALT 2500, Amylase 300. What's the appropriate management? | A. Administer N-acetylcysteine @SWR-4847
q: An old man known case of DM, HTN, CKD, presents to the emergency department with a ? day history of progressively worsening SOB and orthopnea, he is on metformin, lisinopril....., on examination he has bilateral basal crackles, lower limb edema, Cardiovascular examination reveals a new 2/6 systolic murmur. Which of the following subspecialties should be consulted? | C. Cardiology for possible diagnosis with HF and valvular disease @SWR-5063
idea:
- [[0]] Same ESRD effusion stem: stable BP = dialysis, hypotension with muffled heart sounds = pericardiocentesis.
- [[1]] Normal creatinine before, AKI two weeks after an ARB: blame the ARB, not the CCB.
- [[2]] AF + claudication in exam recalls = diltiazem; AF + heart failure = digoxin.
- [[2]] ⚠ Diltiazem is acceptable here, but the stated reason is outdated: current PAD guidance says cardioselective beta blockers such as metoprolol are not contraindicated in intermittent claudication, so metoprolol would also be a valid rate control choice in real practice.
- [[3]] Fentanyl is the opioid usually preferred in CKD, but it can still cause neurotoxicity; the clue is the temporal link to starting the drug.
- [[4]] Low GFR in an osteoporosis question removes bisphosphonates and points to denosumab.
- [[5]] In ESRD, dialysis treats uremic pericarditis, but hypotension means tamponade, and tamponade means needle first.
- [[6]] CKD 4 + osteoporosis = denosumab, not alendronate; watch calcium.
- [[7]] Treat the organ that explains all the findings: overload plus murmur is cardiac, not renal.
- [[8]] Chronic HBV in the stem is a distractor: an acute hepatitis with a clear overdose history is the drug until proven otherwise.
- [[9]] Pick the specialty that matches the NEW finding (murmur plus heart failure), not the background chronic diseases.
== CKD, anemia of CKD & dialysis  (4/4)
q: A 65-year-old man with end-stage renal disease (ESRD) on regular hemodialysis three times per week presents with worsening shortness of breath. Vital signs: BP: 145/85 mmHg HR: 105/min RR: 24/min SpO2: 92% on room air Temperature: 37.0C Laboratory investigations show a markedly elevated serum urea (BUN). On examination, heart sounds are mildly muffled. There is no hypotension, raised JVP, or pulsus paradoxus. Echocardiography demonstrates a moderate pericardial effusion without right atrial or right ventricular collapse. What is the most appropriate next step in management? | D. IV furosemide @SWR-5070
idea:
- [[0]] Muffled heart sounds alone are not tamponade; without hypotension, raised JVP, pulsus paradoxus or chamber collapse, do not reach for the needle.
- [[0]] ⚠ Most sources favour A (urgent/intensified hemodialysis): the markedly raised urea points to inadequate dialysis, and uremic or dialysis associated effusion without tamponade is treated with intensive dialysis. In an ESRD patient the response to IV furosemide is usually poor because of little or no residual renal function, and any fluid overload is removed by ultrafiltration during dialysis.
== UTI, pyelonephritis & stones
q: Alcoholic patient with recurrent epigastric pain and multiple admissions. Mildly elevated amylase. Most likely diagnosis? | B. Chronic pancreatitis @SWR-0767
q: A young male presented to the emergency department (I don't remember his exact complaint — jaundice / fatigue — but I remember the question mentioned he usually feels fatigued easily even before his current complaint, plus SOB). But I'm 100% sure from the following: he had right hypochondrial pain and splenomegaly. Labs showed a hemolytic pattern(sorry, I don't remember the labs except reticulocytes were 2). Ultrasound showed: calculous cholecystitis. (The question came with a picture of a blood film — 100% sure it was not in Joe's recall pics. I'll try to find and add the image. I was hesitant to add this question, but inshallah it will help.) What is the most likely diagnosis? | D. Hereditary spherocytosis @SWR-1297
q: healthy 40-year-old woman has had 4 urinary tract infections in one year and wants to prevent recurrence. What is the best evidence-based choice? | D. Continuous low-dose antibiotic prophylaxis @SWR-1943
q: 3- A young male(17maybe) can't remember the exact symptoms he was complaining of but had right hypochondrial pain and splenomegaly. Labs showed a hemolytic pattern Ultrasound showed: calculous cholecystitis (The question came with a new picture of a blood film but I couldn't find it) What is the most likely diagnosis? | D. Hereditary spherocytosis @SWR-2814
q: 29 years women complaining of frequent urinary tract infection What is the best strategic promotion to prevent such condition? No time or frequency of recurrence | C. Cranberry juice @SWR-3763
q: A young male presents to the Emergency Department with fatigue/shortness of breath and right hypochondrial pain. He has splenomegaly and laboratory evidence of hemolysis. Ultrasound shows calculous cholecystitis. A peripheral blood film image is provided. What is the most likely diagnosis? | D. Hereditary spherocytosis @SWR-4418
idea:
- [[0]] A mild amylase rise falls short of the ≥3x enzyme criterion for acute pancreatitis; with recurrent pain in a drinker, think chronic pancreatitis.
- [[1]] Spherocytes + Coombs positive = AIHA; spherocytes + Coombs negative + family history/gallstones = hereditary spherocytosis.
- [[2]] If the stem says infections follow intercourse, switch to post-coital single-dose prophylaxis.
- [[3]] Spherocytes plus a positive Coombs test is AIHA, not HS; check the Coombs before naming the diagnosis.
- [[4]] If an antibiotic prophylaxis option appears for documented recurrent UTI, it usually beats cranberry; here it was absent.
- [[5]] Spherocytes alone do not settle the diagnosis; a chronic course with splenomegaly and gallstones means HS, a new Coombs positive picture means AIHA.

== Diabetes & DKA  (1/3)

Family history: relative with type 1 DM Labs: Elevated PTH Normal calcium HbA1c: 8% Normal urine osmolality and serum osmolality, Elevated C-peptide: 0.09 ng/mL Which is the most likely diagnosis? | B. q: A 12-year-old pa7ent presents to the clinic with diarrhoea, vomi7ng, miosis, bronchoconstric7on, swea7ng, and excessive saliva7on. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Organophosphate poisoning @SWR-1134
q: {{rep:×2}} A patient presents with acute hemiparesis, facial droop, and aphasia. A non-contrast head CT shows no evidence of infarction or hemorrhage. What is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Ischemic stroke @SWR-1269
q: patient with type one diabetes developed a tonic clonic seizure, they provided his labs and asked about the cause of seizure Na 132 K 3.9 Normal chloride (can't remember the value) Random blood glucose 4.9 | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-1708

q: A patient presented with a generalized seizure lasting approximately 2-4 minutes. EEG showed generalized seizure activity. Laboratory investigations revealed hypocalcemia and hyponatremia, while the other electrolytes were normal. What is the most likely diagnosis/cause of the seizure? | B. Generalized tonic-clonic epilepsy ⚠ @SWR-1710
q: {{rep:×2}} A female patient (not sure if she was symptomatic or not) undergoes testing for diabetes Fasting blood glucose 6.5 A1C 6 OGTT was high C peptide within the normal range What is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Prediabetes @SWR-2213
q: A 19 Years Old Female with Polyurea & Polydipsia, Acanthosis Nigricans And Obesity With Family Hx Of Type 2 DM Comes For Diabetes Screen Fasting blood glucose 6.5 A1C 6 OGTT was 208 C peptide within the normal range What is the most likely diagnosis? | C. Type 2 Diabetes @SWR-2215
idea:
- [[0]] A "known type 2" diabetic with very low C-peptide is a type 1 until proven otherwise; ignore the PTH distractor when calcium is normal.
- [[1]] Only established ASCVD, heart failure or CKD shifts the first choice toward GLP-1 agonist or SGLT2 inhibitor; none is present here.
- [[2]] In a child with DKA on treatment the feared complication is cerebral edema; in an adult with low K+ it is worsening hypokalemia.
- [[3]] Pinpoint pupils plus WET (salivation, sweating, bronchorrhea) = organophosphate; pinpoint pupils with DRY respiratory depression = opioid.
- [[4]] A normal early CT does not exclude ischaemic stroke; it excludes hemorrhage.
- [[5]] A mildly abnormal value (Na 132, glucose 4.9) does not explain a seizure; if an option like 'none of the above' or 'epilepsy/structural cause' appears, weigh it.
- [[6]] Read the reference range printed next to the value; a result just outside it is not a seizure cause.
- [[7]] If the exam gives the actual values and they are mild, choose the epilepsy option; if they are severe, choose the electrolyte.
- [[8]] Same numbers, different answer: asymptomatic gives prediabetes, add polyuria/polydipsia and it becomes type 2 diabetes.
- [[9]] Acanthosis nigricans in a diabetic teenager points to insulin resistance, so type 2, not type 1.

q: A patient with a history of diabetes mellitus, hypertension, and stage 3 chronic kidney disease is taking lisinopril, metformin, and atorvastatin. He presents with bilateral lower-limb edema, shortness of breath, and dry cough. On examination, a new grade 2/6 cardiac murmur is detected. His clinical condition is deteriorating. A chest X-ray image is provided. Which specialist should be consulted most urgently? | B. Cardiologist to investigate possible valvular heart disease @SWR-4484
q: A 60-year-old male presented to the OPD with a 20-year history of well-controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus treated with oral hypoglycemic agents. He has a history of COPD and a myocardial infarction 2 years ago. He has a 30-year history of smoking one pack per day. He received the influenza vaccine 6 months ago. What is the most appropriate vaccine to give this patient? | A. Shingles vaccine @SWR-4537
q: A 30-year-old patient presents to the Emergency Department with lethargy, polyuria, and polydipsia. His laboratory investigations show a low C-peptide level of 0.09. He has a family history of type 2 diabetes mellitus, and his nephew has type 1 diabetes mellitus. What is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus @SWR-4794
idea:
- [[0]] Same HbA1c 5.8% but BP 149/90 on repeated readings flips the answer to adding an ACE inhibitor.
- [[1]] When a patient with liver disease is still drinking, "stop alcohol" beats every other lifestyle or drug advice.
- [[2]] No cardiovascular or renal comorbidity mentioned = metformin first; GLP-1 agonist becomes the answer when ASCVD, CKD or heart failure appears in the stem.
- [[3]] When the stem pointedly says a key lab was not done, the answer is usually "cannot be determined" or "order that test".
- [[4]] If the version you meet adds visual hallucinations, fluctuating cognition, parkinsonism or REM sleep behaviour disorder, Lewy body dementia is the answer; insomnia with autonomic overactivity alone is the fatal familial insomnia fork.
- [[4]] ⚠ As recalled, insomnia with autonomic hyperactivity (tachycardia, sweating), dysphagia, abnormal eye movements and seizures fits fatal familial insomnia, a prion disease (option C), better than DLB, whose core features (visual hallucinations, fluctuations, parkinsonism) are absent. Hypoglycemia is not a recognised DLB feature.
- [[5]] "No infarction on CT" in an acute deficit is expected early; do not let it push you off ischemic stroke.
- [[5]] ⚠ Hypoglycemia is a strong alternative as the classic mimic with normal imaging; the key relies on early CT being insensitive for ischemia. If the imaging was MRI (DWI), a normal result would argue against infarction and favor a mimic.
- [[6]] A very low HbA1c on a sulfonylurea should prompt a question about hypoglycemia; if he has hypoglycemic episodes, de-escalate rather than reassure.
- [[7]] Do not let the CKD distract you: a NEW murmur in a decompensating patient is the decisive clue and points to cardiology.
- [[8]] If pneumococcal vaccine is offered, it competes strongly in a smoker with COPD and diabetes; recheck the options before choosing zoster.
- [[9]] Age of onset does not exclude type 1 DM; a low C-peptide in an adult still means type 1 (or LADA).

idea:
- [[0]] Creatinine is the raw number; eGFR is what defines and stages CKD.
- [[1]] A diabetic with erratic postprandial glucose and bloating has gastroparesis until proven otherwise; insulin timing should be matched to the delayed absorption.

q: A 29-year-old man is evaluated for a diagnosis of hypercalcemia. 5 days ago, he was treated of a kidney stone in the Emergency Department. Physical examination is unremarkable (see lab results), Blood pressure 140/79 mmHg, Heart rate 85 /min, Respiratory rate 13 /min, BMI 30 kq/m2 Test Result Normal Values Sodium 139 134-146 mmol/L Potassium 4.0 3.5-5.1 mmol/L Chloride 99 97-108 mmol/L Bicarbonate 23 21-28 mmol/L Calcium 2.95 2.15-2.62 mol/L Phosphate, inorganic 0.76 0.82-1.51 mmol/L Parathyroid hormone (intact PTH levels) 200 1.1-5.3 pmol/L Blood urea nitrogen 9.5 2.8 to 8.9 mmol/L Creatinine 125 44-115 mol/L Which of the following is most likely cause for his hypercalcemia? | B. q: Middle aged man, recent URTI, progressively worsened. Admitted and intubated. Vitals: normal BP, high HR (90s), 22 RR, no fever. ABG NORMAL EXCEPT FOR HYPOXEMIA Diagnosis: | B. Pulmonary edema @SWR-3322
idea:
- [[0]] Never treat a low T4 until you know the TSH; if the pituitary is the problem, give steroids before thyroxine.
- [[1]] New psychiatric or cardiac symptoms in a patient on amiodarone or lithium: check thyroid function first.
- [[2]] In suspected pituitary failure, giving thyroxine before steroids can trigger an adrenal crisis.
- [[3]] 'Symptomatic' in the lead-in means beta-blocker; 'definitive' or 'reduce hormone' means methimazole.
- [[4]] When both are started together, the exam favours the drug that acts fastest on the presenting symptom (here the irregular pulse).
- [[5]] Fatigue plus dyslipidaemia should prompt a TSH before any lipid drug.
- [[6]] If the option list had 'start levothyroxine', it is a trap: in suspected pituitary failure, steroids go first and the diagnosis must be confirmed.
- [[7]] High calcium with high PTH is primary ONLY if phosphate is low; high phosphate shifts it to tertiary.
- [[8]] The low phosphate is the clue that separates primary from secondary and tertiary hyperparathyroidism.
- [[9]] Neuromuscular weakness and overdose fail the pump, so CO2 rises; a normal CO2 with hypoxemia points to the lung itself.

q: A 35-year-old woman is evaluated in the clinic for thyroid enlargement. She is asymptomatic and has no significant past medical history. Her examination reveals a firm, mobile, non-tender nodule at the lower pole of the right lobe of the thyroid gland. The left lobe is normal to palpation. Which of the following investigations is the most appropriate initial test? | D. Thyroid stimulating hormone (TSH) @SWR-4629
q: Woman came with 3 months of fatigue and lethargy (?), she is medically and surgically free Labs: TSH i don't remeber the exact number but high T4 = 12.4 (normal) Anti peroxidase antibodies = 70 (high) What is the most appropriate next step | B. Start thyroxine @SWR-4721
q: A female patient presents with features of Cushing's syndrome for 4 months(she had purpule striae and weight gain. Laboratory tests show bp140/80 K low High cortisol 8am: 840 4pm: 500 ACTH: 15 TSH: 4 What is the most likely diagnosis ? | A. Pituitary adenoma @SWR-4726
idea:
- [[0]] Before blaming the SSRI or the mind, look at the amiodarone: TSH and free T4 first.
- [[1]] Rapid growth in an elderly patient without Hashimoto suggests anaplastic; rapid growth ON a Hashimoto background suggests lymphoma.
- [[2]] Low TSH sends you to the thyroid scan; normal TSH sends you to ultrasound.
- [[3]] Equal pulses rule out the coarctation distractor; pallor versus flushing decides between the other two.
- [[4]] Snoring + smoker with hyperinflation + high CO2 with acidosis = overlap syndrome: BiPAP, not CPAP, surgery or physiotherapy.
- [[5]] Normal CO2, or high CO2 with a normal pH (compensated), still points to CPAP after a sleep study; high CO2 with acidosis (pH below 7.35) points to BiPAP.
- [[6]] Asymptomatic, antibody negative, single mildly raised TSH: observe and repeat; symptoms or anti-TPO positive: levothyroxine.
- [[6]] ⚠ Many guidelines (and the reference's own first step for subclinical hypothyroidism) advise confirming a raised TSH on repeat testing before starting treatment; with a single TSH of 7.7 and normal T4, option B is defensible. The key A relies on symptoms plus anti-TPO positivity (the stem's 'anti TPO 70 normal' wording is unclear).
- [[7]] FNA is the most accurate test for a nodule, but the most appropriate INITIAL test is always TSH; if the stem already gives a normal TSH, the answer becomes ultrasound or FNA.
- [[8]] Positive anti TPO plus symptoms tips a mildly raised TSH from watching to treating.
- [[9]] A "normal" ACTH in the face of high cortisol is inappropriately normal and still means ACTH dependent, usually pituitary.

idea:
- [[0]] When the only clues are "infection, deteriorated, intubated" with no neuro signs, pick the lung process, not a neuromuscular disease.
- [[1]] Low TSH means scan before FNA (hot nodules do not need biopsy); normal TSH means ultrasound then FNA.
- [[2]] Low cortisol plus low thyroid: steroid before thyroxine, every time.
- [[3]] If pCO2 is normal, think lung parenchyma (edema/ARDS); if pCO2 is high, think pump failure (drugs, nerves, muscles, COPD).

She lost 5 kg, but attributes this to eating healthy food. She has noticed a recent scar on the back of her hand, which has started to turn very dark as well as generalized skin hyperpigmentation. What is the most appropriate investigation to confirm the diagnosis? | A. Synacthen test ( ACTH stimulation test) @SWR-0850
q: Female with low BP, darkening of skin, low sodium, weight loss. ACTH was high as was morning cortisol. What is dx? | A. primary adrenal insufficiency @SWR-1123
q: 17 years old male, with 6 months of recurrent episodes of muscle cramps and fatigue, presented with muscle weakness since the morning. His father was diagnosed with HTN when he was 30 years old. genetic testing requested SCNN1B and SCNN1G 100% sure of the labs whats the most likely diagnosis? BP 162/102, HR 70, Na: 142, K: 2.7, bicarb: 34, renin serum low (mentioned like this with no numbers), aldosterone serum low | B. liddle syndrome @SWR-2079
q: 30 male with intermittent claudication , headache, blurred vision due to sever HTN All labs normal CXR normal Most appropriate investigation? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-2434
q: A male patient presents with dizziness, particularly when standing up. He has a 2-month history of weight loss and abdominal pain. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Primary adrenal insufficiency @SWR-2556
q: 30 male with intermittent claudication, headache, blurred vision due to sever HTN All labs normal CXR normal Most appropriate investigation? | A. Echo Doppler @SWR-2788
idea:
- [[0]] C-peptide separates the patient's own insulin from injected insulin, and type 1 from type 2.
- [[1]] Cushing adenoma = steroid cover; pheochromocytoma = alpha-blocker; do not swap them.
- [[2]] Low ACTH Cushing means the other adrenal is asleep; it needs steroid cover until it wakes up.
- [[3]] Suppression tests are for excess, stimulation tests are for deficiency.
- [[4]] Darkening of new scars is a classic Addison clue: think ACTH excess, then think synacthen.
- [[5]] Hyperpigmentation equals high ACTH equals primary; no pigmentation with low cortisol points to the pituitary.
- [[6]] Spironolactone does not work in Liddle syndrome because the problem is the ENaC channel, not aldosterone.
- [[7]] Claudication in a young hypertensive should make you check femoral pulses: that single clue moves you from renal or adrenal tests to imaging of the aorta.
- [[8]] Hyperpigmentation or hyperkalemia points to primary; their absence with a pituitary history points to secondary.
- [[9]] Claudication is the clue that separates coarctation from the other secondary causes of hypertension in a young patient.

q: 40s? man with resistant hypertension on 2 medications: amlodipine and losartan. Labs show hypokalemia and high bicarbonate (was 34, above exam range by 2 like that). Which test will lead to the diagnosis? | C. Urine metanephrines @SWR-3337
