q: young patients with blood pressure of 150/90,on ACI and amlodpine family hx of hypertension and stroke at young age, on physical examination power of the left side 3/5. Labs potassium was slightly decreased I guess 3. Something Bicarbonate was increased also 35 or 36 I forgot the stem but it was asking about investigation | A. The aldosterone-to-renin ratio (ARR) @SWR-3551
q: A 28-year-old man is diagnosed with hypertension and is currently taking losartan and amlodipine. Despite treatment, his blood pressure remains uncontrolled. He has a family history of stroke. On examination: 4/5 power proximally Laboratory investigations reveal: Serum potassium was low, around 3.1-3.4 Serum bicarbonate was high i think 34 mmol/L Which of the following is the most appropriate investigation? | A. Aldosterone: renin ratio @SWR-3621
idea:
- [[0]] If palpitations tempt you toward pheochromocytoma, check for claudication or a CXR sign: those point to coarctation.
- [[1]] Low aldosterone rules out primary hyperaldosteronism; with normal cortisol and a dominant family history it is Liddle.
- [[2]] If the stem gives ataxia and eye signs instead of asterixis, the answer becomes thiamine deficiency (Wernicke), not high ammonia.
- [[3]] If the stem asks for the first test in galactorrhea, it is pregnancy test or prolactin; once prolactin is already high, the answer becomes sella (pituitary MRI).
- [[4]] Hyperkalemia is the tie breaker: it means aldosterone is gone too, which happens only when the adrenal gland itself fails (primary).
- [[5]] The trap this recall stressed is serum versus urine metanephrines: both test for pheochromocytoma, and the source keyed the 24 hour urine test.
- [[5]] ⚠ Hypertension on 2 drugs with hypokalemia and metabolic alkalosis (HCO3 34) is the classic presentation of primary hyperaldosteronism; standard guidance screens with the aldosterone:renin ratio (A). Pheochromocytoma (metanephrines) is suggested by paroxysmal symptoms, which are not described. Unless the real stem includes adrenergic paroxysms, A is favoured over the keyed C.
- [[6]] In hyponatraemia there is no truly "normal" urine osmolality; anything not maximally dilute means ADH is on.
- [[7]] If the stem adds sweating and palpitations, the answer flips to plasma metanephrines; if it adds radio-femoral delay or an arm to leg BP gap, it is coarctation.
- [[7]] ⚠ The recalled stem has no coarctation clue (arm to leg BP gradient, radio-femoral delay), and echo is usually the initial rather than the confirmatory test (CT or MR angiography confirms). If the full stem had episodic adrenergic symptoms, plasma metanephrines would be favoured. Keep the key, but check the full recall.
- [[8]] ACE inhibitors raise renin and can falsely lower the ARR, but ARR is still the first screening test for low K plus alkalosis with hypertension.
- [[9]] Losartan raises renin and can lower the ratio (false negative), but the ARR is still the first test; correct potassium before testing.
== Adrenal & pituitary  (3/3)
q: 17 yo male came with elevated blood pressure (~149/89), he takes 2antihypertensive drugs and a family history of death at young age Which is most appropriate to reach the diagnosis? | A. aldosterone:renin ratio @SWR-4066
q: 21 year old male patient young on 2 antiHTN. BBP is still high. Family history of death and stroke at young age. BMI 21. What test would you order to confirm the diagnosis? | A. Renin Aldosterone Ratio @SWR-4067
q: young patients with blood pressure of 150/90,on ACI and amlodpine family hx of hypertension and stroke at young age, on physical examination power of the left side 3/5. Labs potassium was slightly decreased I guess 3. Something Bicarbonate was increased also 35 or 36 I forgot the stem but it was asking about investigation | A. The aldosterone-to-renin ratio (ARR), @SWR-4403
q: Patient with seizure for 3 days. His labs shows hyponatremia (130), normal K+ (4.4), and low serum osmolarity (240). His urine analysis shows normal urine osmolarity (890). What is the diagnosis? | D. SIADH @SWR-4630
q: 65-year-old patient presented with a single episode of seizure. Labs: Na+ approx 112 mmol/L K+: Normal Urine osmolality: Forgotten Possible diagnoses: | D. SIADH @SWR-4631
q: 51 years old female newly diagnosed hypertension, despite treatment, multiple readings comes as 160/80 (i am sure about systolic but not diastolic pressure). She also experienced symptoms of headachs, palpitations, and ***** (I can't remember the third symptom). What is the next step in management? | C. serum chatecholamines @SWR-4894
q: 43y old women attended opd for assessment with weight gain and easly brusing complain for 3-4 months. She also noticed to be having irregular menstruation, truncal obesity and puprble striae Bp: 143/88 ACTH : 15 Cortisol 8am: 870 Cortisol 4pm: 560 Asking about the diagnosis: | B. Pituitary adenoma @SWR-5104
idea:
- [[0]] Young + resistant HTN + family history of early stroke = think aldosterone (ARR); paroxysmal headache, sweating, palpitations = think metanephrines.
- [[1]] "Confirm" in the lead-in does not move you to metanephrines: with no paroxysms and a normal BMI, the aldosterone axis is still the target.
- [[2]] Hypokalemia with high bicarbonate in a hypertensive points to aldosterone, not catecholamines.
- [[3]] Low Na with HIGH K points to Addison; low Na with NORMAL K and concentrated urine points to SIADH.
- [[4]] Normal potassium is the clue that removes Addison's; mineralocorticoid excess states (Conn, Cushing) drop potassium, not sodium.
- [[5]] Never image the adrenals or give a beta blocker before alpha blockade in suspected pheochromocytoma; biochemistry first.
- [[6]] A normal-looking ACTH in a cushingoid patient is NOT suppressed, so it still points to the pituitary, not the adrenal.

q: A 28-year-old woman presents with a 6-month history of chronic diarrhea, abdominal bloating, and intermittent abdominal discomfort. She reports passing bulky, foul-smelling stools several times per day. During this period, she has experienced progressive fatigue and unintentional weight loss despite having a normal appetite. She denies fever, hematochezia, recent travel, or antibiotic use. On examination, she appears pale and thin, with mild abdominal distension but no focal tenderness. BP: 108/68 mmHg HR: 92/min RR: 17/min Temp: 36.8C. Laboratory investigations show: Hb: 9.2 g/dL, MCV: 70 fL, WBC: 6.8 x 10^9/L, Platelets: 430 x 10^9/L, Ferritin: 6 ng/mL, Serum iron: Low, Albumin: 30 g/L, Calcium: 8.0 mg/dL, Vitamin D: Low, CRP: Normal, ESR: Mildly elevated, ALT: Mildly elevated. q: Which electrolyte pattern is characteristic of tumor lysis syndrome? | A. Hyperkalemia + hypocalcemia @SWR-4490
q: A 65-year-old man with a significant smoking history presents for routine health screening. What is the key screening test? | C. Abdominal aortic aneurysm screening by ultrasound @SWR-4499
idea:
- [[0]] If the option read LONG term steroids, it would beat alcoholism; the word 'short' removes it.
- [[1]] Low calcium in a cancer patient after chemo points to tumor lysis; high calcium points to bone metastasis or myeloma.
- [[2]] Unexplained iron deficiency that fails oral iron in a young adult = think celiac before anything else.
- [[3]] If the stem gives ESRD with LOW calcium and LOW vitamin D and asks "most appropriate management", the answer flips to calcitriol.
- [[4]] Bone deformity = vitamin D; bleeding gums = vitamin C; night blindness = vitamin A.
- [[5]] "Manage his phosphate" in early CKD = diet first; calcitriol is the answer when the stem is ESRD with low calcium and the question targets PTH.
- [[6]] In TLS, calcium is the only one that falls; if an option says hypercalcemia, it is not TLS.
- [[7]] Male + 65 to 75 + ever smoked = AAA ultrasound; a 65-year-old woman would point to DEXA instead.

q: 40 something y o male came complaining of easy bleeding, he has gum bleeding and skin rash, labs: Hg 12.5 , PLT: 5 , WBC: 7.6 (100% sure of the labs) no shistocytes in blood film What is the cause? | A. ITP @SWR-0196
q: An 18-year-old male presented with intermittent abdominal pain and chronic diarrhea. The diarrhea was non-bloody and without mucus. He appeared slim and pale and reported easy fatigability. Laboratory investigations showed: Hemoglobin: 100 g/L, MCV: High, Vitamin B12: Low Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? | B. Crohn disease @SWR-0211
q: Picture of a smear showing sickled cells — asking for the most appropriate test to confirm the diagnosis. | A. Electrophoresis @SWR-0252
q: Which type of hemoglobin is not found on electrophoresis? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-0407

idea:
- [[0]] Travel to the tropics plus folate AND B12 deficiency = tropical sprue; celiac spares B12 and has no travel clue.
- [[1]] Chronic inflammatory disease plus high ferritin = ACD; ferritin can be falsely normal in IDA with inflammation, never 900.
- [[2]] The word 'low Hb' tempts aplastic anemia; check the WBC and the size of the Hb drop before calling pancytopenia.
- [[3]] 'Low Hb' without numbers led to aplastic anemia in older keys; with a normal WBC and Hb 12.5 the answer is ITP.
- [[4]] Bloody diarrhea with urgency flips it to ulcerative colitis; iron deficiency with bloating flips it to celiac disease.
- [[5]] "Initial" test = smear; "confirm" or "diagnostic" = hemoglobin electrophoresis.
- [[6]] Read whether the stem describes a sickle patient (HbA missing) or a normal person (HbS missing) before choosing.
- [[7]] Trait still has HbA; only disease (SS) lacks it completely.
- [[8]] "Not present" in sickle disease = HbA; "raised" = HbS and HbF.
- [[9]] If bone marrow biopsy appears among the options for 'gold standard', pick it; for 'best initial/confirmatory blood test', pick ferritin.

q: {{rep:×2}} Most specific for IDA? | A. LOW ferritin @SWR-0431

idea:
- [[0]] Ferritin is specific when LOW; a normal or high ferritin is the non-specific (acute phase) result.
- [[1]] Haemolysis about 2 to 3 weeks after an atypical chest infection = think Mycoplasma and cold (IgM) agglutinins.
- [[2]] A low ferritin confirms iron deficiency, but a normal ferritin does not exclude it when inflammation is present.
- [[3]] Stable patient with IDA: oral iron first; IV iron only when oral fails or cannot be absorbed.
- [[4]] Any variant letter other than A, A2 and F (S, C, E) means a hemoglobinopathy, not a normal person.
- [[5]] Trait has HbA; disease has none; that single band separates them.
- [[6]] Positive anti-HBs is a protective antibody; do not read it as hepatitis B infection.
- [[7]] Read the lead-in: 'in fluid terms' points to volume replacement (blood with Hb 6), while 'reverse anticoagulation' points to PCC plus vitamin K.
- [[8]] A sickling test is negative in thalassemia, so it can falsely reassure; electrophoresis covers both.
- [[9]] Indirect bilirubin up with normal reticulocytes, Hb and smear after a stressor is Gilbert syndrome, not hemolysis.

q: A man is known to have sickle cell trait (HbAS). Two of his siblings have sickle cell disease (HbSS). q: 28 y/o male c/o fatigue, fever and confusion, also pallor but no organomegaly. Labs: high Cr and Urea hgb 7 plt 70 What appropriate test to do? | D. Blood film for schistocytes @SWR-2847
idea:
- [[0]] Per the compiler, small hand joints flip the answer to tTG (celiac), while large joints keep it at ileocolonoscopy (Crohn's).
- [[0]] ⚠ Features (bloating, iron deficiency, aphthous ulcers, arthralgia) overlap with celiac disease, and with a 'best INITIAL diagnostic test' lead-in and no IBD-specific clue (blood in stool, perianal disease, raised inflammatory markers) many keys would choose IgA tTG; the compiler's choice depends on a joint distribution not stated in this recall.
- [[1]] Spherocytes appear in both hereditary spherocytosis and warm AIHA; only the DAT separates them.
- [[2]] Pneumonia plus hemolysis points to IgM cold agglutinins (Mycoplasma); pneumonia without hemolysis is a different organism.
- [[3]] The sickle cell and acute chest syndrome background is a distractor; the lead-in is about what happens during the transfusion.
- [[4]] "After eating" + episodic hemolysis = favism (G6PD); "after cold" = cold agglutinin disease.
- [[5]] Low platelets with a positive Coombs is Evans; low platelets with a negative Coombs and schistocytes is TTP.
- [[6]] MCV 88 points to the reticulocyte count first; endoscopy becomes the answer once iron deficiency is shown (or as the source keys it here).
- [[6]] ⚠ For a normocytic anemia (MCV 88) the standard next step is the reticulocyte count (C) with iron studies; GI endoscopy (upper and lower) follows once iron deficiency or blood loss is established. Upper GI endoscopy alone as the immediate next step is debatable.
- [[7]] Bloody diarrhea with raised inflammatory markers pushes toward IBD and endoscopy; non-bloody diarrhea with iron deficiency pushes toward celiac serology.
- [[8]] Affected siblings are a distractor once the man's own genotype is proven HbAS; only the partner's result changes the child's risk.
- [[9]] Suspected TTP: do not transfuse platelets and do not wait for ADAMTS13 to start plasma exchange.

q: {{rep:×3}} A long case about a patient with intermittent bloody diarrhea, abdominal bloating, anemia, and mild diffuse abdominal tenderness, but there is no guarding, rigidity, or organomegaly. Colonoscopy showed patchy plaques of information affecting ascending, transverse, and descending colon. The patient was on IV methylprednisolone but the symptoms presist. Which of the following is the most appropriate treatment to be added: | C. calcinurin inhibitors @SWR-3080
q: A patient presents with jaundice and laboratory findings suggestive of hemolysis. What is the most appropriate initial investigation? | A. Direct Coombs test @SWR-3082
q: Female patient pale and slim and also palor and non blood diarrhea and distention HB 100 MCV 100 Low folic acid Low B12 What is the diagnosis? | A. Celiac @SWR-3186
idea:
- [[0]] Normal PT/aPTT with low platelets and schistocytes = TTP/HUS, not DIC; adult + confusion favours TTP.
- [[1]] Changing the oral preparation (liquid, more frequent doses) does not fix failed absorption; after a failed adequate oral course the answer is IV iron.
- [[2]] RBC count is the switch: truly high RBC with microcytosis = thalassaemia trait; high RDW with normal/low RBC = iron deficiency.
- [[2]] ⚠ A near-identical recall (asymptomatic 15-year-old, Hb 98, MCV 69, RBC 5.3 flagged high) is keyed to beta thalassaemia trait. If the RBC count is truly raised with a normal RDW, beta trait is favoured; the IDA key here depends on the compiler's note that the RBC was not truly high and RDW was about 20.
- [[3]] "Best initial test" = ferritin; "gold standard/confirms" = bone marrow iron stain; read the verb.
- [[3]] ⚠ In current practice a low serum ferritin is considered diagnostic of iron deficiency and bone marrow examination is rarely needed (historical gold standard only); if a version asks for the most appropriate or diagnostic test, serum ferritin is favoured.
- [[4]] Falling saturation despite antibiotics in SCD = transfuse; do not just change the antibiotic.
- [[5]] Reticulocyte count shows whether the marrow is responding; Coombs separates immune from non immune hemolysis; the blood film shows the red cell morphology. Match the option to what the stem still leaves unanswered.
- [[6]] Low ferritin in an older adult is a GI bleed source question, not a nutrition question: scope both ends.
- [[7]] Failing IV steroids = rescue with calcineurin inhibitor; responding to steroids = add thiopurine.
- [[8]] Spherocytes do not settle the diagnosis; the direct Coombs test separates warm AIHA from hereditary spherocytosis.
- [[9]] Low folate with bloating favours celiac; isolated or marked low B12 with RLQ signs flips the answer to Crohn's.

q: A man in his 30s presents with dark urine. Labs show: Low haptoglobin High LDH Evidence of hemolysis No Coombs result mentioned No spherocytes mentioned What is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria (PNH) @SWR-4367
idea:
- [[0]] "Most appropriate / diagnostic" = ferritin; "gold standard / definitive" = bone marrow iron; read the lead-in verb.
- [[1]] Very low MCV with barely low Hb and a high RBC count points to thalassemia trait; a high RDW and thrombocytosis point to iron deficiency (see the IDA version of this recall).
- [[2]] Decide by the Hb: low Hb needs iron tablets, not diet alone; always look for the source of iron loss.
- [[3]] Fever was not mentioned, but chills plus breathing difficulty during a transfusion is enough: stop it first.
- [[4]] Hemolysis + gallstones + leg ulcers = sickle cell; hemolysis + gallstones + splenomegaly + spherocytes = hereditary spherocytosis.
- [[5]] Add a blind loop or prior bowel surgery and high folate with low B12: the answer flips to SIBO.
- [[6]] "Best initial" = ferritin; "definitive" or "gold standard" = bone marrow.
- [[7]] "Most definitive" = bone marrow iron stain; "best initial" = ferritin; microcytosis not responding to iron with normal RDW = Hb electrophoresis.
- [[8]] Acute onset in a well adult without family history favours an acquired cause (AIHA or PNH) over spherocytosis; dark urine tips it to PNH.
- [[9]] Coombs-negative hemolysis with dark urine in a young adult = PNH; if Coombs is positive, switch to AIHA.
== Anemia & hemoglobinopathies  (6/7)
q: A 20-year-old university student presents with pharyngitis/sore throat, conjunctivitis and a respiratory illness. Laboratory findings show a hemolytic pattern with elevated LDH, reticulocytes and bilirubin. What additional finding is expected? | B. Elevated IgM cold agglutinin @SWR-4438

q: 20s yo male patient with sickle cell disease presented to the ED with acute vaso-occusive crisis. He was managed with IV fluids, morphine and enoxaparin. His labs showed anemia and thrombocytopenia (80). Creatinine and urea were normal. What's the cause of his thrombocytopenia? | D. Heparin Induced Thrombocytopenia (HIT) @SWR-4758

He has iron deficiency on labs and a high WBC only. What is the highest-yield diagnostic test? | A. Tissue transglutaminase antibody @SWR-4789
q: Patient presents with signs of iron deficiency anemia. She has experienced heavy menstruation for the past (6 months I think) and is pale. What confirms the diagnosis ? | B. Serum Ferritin @SWR-4842
q: Pateint present with symptoms of hemolytic anemia and DARK urine All hemolytics work up done and shows hymolytic pattern (except retics count not done yet ) what is the mosi appropriate INITIAL step | D. retics count @SWR-4914
q: Female with 2 weeks history of dark urine and tiredness. Labs showing hemolytic anemia, low heptoglobin, high LDH, high indirect What is the most important NEXT step? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-4915
q: A female patient came with symptoms of IDA, and increased frequent of menses. Which of the following investigations has the highest diagnostic value? | B. serum ferritin @SWR-4950
q: high diagnostic test to diagnose underlying disease (hx and labs showed microcytic anemia). | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-5002
idea:
- [[0]] Conjunctivitis tempts you to adenovirus, but the hemolysis clue is what decides: hemolysis means cold agglutinins and Mycoplasma.
- [[1]] FOBT screens the well; once IDA is proven, scope (OGD + colonoscopy) instead of testing stool.
- [[2]] A negative FOBT never ends the work up of iron deficiency anaemia.
- [[3]] Platelets fall after any heparin (even enoxaparin) with no schistocytes = HIT; stop heparin but keep anticoagulating with a non-heparin agent.
- [[4]] Iron deficiency that does not respond to oral iron, with no blood loss, is celiac disease until proven otherwise.
- [[5]] Bone marrow only when the stem says "gold standard" or "definitive"; otherwise ferritin.
- [[6]] 'Initial' with retics missing points to the reticulocyte count; once the panel is complete and hemolysis confirmed, the next step becomes the Coombs test.
- [[6]] ⚠ Debated: with hemolysis already shown (dark urine, hemolytic pattern), many keys favour the direct Coombs test as the next step; the reticulocyte count is keyed here only because the lead-in says INITIAL and it is the missing baseline test, and the author himself calls its added value debatable.
- [[7]] Read the verb: 'initial' with retics missing favours the reticulocyte count; 'next' after a confirmed hemolysis panel favours the Coombs test.
- [[8]] Ferritin is an acute phase reactant, so it can be falsely normal with inflammation; a LOW ferritin, however, is virtually diagnostic of iron deficiency.
- [[9]] "Gold standard" or "most accurate" = bone marrow; "best", "first" or "most useful" blood test = ferritin.
== Anemia & hemoglobinopathies  (7/7)
q: A 20-years-old male patient with sickle cell disease presented to the ED with acute vaso-occlusive crisis. He was managed with IV fluids, morphine and enoxaparin. His thrombocytopenia (80). Creatinine and urea were normal. What's the cause of his thrombocytopenia? | D. HIT (Heparin-induced thrombocytopenia) @SWR-5010
q: Scenario of a women with heavy menstrual bleeding for 6 months, labs showed low Hb, low MCV Most definitive in confirming the dx? | B. Bone marrow @SWR-5032
q: 20 y/o university student patient presents with pharyngitis, conjunctivitis, cervical lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly (not sure),......, Labs showed clear picture of hemolytic anemia (LDH, Rituclocytes, Bilirubin all elevated). What do you expect else? | B. Elevated IGM Cold Agglutinin @SWR-5064
q: I think there was a new Q I can't recall it fully but there was a Heinz body and bite cell what is the diagnosis ( no picture ) | B. G6PD @SWR-5086
q: 15-year-old boy present for routine follow-up. Labs: RBS: 5.3 (was slightly high) Hb: 98 (low) MCV: 69 (low) MCHC: 230 (within normal range) What is the diagnosis? | B. Beta thalassemia trait @SWR-5147
idea:
- [[0]] In HIT you do NOT transfuse platelets or simply stop anticoagulation: replace heparin with a non heparin anticoagulant because of the thrombosis risk.
- [[1]] 'Most definitive' or 'gold standard' means bone marrow; 'best initial' means ferritin.
- [[2]] Respiratory or pharyngeal infection + hemolysis = think cold agglutinins (IgM), not a second organism.
- [[3]] Do not test G6PD activity during an acute crisis: young cells have more enzyme and can give a falsely normal result; repeat after recovery.
- [[4]] Low MCV with a high RBC count in a well child = thalassemia trait; low MCV with high RDW and low ferritin = iron deficiency.

idea:
- [[0]] Initial action means stop heparin; if an option says stop heparin and start a non-heparin anticoagulant, that is the complete best answer.
- [[1]] Any blast population on the smear points to a production problem in the marrow; look for blasts before calling it immune.
- [[2]] Normal clotting tests with schistocytes = TTP/HUS; abnormal clotting with low fibrinogen = DIC.
- [[3]] HIT causes clots, not bleeding: stopping heparin is not enough, a non heparin anticoagulant is needed.

q: A 43-year-old man presented to the hospital with right leg swelling and pain. He has a history of pulmonary embolism 5 years ago and is currently not taking any medications. idea:
- [[0]] "Already on aspirin" removes aspirin as an answer; the question becomes what to add, not what to start.
- [[1]] Dabigatran is reversed by idarucizumab, not PCC or andexanet; do not mix up the DOAC antidotes.
- [[2]] Vitamin K is the trap: it reverses only warfarin, never apixaban or dabigatran.
- [[3]] Normal INR on a DOAC does not mean no anticoagulant effect; still reverse when there is intracranial bleeding.
- [[4]] If 'stop warfarin' is offered for a provoked DVT that has completed 3 months, the author would choose it; for AF (lifelong anticoagulation) stop the herb instead.
- [[5]] Warfarin in the list beats amlodipine every time; no warfarin, pick amlodipine.
- [[6]] Rifampicin hits liver-metabolized drugs; lisinopril and HCTZ go out through the kidney untouched.
- [[7]] Two drugs are 'right' here; the one that clots or bleeds wins.
- [[8]] Mechanical valve + rifampicin = INR falls; increase warfarin, never drop the rifampicin.
- [[9]] Any previous VTE + a new clot = long-term anticoagulation; 2 months is never enough.

- [[1]] A major bleed (hematemesis, intracranial bleed, hemodynamic compromise) flips the answer to IV vitamin K plus PCC or FFP.
- [[1]] ⚠ Standard algorithms for INR 4 to 10 also hold warfarin doses temporarily (and for symptomatic bleeding the reference algorithm gives vitamin K plus FFP or PCC); stopping the herbal alone is the best of the listed options but incomplete.
- [[2]] "History of PE" or "previous DVT" in the stem turns a finite course into long-term anticoagulation.

- [[4]] Read the vitals first: hypotension flips the answer from anticoagulation to thrombolysis.
- [[5]] TIA + ipsilateral carotid ≥70% = endarterectomy; TIA + AF = anticoagulate.
- [[6]] The examiner hides the answer in two numbers: CrCl below 30 (choose UFH) and BMI 40 (choose the higher, every-8-hour dose).
- [[7]] Vitamin K alone is never enough for a major bleed: it must be paired with PCC (or FFP if PCC is not an option).
- [[8]] Normal INR in a DOAC patient does not mean no anticoagulation; reverse the drug, not the lab value.
- [[9]] Hypotension changes the answer from heparin to thrombolysis; antiplatelets are never the answer for PE.

- [[1]] Same PE with BP 125/70 = anticoagulate only; hypotension is the trigger for thrombolysis.
- [[2]] When two antiplatelets and one anticoagulant are offered for a venous clot, the anticoagulant is the answer.
- [[3]] Thrombosis while on heparin with platelets falling by more than half at days 5 to 10 = HIT; then enoxaparin becomes the wrong answer.
- [[4]] Look for the blood pressure: hypotension turns LMWH into UFH/thrombolysis, and if alteplase is offered for an unstable patient it wins.
- [[5]] The lead in "INITIAL" with a high Wells score means treat first; with low probability the initial step becomes D dimer.
- [[6]] Intact distal pulses with a swollen leg = venous problem, so venous duplex; absent pulses and a cold limb = arterial, so ABI or angiography.
- [[7]] No ST elevation means no thrombolysis: the clot in NSTEMI is platelet rich and partially occlusive.
- [[8]] Life-threatening bleeding at any INR = IV vitamin K plus PCC; a minor nosebleed with INR 4 = remove the cause, hold, recheck.
- [[8]] ⚠ Standard practice for minor bleeding with INR 4.2 is to hold warfarin (and consider low dose vitamin K) as well as removing the cause; A is keyed because no such option was recalled.
- [[9]] "No bleeding" sends you to the hold-and-monitor branch; FFP appears only when there is bleeding.

- [[2]] Score 3 = aspirin alone; score 4 = dual; AF on ECG = anticoagulate regardless of score.

- [[7]] ⚠ Debated key. - [[9]] Hypertension is a distractor: without a true VTE risk factor, the answer is movement, not anticoagulation.

- [[2]] Ischemic stroke has a time window (3 months); hemorrhagic stroke never expires as a contraindication to tPA.
- [[3]] TIA in a patient with AF is a cardioembolic warning: the answer is anticoagulation, not aspirin.
- [[4]] Remove the cause of the high INR but do not abandon anticoagulation in AF; major bleeding is what flips the answer to reversal.
- [[4]] ⚠ Partial disagreement. - [[5]] Remove the cause of the high INR but do not abandon anticoagulation in AF; major bleeding is what flips the answer to reversal.

The high-INR algorithm sends any warfarin patient with signs of bleeding (here nosebleed and bruises) to the bleeding branch (admit, IV vitamin K with PCC or FFP), and even guidelines that grade this as minor bleeding hold warfarin temporarily and consider low-dose vitamin K. So stopping warfarin for now is also defensible; the key stays with stopping the herbal product and rechecking the INR because warfarin must not be abandoned permanently in AF.
- [[6]] Cancer plus a hospital stay is the stem's way of saying 'think clot, not infection'.
- [[7]] Rheumatic MS or a mechanical valve = warfarin, never a DOAC.
- [[8]] If the stem says low pretest probability (Wells unlikely), D dimer comes before ultrasound.
- [[9]] No bleeding means no IV vitamin K and no FFP; the only reversal-for-everyone scenario is active bleeding.

idea:
- [[0]] Stenosis means antiplatelet escalation; only an embolic cardiac source (AF, mural thrombus, mechanical valve) switches the answer to anticoagulation.
- [[1]] If the stem adds atrial fibrillation, the answer flips from antiplatelet + statin to anticoagulation.
- [[2]] "Most important fluid" points to blood for Hb 6 in shock; "next step to stop the bleeding" in a warfarin patient points to vitamin K plus PCC.
- [[3]] Hemorrhagic stroke at ANY time bars thrombolysis; ischemic stroke bars it only if within 3 months.
- [[4]] Exam wording may be garbled: "Purified protein factor" means prothrombin complex concentrate; with a high INR and active bleeding, factor replacement beats any crystalloid.
- [[5]] When the options list only one anticoagulant (warfarin) against antiplatelets, the anticoagulant is the answer for any AF patient scoring 2 or more.
- [[6]] Never thrombolyse ST depression; but check that inferior ST depression is not reciprocal to a high lateral (I, aVL) STEMI.
- [[7]] Thrombosis plus positive antiphospholipid antibodies on a DOAC: switch to warfarin.
- [[8]] Warfarin after TIA needs a reason (AF, cardiac thrombus, mechanical valve); 'recurrent TIA without AF' points to antiplatelets in current guidelines.
- [[8]] ⚠ Current guidelines (AHA/ASA, and the reference stroke notes) favor antiplatelet therapy for non-cardioembolic TIA, with aspirin plus clopidogrel (option C) for about 21 days after a high-risk TIA; warfarin is not indicated without AF or another cardioembolic source (no benefit over aspirin and more bleeding in WARSS/WASID). The keyed warfarin reflects outdated 'crescendo TIA' teaching.
- [[9]] Large trials found no benefit of intensive IV insulin over standard subcutaneous insulin, so in practice either route is used; the exam point is simply 'do not ignore hyperglycemia in stroke'.

q: 75 years old women know case of DM2, HTN , stage 3 renal impairment and dyslipdemia , was found to have atrial fibrillation on routine clinc visit , her vital are all normal , Echo show no structural problem , the desiccation to start on anticoagulant, most appropriate? | C. Apixaban @SWR-4772
q: Pt presented post op 5 days with SOB and right pleuritic chest pain, right leg swelling and redness compared to left Low o2 between 85-88 Tachycardia What is the INITIAL step in ASSESSMENT? | D. LMWH @SWR-4810
q: A 30-year-old man presents with unilateral calf pain and swelling. He has no significant past medical history, no chronic diseases, and no previous thromboembolic events.. What is the initial treatment? | B. DOAC anticoagulation @SWR-4905
q: 34 woman came with sob and chest pain that increased with inspiration, right leg swelling , which is painful with dorseflextion HR= 110 Blood pressure: 110/70 RR: 25 Most appropriate intial management: | B. Enoxaprin 10/kg sc ⚠ @SWR-4909
idea:
- [[0]] Unprovoked clot + tolerating anticoagulation + low bleeding risk = continue indefinitely; a transient trigger = stop at 3 months.
- [[0]] ⚠ Reference teaching states unprovoked DVT = 6 months (option B), so versions keyed B exist. Current CHEST and ASH guidance favours extended indefinite anticoagulation after the initial 3 months when bleeding risk is low, which supports the key C.
- [[1]] Read "completed 3 months" first: a therapeutic INR at the end of a provoked DVT course means stop, not adjust.
- [[1]] ⚠ Debated recall: the other version (SWR-0620) is keyed C and Athar answers C. D holds only if the DVT was provoked (3 months is then the full course); the stem does not say so, and for an unprovoked DVT extended anticoagulation is considered.
- [[2]] Mechanical valve or mitral stenosis flips the answer to warfarin; renal impairment among DOACs points to apixaban.
- [[3]] 'Initial step' in a high-probability PE is empirical anticoagulation; the diagnostic test only comes first when probability is low or the drug is contraindicated.
- [[4]] Pregnancy or active cancer changes the answer from DOAC to LMWH.
- [[5]] Tachycardia alone does not make PE unstable; hypotension does, and only then does thrombolysis beat anticoagulation.
== Leukemia, lymphoma & myeloma
q: Patient with Hodgkin lymphoma Cant urinate for 24 hours labs within normal In addition to hydration what will you add? | A. Furosemide @SWR-1308
q: 22-year-old male presented with pancytopenia lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly, pathology of lymph node biopsy showed Reed-Sternberg cells, What is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Hodgkin lymphoma @SWR-1470
q: A patient with leukemia who is 3 days post-chemotherapy collapses on the ward. What is the most likely cause? | B. Arrhythmia @SWR-2338
q: 19 yo girl with fatigue and jaundice. 3 weeks ago had chest infection which was treated with antibiotics. MCV, MCH was normal, Hb92, Reticulcytes 7, LDH (high), indirect bilirubin (high). Blood smear showed auto agllutination,Polychromasia, spherocytes, . Most likely underlying cause? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3032
q: 65 age Old patient with lymphoma came to the ER. (I can't remember why.) after chemo Labs showed: WBC was low (4), but neutrophils were normal. Options: | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-4038
q: Patient with Burkitt lymphoma (DLBCL in another recall) receiving B-CHOP chemotherapy. Labs: K = 6, hypocalcemia, unable to urinate. What is the most appropriate management in addition to hydration? | C. Rasburicase @SWR-4759
idea:
- [[0]] The same stem with high uric acid or deranged electrolytes moves the answer toward rasburicase.
- [[1]] Reed-Sternberg = Hodgkin regardless of the other findings in the stem.
- [[2]] Same patient but 10 days after chemotherapy with fever = neutropenic sepsis; it is the timing that flips the answer.
- [[3]] A recent chest infection plus autoagglutination is the Mycoplasma cue; pharyngitis plus lymphadenopathy is the EBV cue.
- [[4]] A "normal" neutrophil percentage can hide severe neutropenia when the total WBC is very low; always compute the absolute count.
- [[5]] In established TLS the answer with hydration is rasburicase, not allopurinol; avoid it in G6PD deficiency.
# Neurology
== Stroke & TIA  (1/3)
q: Patient presents after 30 minutes of slurred speech that resolved, no trauma or drug use or fever. Carotid shows stenosis 48% Best management besides controlling risk factors? | A. Observe @SWR-0032
q: {{rep:×2}} Women came with weakness, ECG (image show Afib), CT : shows infraction HR 300 what is the cause of the stroke? | A. Atrial Fibrillation (A.fib) @SWR-1003
q: A woman develops loss of sensation in an arm when she extends her neck. Which investigation is most appropriate? | B. MRI of the cervical spine @SWR-1488
q: 38 years old woman has weakness of the right arm with neck extension, MRI brain done and reported normal, what is the most appropriate NEXT investigation? | C. cervical spine MRI @SWR-1489
q: 34 yo pt was at irradiation for obstruction and reserved a lethal dose it was estimated 11 gray for howl body and 20 gry for specific Areas as a result he started having nusa and vomiting 2 weeks later he's complaining of loss of appetite and malaise What is the qunsicunsis | B. Bone marrow distraction @SWR-1612
q: case about a 70-year-old male with hypertension came presenting with a stroke. Calculate his CHADVASC2 score. | D. 4 @SWR-1723
q: A 72-year-old man is brought to the clinic by his wife due to episodes where he suddenly stops speaking, develops a blank, staring look, and displays repetitive facial grimacing and lip-smacking after which he becomes agatated. What is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Temporal lobe seizure @SWR-2340
q: A 46 year old woman presents with sudden onset facial weakness and inability to speak In her youth she had a history of fleeting joint pain, for which she required aspirin. She is currently undergoing Holter monitoring. An embolic stroke is suspected. A transesophageal echocardiogram is ordered. What is the most likely cause of her arrhythmia? | A. Atrial fibrillation @SWR-3098
q: Male, hypertensive, presents after an episode of left upper limb weakness that lasted for 5 minutes then completley resolved On examination there was No carotid bruit or cardicac murmurs and a Normal ECG What is the most appropriate next step | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3278
q: Female came with PE symptoms Elevated BP( i don't remember the number) And they want to give her tpa what will you do before give the medication | A. Confirm no recent stroke or bleeding before administration the tpa @SWR-3301
idea:
- [[0]] "Observe" here means medical therapy without intervention; read it as such when no drug option is listed.
- [[1]] Irregularly irregular with no P waves = AF; regular sawtooth = flutter, whatever rate is quoted.
- [[2]] The trigger "when she extends her neck" localizes the problem to the cervical spine, not the carotid.
- [[3]] Neck extension as the trigger is the clue that moves the work up from the brain to the cervical spine.
- [[4]] Timing separates the effects: weeks after exposure = marrow failure; years after = malignancy.
- [[5]] Stroke/TIA and age ≥75 are the only 2-point items; age 70 earns just 1.
- [[6]] Postictal confusion separates focal impaired awareness (temporal) seizures from absence seizures, which have none.
- [[7]] In a young woman with stroke, past migratory arthritis is the clue to rheumatic mitral stenosis with AF.
- [[8]] Absent bruit does not exclude significant carotid stenosis, so a carotid territory TIA still needs carotid imaging; if the lead-in asks for brain imaging, MRI wins instead.
- [[9]] High BP is fixable, a prior bleed is not: always rule out the unfixable contraindications first.
== Stroke & TIA  (2/3)
q: Long case for 90 year old female with sudden loss of consciousness, brought to hospital then became not oriented to time or place and agitated and say ( where am I!?) after give her Halo she ...... I can't remember the rest. Options : | B. Delirium @SWR-3432
q: Patient presents with neurological symptoms (confusion etc..) CT brain was done and is normal. Carotid imaging demonstrates 45% carotid artery stenosis. What is the most appropriate management? | C. Aspirin @SWR-3463
q: 58 yo women know to have htn dyslipedemia complain of sign of TIA for 30 min then resolved Ct normal Us carotid 45% What to do beside assuring risk factor? | A. Observe @SWR-3609
q: Previous ischemic stroke less than year I think Now coming with saddle PE What is the treatment? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3999
q: 80 year old man, complains of chest pain, presyncope and sob on examination ejection systolic murmur radiate to the carotids? Which of the following is the highest diagnostic? | A. echo @SWR-4018
q: Female patient presented to the clinic for a regular check-up. She has a history of RHF 15 years ago. Her cardiac examination shows a crescendo-decrescendo murmur radiating to the carotid. What is the most appropriate to her ? | B. Urgent echo @SWR-4249
q: 80 year old man, complains of chest pain, presyncope and sob on examination ejection systolic murmur radiate to the carotids? Which of the following is the highest diagnostic? | A. echo @SWR-4271
q: A patient is admitted to the hospital with an ischemic stroke that occurred 2 days ago. Which of the following is the most appropriate treatment? | B. Aspirin + high-intensity statin @SWR-4375
q: A woman with a past medical history of fleeting joint pains was previously treated with high-dose aspirin. She now presents to the hospital with weakness, and an embolic stroke is suspected. Which of the following is the most likely underlying cause? | B. Atrial fibrillation @SWR-4453
q: A woman in her 60s with diabetes, hypertension, dyslipidemia and an ischemic stroke 1 month ago presents with acute chest pain and ST-segment elevation on ECG. What is the appropriate reperfusion approach? | C. Urgent coronary angiography @SWR-4467
idea:
- [[0]] Acute confusion in a hospitalised elderly patient is delirium until proven otherwise; benzodiazepines worsen it unless the cause is withdrawal.
- [[1]] Change the number to 75% stenosis in a symptomatic patient and the answer becomes carotid endarterectomy.
- [[2]] "Observe" here means no procedure, not no treatment: antiplatelet and statin are still started after a TIA.
- [[3]] Before choosing alteplase for unstable PE, check for a recent stroke; if present, go mechanical.
- [[4]] Chest pain in AS is from the valve: echo first; angiography only as pre valve replacement workup.
- [[5]] Rheumatic heart disease is not, on its own, an indication for dental IE prophylaxis; do not confuse it with secondary penicillin prophylaxis against recurrent rheumatic fever.
- [[6]] Chest pain in AS is from the valve: echo first; angiography only as pre valve replacement workup.
- [[7]] The trap is the statin intensity: any option pairing stroke with a moderate or intermediate statin is wrong, even if the antiplatelet looks reasonable.
- [[8]] Trap: the aspirin is a clue to past rheumatic fever, not the cause of the stroke; think valve → AF → embolus.
- [[9]] 'Stroke 1 month ago' is there to kill the thrombolysis option; once it is gone, choose the cath lab answer that is offered.
- [[9]] ⚠ For a stem asking for the 'reperfusion approach' in STEMI, primary PCI (A) is the guideline answer; angiography is the diagnostic first part of the same procedure. A similar recall with PCI listed is keyed to PCI, so this version is debatable between A and C.
== Stroke & TIA  (3/3)
q: 62-year-old woman known to have type 2 diabetes mellitus, hypertension, dyslipidemia, and ischemic stroke 1 month ago, presented to the Emergency Room complaining of central stabbing continuous chest pain for 3 hours. She denied any dyspnea, palpitation, or change in consciousness. The patient is taking insulin, lisinopril, metoprolol, lipitor, and aspirin. Examination revealed she is in pain. She was started on oxygen, morphine, and sublingual nitroglycerin (see image). Which of the following is the most appropriate next step? | C. Urgent coronary angiography @SWR-4468
q: 30-year-old man has episodes of staring with a blank face, lip smacking, grimacing and automatisms. He has labile emotions and can suddenly become aggressive. | A. Temporal lobe epilepsy @SWR-4684
idea:
- [[0]] In ACS stems, a recent stroke, bleed or surgery is the clue that removes thrombolysis and makes the cath lab answer correct.
- [[1]] Automatisms alone do not mean absence: an ADULT with lip smacking, postictal confusion or an aura is temporal lobe epilepsy; a CHILD with seconds long blank spells many times a day is absence.
== Seizures & epilepsy  (1/2)
q: A patient presents with new-onset seizure; he seized 5 times without regaining consciousness between episodes. Which of the following is the most appropriate management? | A. Benzodiazepine @SWR-0253
q: {{rep:×2}} Epilepsy patient on carbamazepine 600 mg BID (not sure since how long) she still have it 1-2 times per month what is the appropriate management? | A. Carbamazepine 800 mg bid, @SWR-1241

During Surgery, He Was Given Hypotonic Fluids D5W Serum Na: 121 Serum Osm: ~250 (Something In This Range) Urine Osm: 600 Urine Sodium 46 What's The Management (Don't Remember Exact Stem) No Mention Of Any Seizure Or Any Signs Of Hypo-Na Other Than What I Mentioned Above | A. Fluid Restriction & Monitoring @SWR-1762
q: A young man developed a tonic-clonic seizure for the first time. The rest of the stem stated that the history and exam were negative for EVERYTHING (history went all the way back to birth, developmental, family). What is your next step? | B. CT Head @SWR-1904
q: Women came after 2 weak postpartm with headache and tonic-clonic seizure, and headache not relieved by paracetamol. What is the most appropriate next step | B. Head ct and venogram @SWR-2307
q: {{rep:×2}} A woman in her 20s with no previous history of epilepsy presents with brief, sudden, jerky movements of her arm. The rest of the history and examination are unremarkable. Which of the following is the most appropriate treatment? | B. Valproic acid @SWR-2934
q: Epilepsy patient (Type Of Epilepsy NOT Mentioned) on carbamazepine 600 mg BID (not sure since how long) she still have it 1-2 times per month what is the appropriate management? Carbamazepine Drug Level (40, Was Within Normal Range) | A. Carbamazepine 800 mg bid, @SWR-3596
q: woman came with 4 attacks tonic-clonic seizures without getting consciousness between the attacks, she known case of epilepsy and not compline on medction What is the first line treatment ? | B. Benzodiazepine @SWR-3735
idea:
- [[0]] Any ongoing seizure = benzodiazepine first; chronic antiepileptics (lamotrigine, carbamazepine) are never the acute answer.
- [[1]] If the stem says generalized or myoclonic seizures, the answer flips away from carbamazepine.
- [[2]] Sodium channel blockers (carbamazepine, phenytoin, and sometimes lamotrigine) can aggravate myoclonic and absence seizures.
- [[3]] Ethosuximide works for absence only; a myoclonic stem with ethosuximide offered is a distractor.
- [[4]] "Rapidly" is the trap word; hypertonic saline is never given as a rapid uncontrolled correction.
- [[4]] ⚠ Confusion and drowsiness are neurological symptoms; current guidance (and the reference rule "symptomatic hyponatremia: hypertonic saline 3%") would favour a controlled hypertonic saline bolus if offered. Fluid restriction is the best listed choice only because option C specifies rapid correction.
- [[5]] Febrile child aged 6 months to 6 years with a brief generalized seizure = febrile seizure, no CT and no EEG needed.
- [[6]] Read the lead-in: "initial" may justify plain CT, but "most definitive" or "highest diagnostic value" means the venogram.
- [[7]] If the stem stresses a woman planning pregnancy and levetiracetam is offered, it beats valproate.
- [[8]] A level 'within range' is not a ceiling; if the patient tolerates it and the daily dose is below 1600 mg, uptitrate before adding a second drug.
- [[9]] No drug given yet = benzodiazepine; benzodiazepine already failed = phenytoin.

q: A 17-year-old patient with a known history of epilepsy is currently taking Carbamazepine 600 but continues to experience seizure attacks 1-2 times per month. What is the most appropriate next step in management? | A. Increase the dose of Carbamazepine to 800 mg BID @SWR-4161

q: A 30-year-old man is brought by his family because of behavioral changes. He has episodes of emotional lability, lip smacking, and grimacing. Sometimes, following these episodes, he becomes agitated. What is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Temporal lobe epilepsy @SWR-4683
q: 17 Year Old Female With Frequent Fainting And Fall, Last Time While Talking To Her Father Felt Her Legs Are Weak And Fell Down, Lost Consciousness, Then Regained Level Of Consciousness, What Is The Type Of Seizure? | A. Atonic @SWR-4753
q: 35 years old healthy male presented with first time seizure medically free he Is not on any medication . (his wife was with him and she is the one who saw him during the attack) tonic clonic generalized seizure Which of the following is most appropriate initial investigation? | A. serum electrolytes  and blood glucose @SWR-4907
q: Q7. 27 year old male with multiple attacks of tonic clonic seizures for 3 hours without regaining of consciousness between the attacks. He is known case of epilepsy who is not adherent to his meds. Which of the following the most appropriate initial management of his case (not sure about the stem but it was initial): | C. Benzodiazepine @SWR-4983
q: Epilepsy patient (Type Of Epilepsy NOT Mentioned) on carbamazepine 600 mg BID (not sure since how long) she still have it 1-2 times per month what is the appropriate management? Carbamizapine Drug Level (Written in Number , Was Within Normal Range) | A. Carbamazepine 800 mg bid, @SWR-5043
q: 27 year old male with multiple attacks of tonic clonic seizures for 3 hours without regaining of consciousness between the attacks. He is known case of epilepsy who is not adherent to his meds. Which of the following the most appropriate initial management of his case (not sure about the stem but it was initial): | C. Benzodiazepine @SWR-5100
idea:
- [[0]] 'Not controlled' and the dose is below maximum: the answer is always increase the dose, not add a drug.
- [[1]] However long the seizure has lasted, the first drug given is still a benzodiazepine; in pregnancy with eclampsia it is MgSO4.
- [[2]] Automatisms + postictal confusion = temporal lobe; automatisms without postictal phase in a child = absence.
- [[3]] Falling limp without jerking = atonic; falling stiff = tonic; staring without falling = absence.
- [[4]] If the stem already says "labs normal", the answer moves to brain imaging, not labs.
- [[5]] No drug given yet = benzodiazepine; benzodiazepine already given and failed = phenytoin or another second line agent.
- [[6]] A 'normal' level does not mean an adequate dose; treat the patient, not the level.
- [[7]] However long the seizure has lasted, the first drug given is still a benzodiazepine; in pregnancy with eclampsia it is MgSO4.
== Headache  (1/2)
q: {{rep:×2}} A 27-year-old presents with sudden severe headache while bending down, described as "worst of my life." CT brain is negative. Next step? | B. Lumbar puncture @SWR-0622
q: 51 year old with headache and nick stiffness her husband confirmed that she had fever csf shows turbed fluid polymorths low glucose high protein what is the most likely organism | B. streptococcus pneumonia @SWR-1639
q: A 51-year-old woman presented with 2 days of malaise and headache, worsening neck pain, fever, confusion, and positive Kernig sign. CSF: Turbid High protein Low glucose neutrophilic/polymorph predominance Most likely organism? | C. Streptococcus pneumoniae @SWR-1752
q: Weightlifter with worst headache of his life and vomiting. Most appropriate management. | B. CT without contrast @SWR-1789
q: A 52-year-old man presents to the emergency department with a sudden-onset severe headache described as "the worst headache. Which of the following is the best diagnostic test? | A. Lumbar puncture @SWR-1968
q: {{rep:×2}} A patient on chemotherapy developed fever, headache and diarrhea. Her ANC is 200. She is hypotensive and tachycardic but oriented. Which of the following should be included in the initial diagnostic assessment? | A. Lactate and procalcitonin @SWR-2389
q: {{rep:×2}} Elderly comes with left sided hemiplegia affecting the left arm , leg and face with intact sensation and speaking, he has a history of uncontrolled HTN and not adhered to his medications, where is the most likely site of the lesion? | C. Basal ganglia @SWR-3047
q: {{rep:×2}} 75 Yo male headache slurred speech and left sided weakness BP 165/95 CT: Thalamic hematoma or smth What is the most appropriate management to prevent extension of hematoma ? | D. reduce systolic BP to 140 @SWR-3108
q: A woman presents with hypertension associated with sweating, headache, and palpitations, What is the most appropriate initial investigation? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3473
q: 52 y/o knows case of HTN refusing to take his medication, came to ER complaining of left upper and lower hemiplegia . ct done: ct brain showed hemorrhage. where do you expect the hemorrhage to? | C. basal ganglia @SWR-3881
idea:
- [[0]] Negative CT never ends the SAH workup in a thunderclap headache; LP comes next.
- [[1]] Over 50 means you ADD Listeria cover to the antibiotics, but the most likely organism is still pneumococcus.
- [[2]] Listeria is the answer only with a clue: pregnancy, immunosuppression or old age as the focus of the stem.
- [[3]] Exertion (lifting, straining, bending, coitus) plus worst headache of life = SAH; first test is always plain CT, not angiography.
- [[4]] 'Initial' or 'first' test = CT; 'confirm' after a negative CT = LP; 'find the source' = angiography.
- [[4]] ⚠ Most guidelines make non-contrast CT the first and usually best diagnostic test for suspected SAH (Part 1 of this recall keyed C); LP is reserved for a negative CT done more than 6 hours after onset.
- [[5]] Hypotension in neutropenic fever: cultures, lactate, fluids and antibiotics now; LP is off the table while unstable.
- [[6]] Add sensory loss to the picture and the site shifts to the thalamus; add aphasia or neglect and it becomes cortical.
- [[7]] Cerebellar hemorrhage is the exception: a large one (over 3 cm) or one with hydrocephalus needs emergent surgery.
- [[8]] Read what 'initial investigation' is for: confirming pheochromocytoma means a biochemical test (never imaging first), while assessing palpitations at the bedside means an ECG.
- [[9]] Thunderclap headache with neck stiffness and no focal deficit points to subarachnoid hemorrhage, not hypertensive ICH.

He reports having an episode of severe sore throat and fever 5 days ago??, for which he did not seek medical evaluation. Which of the following laboratory findings would best support the provisional diagnosis in this patient? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-5037
idea:
- [[0]] No improvement on amoxicillin = think beta-lactamase producers and add clavulanate before imaging.
- [[1]] If the stem had said 2 to 3 weeks, low C3 (PSGN); if 1 to 2 days, normal complement (IgA nephropathy).
== Neuromuscular: GBS, MG, neuropathy
q: Patient with 3 days hx of progressive lower limb weakness with urine retention. Also sensation at T10: | C. Spinal compression @SWR-1252
q: A patient with benign prostatic hyperplasia (BPH) was prescribed amitriptyline for neuropathic pain. He subsequently developed constipation and dysuria/urinary retention. What is the most likely mechanism responsible for these adverse effects? | C. Antimuscarinic effects @SWR-2567
q: Man with (I think BPH but I am really not sure) was asymptomatic then took amitriptyline for neuropathic pain after that had constipation and urinary retention what is the cause of his symptoms: | B. Drug-induced bladder dysfunction @SWR-2568
idea:
- [[0]] A sensory LEVEL means the cord; areflexia and saddle anesthesia mean cauda equina or nerves.
- [[1]] Serotonin syndrome gives diarrhea, not constipation; constipation plus retention is the anticholinergic signature.
- [[2]] Retention plus constipation without back pain or neuro deficit points to the drug, not the cord.
== Movement disorders & dementia
q: A 15-year-old boy is brought to the physician by his parents due to a one-week history of fatigue, visible jaundice, and TREMOR in his hands. His parents note that his speech has recently DYSARTHRK Physical examination is notable for scleral icterus and a distinct greenish discoloration encircling the CORNEOSCLERAL junction Laboratory studies are obtained and reveal the following: Direct bilirubin: 1 8 mol Indirect bilirubin: 20 mol (Normal: 1-6 mol) normal < 18 mol AST 1000+ ALT 1000+ Which of the following is most likely to be found in this patient's further diagnostic workup? | B. Low serum ceruloplasmin @SWR-0449
q: A patient with Wilson disease presents with tremor, jaundice, and low ceruloplasmin. Best treatment? | B. D-penicillamine @SWR-0853
q: Pt w chronic hep B, presented with inc fatigue and (flapping?) tremor, on exam he has shifting dullness Na 133 K 3.2 What to give? | B. Spirolactone @SWR-2409
q: {{rep:×2}} An older patient with dementia is taking 10 concurrent medications, including Quetiapine prescribed for agitation. The patient has no history of cardiovascular disease. Which of the following medications is most appropriate to consider stopping? | B. Quetiapine @SWR-2428
q: Patient with Alzheimer's on medication presented with urinary retention and constipation. What's the cause? | B. Anticholinergic activity causing urinary retention. @SWR-2569
q: 84 year old diagnosed with dementia 2 months ago. Presented and treated with a laceration from a fall. Fortunately, he had no fractures. How to prevent future falls? | C. Improve lightening on stairs in his home @SWR-3118
q: An elderly patient with " ? early dementia ? " had a fall but fortunately did not sustain a fracture. " ? ?What is the most appropriate measure ??" to prevent future fractures? | A. Ensure adequate lighting around the stairs @SWR-3119
q: chronic alcoholic patient presents to the emergency department with jaundice, tremors, and asterixis and confusion. Which of the following is the most appropriate management or next most appropriate ? | A. Administer IV thiamine and glucose to prevent encephalopathy. @SWR-3412
q: dementia pt, he is semi dependable, want to continue to dive, how to asses his ability to drive: | B. Driving test @SWR-3720
q: A patient presents with a typical clinical scenario of dementia. What is the next step? | A. Brain MRI @SWR-4580
idea:
- [[0]] Ceruloplasmin is an acute-phase protein and can look falsely normal in acute liver failure; urinary copper and the KF ring still support the diagnosis.
- [[1]] Penicillamine can transiently worsen neurological symptoms; trientine is the alternative if it is not tolerated.
- [[2]] Hypokalemia precipitates hepatic encephalopathy, so a cirrhotic with low K and asterixis gets the potassium-sparing diuretic.
- [[3]] Antipsychotic prescribed for agitation in dementia = the classic deprescribing target; the statin is the tempting but lower priority choice.
- [[4]] Donepezil itself is pro-cholinergic; retention and constipation in a dementia patient mean an added anticholinergic, not the Alzheimer drug.
- [[5]] "Prevent falls" = remove the hazard (lighting); "prevent fractures from osteoporosis" would point to calcium/vitamin D.
- [[6]] Even when the lead-in says "prevent fractures", the keyed answer here is the fall-prevention measure (stair lighting).
- [[7]] Never give glucose alone to a malnourished alcoholic; thiamine goes in first or together.
- [[8]] If CDR appears among the options, choose it; MMSE "only" is the trap.
- [[9]] LP and EEG are answers only when the stem is atypical (rapid, young, myoclonus, fever); a 'typical' dementia always goes to imaging.

CT head @SWR-3288
q: a 74 years elderly with symptoms of dementia appeared slowly for years, has urinary incontinence, has a history of HIV for years diagnosis: | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3424
q: man known case of HIV, have sizure in tounge bites and bruis in the body In ct no sign of hemorheage Vital signs Temp 37.9 | B. Ct brain @SWR-3426
idea:
- [[0]] Immunocompromised + seizure: CT before LP, every time.
- [[1]] "CT already done, no contraindication" moves you to LP; do not order the CT again.
- [[2]] Positive chlamydia and negative gonorrhoea: no ceftriaxone needed; chlamydia cover alone.
- [[2]] ⚠ Current CDC guidance (2021) prefers doxycycline 100 mg twice daily for 7 days for chlamydia, with azithromycin 1 g as the alternative; if doxycycline was the unrecalled option D it would be favoured. Of the listed options, azithromycin is still correct.
- [[3]] Urethritis plus arthritis with tenosynovitis and skin pustules = disseminated gonococcal infection; with conjunctivitis and a sterile tap = reactive arthritis.
- [[4]] Alcohol use tempts you to blame withdrawal; in an untreated HIV patient a new seizure means image the brain first.
- [[5]] HIV turns a first seizure into a structural-lesion question: image first.
- [[6]] Dementia + urinary incontinence + abnormal gait = NPH; HIV in the history is not automatically the cause.
- [[7]] In HIV, CT (or MRI) first, LP second; but if a CT has already excluded a mass, the LP is next.
- [[7]] ⚠ Version conflict: the same recall in Part 2 is keyed to lumbar puncture. If the stem means a CT was already done and showed no lesion, LP is the logical next step; CT brain is right only if imaging for mass lesions has not yet been done.
== TB  (1/2)
q: Nurse PPD 12 , no x ray mentioned . Tt : | A. Isnonzide 600mg 6month. @SWR-0081

Exam reveals right knee arthritis and limited spinal movement. Cardiopulmonary exam unremarkable. Right knee joint aspiration: WBC 15,000 (normal 0-200). Blood culture negative. q: 48-year-old woman presents with progressive abdominal distension for the past few weeks. She denies fever or jaundice. She was diagnosed with tuberculosis 10 years ago, completed treatment, and has been well since. On examination, there is shifting dullness, suggesting ascites. An ascitic tap is done. Results show: White blood cells: 80 cells/mm', mostly mesothelial cells Ascitic fluid albumin: 15 g/L (1.5 g/dL) Serum albumin: 35 g/L (3.5 g/dL) What is the most likely cause of this patient's ascites? | C. Heart failure @SWR-1834
q: 24 years old Woman presented with cough and lymphadenopathy. She denied weight loss, night sweats, fever. Examination was remarkable for hepatomegaly. (Not see if my mind made this part or if it was included in another question): on imaging she had hilar lymphadenopathy What's the best next step? | D. Biopsy through bronchoscope @SWR-2228
q: Case of Pleural effusion: Serum LDH 100 Pleural LDH 200 Serum protein 60 Pleural protein 35 Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? | C. Tuberculosis @SWR-2733
idea:
- [[0]] Match the cutoff to the person: 12 mm is positive in a nurse but negative in someone with no risk factors.
- [[0]] ⚠ Regimen (isoniazid 6 months) is correct, but the standard daily adult isoniazid dose is 300 mg; 600 mg daily is not a standard dose, likely a recall error.
- [[1]] Negative blood culture does not exclude brucellosis; with raw milk in the stem, order serology, not a biopsy.
- [[2]] "Massive effusion but trachea central" in a smoker = bronchial carcinoma with collapse; the fuller version of this recall keys exactly that.
- [[3]] Massive effusion but trachea and apex beat central in a smoker = carcinoma obstructing the ipsilateral main bronchus.
- [[4]] Low glucose alone does not make it TB; look for the ratio, the time course and the protein level.
- [[5]] Diarrhea is the clue that ties the travel history to water; if cough and night sweats stood alone, think airborne TB.
- [[5]] ⚠ Weight loss, night sweats and cough are the classic TB triad (airborne spread); if an airborne option was among the unrecalled C or D, it may be the better answer. Contaminated water fits only if diarrhea is the dominant feature (enteric fever, giardiasis).
- [[6]] Old TB + high SAAG + normal serum albumin = think constrictive pericarditis (heart failure), not peritoneal TB.
- [[7]] The TB history is a trap: it tempts "tuberculosis", but high SAAG excludes peritoneal TB and redirects to constrictive pericarditis.
- [[8]] Absent B symptoms with bilateral hilar nodes in a young (black) woman = sarcoidosis; pick the least invasive biopsy offered.
- [[9]] Calculate both ratios; one positive criterion is enough to call it an exudate.
== TB  (2/2)
q: A 70-year-old man presented to the Emergency Department complaining of increasing shortness of breath and a productive cough for the past 5 months. He has been a smoker for over 50 years. Over the course of 5 months, he complained of malaise, night sweats, fever, and has lost 10 kg over the last 3 months. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? | A. Lung carcinoma @SWR-2850
q: Old age with 4 weeks history of fatigue, malaise, productive cough, night sweats, came complaining of SOB, upon examination dullness on percussion and diminished air entery, suspected TB pleural effusion Which of the following investigations to confirm diagnosis? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3043
q: Old age with 4 weeks history of fatigue, malaise, productive cough, night sweats, came complaining of SOB, upon examination dullness on percussion and diminished air entery, suspected TB pleural effusion Which of the following investigations to confirm diagnosis? | A. AFB positive ⚠ @SWR-3258
q: Patient with weight loss fatigue (night sweats not sure?) it was giving a picture of malignancy vs TB and on exam there was decreased breath sounds and DULNESS : | A. Needle aspiration @SWR-3564
q: woman presents with progressive abdominal distension for the past few weeks or months? She was diagnosed with tuberculosis 10 years ago, completed treatment, and has been well since. On examination, there is ascites No ascitic tap results was given! Only mild elevation of AST AlT (around 80-90) , albumin around 15 What is the most likely cause of this patient's ascites? | B. cirrhosis @SWR-3703
q: Patient came complaining of progressive dyspnea for 3 months with decreased tactile fremitus, decreased air entry and dullness on the right side. Vitally stable. Whats the cause? LDH: 100 Pleural LDH: 200 I do not remember any fever or jaundice | B. Tuberculosis @SWR-3778
q: Very typical s&s of TB and patient had contact with one affected. And literally said NORMAL NEUROLOGICAL EXAMINATION. And was vitally stable Chest x-ray: cavitation upper right How to manage? | A. Isoniazid Rifampin Pyrazinamide Ethambutol @SWR-4687
q: 49 female opd complain of fever night sweats wt loss PE/generlized lymphedenopathy LN biopsy b cell lymphoma high fgrade started chemotherapy now compplain of fatigue dec urine ouptput Na 135 K 6.6 Ca 1.5 Phosphate 2.7 Uric acid 680 Cr 190 What is the initial management ? | C. iv NS @SWR-4977
idea:
- [[0]] If TB is offered with a TB exposure clue (prison, homeless, apical cavity), TB beats cancer; a pure heavy smoker picture favours carcinoma.
- [[1]] Sensitive = ADA; specific = positive AFB; definitive yield = pleural biopsy. Read the lead-in verb.
- [[2]] Check whether the option says culture or smear and whether the lead-in says confirm or most sensitive; these two words flip the answer between A and D.
- [[3]] Dullness means fluid: tap it with a needle to diagnose; a chest tube is for draining pus, blood or air.
- [[4]] Old, treated TB with no constitutional symptoms does not make it TB ascites; add fever, weight loss and a low SAAG and the answer flips to peritoneal TB.
- [[5]] Pleural LDH higher than serum LDH is an exudate; once the options leave only one exudative cause, pick it.
- [[6]] "Normal neurological examination" is the clue that removes dexamethasone; any meningeal or neurological sign in a TB patient flips the answer to four drugs + dexamethasone.
- [[7]] Allopurinol is the prevention answer; once TLS is established and the lead-in says initial management, hydration comes first.
== Meningitis & CNS infection
q: A 50-year-old man presented to the emergency department with fever, severe headache, neck stiffness, and photophobia for two days. On physical examination, the physician asked the patient to flex his neck forward. This maneuver caused involuntary flexion of the patient's hips and knees. Which of the following clinical signs is demonstrated in this patient? | C. Brudzinski sign @SWR-1044
q: Patient with meningitis asking how to protect house contacts? | B. give prophylaxis @SWR-1112
q: Another scenario about meningitis, CSF finding were given and blood findings, when I calculated the CSF:Serum glucose it was 0.3 ratio, diagnosis? | C. TB meningitis @SWR-1771
q: IV drug user with meningitis, LP showed lymphocytic predominance of cells (count was 200 I believe), high protein, glucose was the exact number of the lower normal range, and blood glucose ratio below 4. | A. TB meninigitis @SWR-1906
q: Meningitis case : came with convulsions- tonic clonic seziure and other Labs: 5 glu ( serum ) 5 glu (LP) Pro 700 high ( 500) Lymphocytosis Most appropriate specific Manengment : | A. iv acyclovir @SWR-4246
q: Meningitis case with tonic clonic seizure Glucose Serum and CSF 5 Lymphocyte 85% How to manage | B. IV Acyclovir @SWR-4247
q: Typical signs of meningitis Serum glucose: 5.2 CSF: Glucose: 4.1 Cells: very high Lymphocytes 70% Protein: 450 (Normal 150-450) What is the diagnosis? | B. Viral meningitis @SWR-4686
idea:
- [[0]] Neck moved, legs respond: Brudzinski; leg moved, pain at the back: Kernig.
- [[1]] Household contacts = prophylaxis; hospital staff and ward = isolation for the first 24 hours of antibiotics.
- [[2]] Low glucose with lymphocytes is TB; low glucose with neutrophils is bacterial. The stem here is incompletely recalled, so check the cell type on your paper.
- [[3]] Low glucose means bacterial or TB; then the cell type decides: neutrophils = bacterial, lymphocytes = TB.
- [[4]] Seizure or altered behaviour with lymphocytic, normal glucose CSF = HSV encephalitis until proven otherwise: start IV acyclovir.
- [[5]] Low CSF glucose with lymphocytes flips the answer to TB meningitis, not viral.
- [[6]] Lymphocytes alone do not separate viral from TB: the glucose decides (normal = viral, low = TB).
== Neutropenic fever & fungal  (1/3)
q: female adult had febrile neutropenia after chemotherapy. What is the initial antibiotic? | A. Cefepime @SWR-0221
q: Breast cancer patient developed febrile neutropenia, given IV pipracillin-tazobactam AND VANCOMYCIN, still no improvement for 5 days. ANC 400, what is the best next step | A. IV antifungal @SWR-0595

q: A 17 year old developed febrile neutropenia and shock post chemotherapy for lymphoma. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial antibiotic? | A. meropenem @SWR-0823
q: A Sickle cell disease patient who underwent bone marrow transplant. He got a fever 1 day after the procedure. He was started on ceftazidime but did not improve after 72 hours. There was no pneumonia or hypotension. What to add ? | A. Voriconazole @SWR-0824
q: Patient underwent bone marrow transplantation due to SCD, developed febrile neutropenia and has [NO] Pneumonia , she was given Ceftazidime and shows no improvement, what to add? | A. Vancomycin @SWR-0825
q: Male with some cancer ended up developing fever prior to initiation of chemotherapy Management: | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-1091
q: A 58-year-old man presents with a 6-month history of progressive breathlessness. He has smoked 10-15 cigarettes per day all his adult life. On physical examination, he has lost the nail fold angle of his fingers. What is the most likely diagnosis? | B. Bronchogenic carcinoma @SWR-1721
q: {{rep:×2}} 64 yr old male came to clinic with progressive SOB on exertion. He also has a productive cough most days that started two years ago. Hypertensive and on Amlodipine. He retired two years ago and used to work in a cotton textile company/factory (forgot which one) xray normal, PFT low with moderate increase after salbutamol | A. COPD @SWR-2311
idea:
- [[0]] Vancomycin is an add-on, never the initial monotherapy, in febrile neutropenia.
- [[1]] Day 1 fever = IV antibiotics; fever still present after 4 to 7 days of antibiotics = add antifungal.
- [[2]] Sickle cell plus fever usually tempts you to ceftriaxone, but 'post transplant' means neutropenia, which demands antipseudomonal cover.
- [[3]] Same stem WITHOUT pneumonia and fever persisting beyond several days flips the add-on to an antifungal.
- [[4]] 'Shock' tempts you to vancomycin, but the question asks for the initial antibiotic, which must cover Pseudomonas.
- [[5]] 'No pneumonia or hypotension' is the clue that kills vancomycin and points to the antifungal.
- [[6]] Check whether an antifungal is listed: if it is, it beats vancomycin when there is no pneumonia or hypotension.
- [[6]] ⚠ Vancomycin is not recommended for persistent neutropenic fever alone; with no pneumonia, hypotension, line or skin infection, the guideline step is an empiric anti-mold antifungal (after about 4 to 7 days). The key works only by elimination because no antifungal is listed.
- [[7]] 'Prior to chemotherapy' is the trap: without a haematological cancer or a low WBC he is not neutropenic, so the cancer type and count decide; antifungal is only for fever that persists on antibiotics.
- [[8]] Smoker plus clubbing = cancer, not COPD.
- [[9]] If the bronchodilator response returned spirometry to normal, or symptoms disappeared on days off work, switch to asthma (occupational asthma).

q: 65 male had a transient ischemic attack one month ago and was started on ACA 81 AS prevention therapy I think it means aspirin; it was written literally like this. He has no comorbidity They stated in the question that he is not a candidate for surgery or something? He also has a recurrent transient ischemic attack now, and he has carotid stenosis. What is the appropriate next step? | C. ASA and clopidogrel @SWR-2689
q: 3- A 30 year old male patient known case of rheumatoid arthritis on treatment with methotrexate presents with fever and rigors. Examination shows oral ulcers with no organomegaly or splenomegaly. (See lab report) Fever 38.9 c what is the most likely diagnosis? | C. Methotrexate induced neutropenia @SWR-2948
q: Fertile neutropenia case started cefipime now it's day 3 and still febrile | A. Vancomycin @SWR-3356
q: 58 years old man known smoker for all of his adult life, smokes 10-15 cigs per day, during examination, the folds in his nails were absent (clubbing), what is the most likely dx? | B. bronchial carcinoma @SWR-3419
q: 37 years old female has breast cancer received dense dose of chemo developed febrile neutropenia received IV piperacillin/tazobactam and IV vancomycin but remain had fever despite ANC <100/MM | B. Initiate IV anti fungal ⚠ @SWR-3717
idea:
- [[0]] Site specific tests (CXR, urine, LP, stool) only follow site specific symptoms; sepsis markers and blood cultures are for everyone.
- [[1]] Smoker with cough is COPD until you see clubbing; then it is cancer.
- [[2]] Persistent fever, stable patient, no focus = antifungal; the same picture with hypotension or an infected line = remove the line.
- [[3]] Partial bronchodilator response with long dust exposure means COPD; full reversibility would mean asthma.
- [[4]] What decides between the options is the extra finding: clear reversibility points to asthma, eosinophilia with peripheral infiltrates to eosinophilic pneumonia, a cavity or fungal ball to aspergillosis, and fixed obstruction after exposure to COPD.
- [[5]] The trap is jumping to anticoagulation for "failed aspirin"; without AF or another embolic source the move is DAPT, not a DOAC or warfarin.
- [[6]] Same RA neutropenia picture with a palpable spleen flips the answer to Felty syndrome.
- [[7]] Vancomycin is an add on, never a sole empiric agent; an antifungal option would outrank it here.
- [[7]] ⚠ Current guidance (IDSA) does not recommend adding vancomycin for persistent fever alone in a stable neutropenic patient; it is added for specific indications (sepsis or shock, catheter or skin infection, MRSA, pneumonia), and an antifungal is added if fever persists beyond 4 to 7 days. Vancomycin remains the best of the listed options.
- [[8]] COPD never clubs: clubbing in a smoker means look for cancer.
- [[9]] Without a stated duration, persistent fever on day 2 means continue and re-culture; by day 4 to 7 it means add an antifungal.
== Neutropenic fever & fungal  (3/3)
q: A patient with rheumatoid arthritis on methotrexate develops chills and mouth ulcers with fever but normal vital signs otherwise. What is the most likely cause? (no labs) no hepatomegaly no splenomegaly Mentioned | C. Methotrexate-induced neutropenia @SWR-4421
idea:
- [[0]] RA + neutropenia with splenomegaly = Felty; RA on methotrexate + mouth ulcers and fever without splenomegaly = methotrexate induced neutropenia.
== Tropical & zoonotic: malaria, brucella
q: man presents to ER with fever, rigors and chills ,He also reports back pain, he has history of drinking unpasteurized milk. What is the most appropriate lab test? | C. Brucellosis serology @SWR-0027
q: Hajj meningococcal chemoprophylaxis. | B. Rifampin for 2 days BID @SWR-1447
q: {{rep:×2}} Reports shows an increase in recurrence of malaria after treatment, what is the type of plasmodium ? | B. Vivax @SWR-1754
q: man , hx of travel to tropical area Presented with sudden fever and joint pain. doctor suspected dengue fever. What is the mode of transmission? | A. Aedes mosquito @SWR-1997
q: A patient recently returned from a tropical region and presents with jaundice, right upper quadrant abdominal pain, nausea, and malaise. Laboratory results show: ALT: 1000 U/L AST: 1200 U/L Total bilirubin: 6.2 mg/dL Which of the following is the most appropriate test to establish the diagnosis? | A. Hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) @SWR-2509
q: A 30-year-old man is planning to travel to a rural area in sub-Saharan Africa for 2 weeks. He has no co morbidities and is healthy The area is endemic for malaria. Which of the following is the most initial preventitive measure? | B. Give yellow fever vaccine and antimalarial chemoprophylaxis @SWR-2983
q: Case of patient with recurrent attack of malaria after treatment, which type? | A. P. Vivax @SWR-3938
q: An epidemiologist is examining the causes of malaria recurrence amongst prior malaria patients. Which of the following plasmodia is responsible for recurrence? | D. Vivax @SWR-4740
idea:
- [[0]] Raw milk in the stem = Brucella; the first test is serology, not imaging.
- [[1]] If the option read "ciprofloxacin single 500 mg dose" for an adult pilgrim, it would also be correct; the trap here is the wrong duration.
- [[2]] Relapse means hypnozoites (vivax, ovale); severity means falciparum.
- [[3]] Aedes bites by DAY (early morning); Anopheles (malaria) bites at night.
- [[4]] HBcAg is never ordered in blood; if you see it as an option, it is the distractor.
- [[4]] ⚠ Return from a tropical region with acute hepatitis favours hepatitis A (or E); if the unrecalled option was IgM anti-HAV or a viral hepatitis panel, that would be preferred over HBsAg.
- [[5]] Repellents and nets are the answer only when the question asks about bite avoidance for an area with no chemoprophylaxis need; in a falciparum area, drugs are mandatory.
- [[6]] Relapse (liver hypnozoites) = vivax or ovale; recrudescence (surviving blood parasites) = falciparum resistance or malariae.
- [[7]] Hypnozoites = relapse = vivax (and ovale); falciparum is the killer but never relapses.

q: {{rep:×2}} 20 yr old female with fever & suprapubic pain & tenderness Temp 38.8. Urinalysis was +ve for leukocytes & nitrites. Most appropriate next step? | B. Take urine culture & start emperic abx @SWR-0935
