q: 20ish male known ALL started on chemo. On day 19 post chemo, grade 1 mucositis mouth, RLQ pain colicky with rebound, bloody stool. Vitally stable Low grade fever 37.8 Low WBC low neutrophils Most appropriate step (not sure): | C. CT scan @SWR-1774
q: A 21 year healthy female with frequent UTI 4 per year wants to prevent it. What is the best clinical based evidence choice to prevent recurrent UTI? | C. Prophylactic Antibiotics @SWR-1882
idea:
- [[0]] Cyclical fever after Africa = blood film; step-ladder fever with relative bradycardia = blood culture for typhoid.
- [[1]] Make her pregnant, febrile with flank pain, or a child and culture becomes mandatory before antibiotics.
- [[2]] 'Extended spectrum penicillin' in a febrile leukemia question = piperacillin-tazobactam.
- [[3]] Adding fever to this same stem changes the answer to culture plus empiric antibiotics.
- [[4]] Fever turns "start antibiotics" into "culture, then start antibiotics"; waiting for the culture result is never right.
- [[5]] Unknown effusion = diagnostic tap first; chest tube only once the fluid shows empyema or blood.
- [[6]] Clindamycin wins only because no penicillin or cephalosporin is offered; if cefazolin, cephalexin or flucloxacillin appears, pick it instead.
- [[7]] A normal voice keeps the problem in the tonsils; a muffled voice or stridor moves it to the airway.
- [[8]] In a neutropenic patient, a scope can perforate the bowel; CT is the safe answer.
- [[9]] If the question asks for the first or simplest measure rather than the best evidence, answers like hydration or cranberry may be keyed.

q: 64 years old presented to ED with fever and malaise. He was recently diagnosed with metastatic recatal adenocarcinoma and was started on chemotherapy one week prior. WBC: 0.7 Neutrophils: 40 (normal 40-60%) What is the important test to Order now? | A. Blood culture @SWR-2858
q: {{rep:×2}} A 22-year-old woman previously healthy was admitted with 3-days history of fever, myalgia and pharyngitis. She gave history of 1-day macular rash over the trunk after she received amoxicillin from private clinic. On examination, she has generalised lymphadenopathy. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? | C. Infectious mononucleosis @SWR-2952
q: 65-year-old male complaining of fever and malaise, recently diagnosed with rectal adenocarcinoma and started chemotherapy prior to his presentation, he looks ill. Temperature: 38.7 WBC: 0.9 What is the most important test to order? | A. Blood cultures @SWR-2984
idea:
- [[0]] Look for the shock type clue: cold extremities with raised JVP or low ejection fraction means inotrope (dobutamine); warm peripheries with fever means more vasopressor.
- [[1]] A single hot joint in an RA patient is not a flare until infection is excluded; never inject steroid first.
- [[2]] Memorize the phrase "low tidal volume to allow permissive hypercapnia"; tidal volume is calculated from predicted body weight, so height, not weight, drives it.
- [[3]] Cultures first, then antibiotics immediately; "calcitonin" and "lactate dehydrogenase" are look alike traps for procalcitonin and lactate.
- [[4]] Read the option letter by letter: lactate is a sepsis marker, lactate dehydrogenase is not.
- [[5]] Add fever or flank pain to the same urinalysis and the answer moves from oral nitrofurantoin to culture plus systemic (often IV) therapy.
- [[6]] Anti-HBs alone = vaccine; anti-HBs plus anti-HBc = recovered from infection.
- [[7]] A normal-looking neutrophil percentage is a trap: always multiply by the total WBC to get the absolute count.
- [[8]] Rash after amoxicillin in a sore-throat patient means EBV, not penicillin allergy.
- [[9]] "Most important test" in febrile neutropenia = blood cultures; "most appropriate management" = cultures plus immediate IV broad-spectrum antibiotics.
== Vaccines & other infections  (3/4)
q: A 53-year-old man presents with suprapubic tenderness and dysuria. He has no fever. Which of the following makes this patient high-risk? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3298

All investigations including brain MRI is normal. What is the most likely diagnosis? | D. Myoclonic @SWR-3302

q: CHF, HTN, DM I think, any way he has i dont remember maybe lung infection?, what med to stop | C. Metaformin @SWR-3597

q: Long scenario . I can only recall the labs: Hep A antegen negative Hep B surface antegine positive Hep b core antigen IgM pisitive Hep b core antigen IgG negative Hep B surface antibody negative Hep B core antibody negative Hep C negative Which hep virus is the cause of the acute infection? | B. B @SWR-3758
q: 25 years old female went to a hike in the mountains 3 days ago, presented the the ER with fever, S3 and bilateral crepitations. | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-3839
q: Patient came with symptoms of uti or pylonephritis? She has fever and dysuria She was vitally stable except for high temp and maybe hypotensive ? 95/63? Culture is pending How to manage the patient? | A. IV ceftriaxone @SWR-4164
q: diabetic patient came presenting ulcer with signs of cellulitis with erythema, not well demarked and fever What is the antibiotic will give | D. Clindamycin @SWR-4383
q: A patient recently received chemotherapy and now presents with fever. Hb, platelets and total WBC are low; the neutrophil percentage may appear numerically normal despite a low absolute neutrophil count. What is the next step? | B. Start empiric broad-spectrum antibiotics @SWR-4470
idea:
- [[0]] A symptom of the disease (suprapubic tenderness) is never the answer to "what makes this patient high risk".
- [[1]] Brief single jerks = myoclonic; rhythmic repeated jerks = clonic.
- [[2]] If the stem gives only an age of 65 or more and no risk factor, pneumococcal is the age-triggered vaccine; meningococcal needs a risk such as Hajj or asplenia.
- [[3]] Same CHF/DM/HTN patient: infection points to metformin, K 6.0 points to the ACE inhibitor; the lab or event in the stem flips the answer.
- [[4]] Neutropenic fever with RLQ pain is typhlitis, not appendicitis: CT, not endoscopy, and antibiotics before surgery.
- [[5]] IgM anti-HBc is the marker of acute HBV and the only marker positive in the window period; HBsAg alone cannot tell acute from chronic.
- [[6]] Myocarditis plus myositis after a viral illness: enterovirus first, with Coxsackie B as the textbook name.
- [[7]] Fever turns a UTI into pyelonephritis: nitrofurantoin is out, ceftriaxone is in.
- [[8]] Systemic signs (two or more SIRS criteria) make a diabetic foot infection severe; if the stem adds sepsis, gangrene or osteomyelitis, the answer moves to broad IV therapy.
- [[8]] ⚠ Fever can be a sign of systemic infection: if it comes with at least one more SIRS sign (HR >90, RR >20, WBC >12 or <4), IDSA/IWGDF class the diabetic foot infection as severe and favour broad IV therapy (e.g. piperacillin-tazobactam, plus vancomycin if MRSA risk); clindamycin fits mild or moderate infection. Kept as keyed.
- [[9]] Never trust the neutrophil percentage in a leukopenic patient; calculate the absolute count.
== Vaccines & other infections  (4/4)
q: A patient [context uncertain - possibly a motor vehicle accident / trauma / infection patient] was hypotensive and started on IV fluids, then given norepinephrine at 0.2 [mcg/kg/min]. The patient is not responding and remains hypotensive. What is the most appropriate next step? | C. Add dobutamine @SWR-4769
q: A woman with breast cancer is currently receiving chemotherapy. She presents with flu-like symptoms and is concerned about her weakened immune system. She asks which vaccine is safe to receive? | A. Give an inactivated vaccine @SWR-4908
q: {{rep:×2}} Patient come with history of blood transfusion before 10 years AST = 20 ALT = 22 Serology: HBsAg + HBcAb IgM - HBcAb IgG + HBsAb - HBeAg + What is the diagnosis? | B. Chronic inactive HB infection @SWR-5055
idea:
- [[0]] If the shock is septic the next step is a second vasopressor (vasopressin); an inotrope like dobutamine is for a failing pump (cardiogenic shock, or septic shock with proven myocardial dysfunction).
- [[1]] Pregnancy and immunosuppression share the rule: no live vaccines, but the injected (inactivated) flu vaccine is fine.
- [[2]] Anti HBc tells natural infection from vaccine: positive core antibody can never come from vaccination.

q: 25-year-old woman complains of dizziness and feeling light-headed. She is a teacher and most frequently experiences this when she stand up in the class. She has never fainted. She lost 5 kg, but aributes this to eang healthy food. She has noced a recent scar on the back of her hand, which has started to turn very dark as well as generalized skin hyperpigmentaon. What is the most appropriate invesgaon to confirm the diagnosis? | A. Synacthen test ( ACTH smulaon test) @SWR-0867
idea:
- [[0]] Change the vessel to "proximal anterior circulation" with a persistent deficit and the same 12 hour patient gets thrombectomy.
- [[1]] The normal coagulation and platelet results are a distractor pulling you to tPA; the 12 hour clock already excludes it.
- [[2]] Lung cancer screening needs a smoking history in the stem; without it, colon wins.
- [[3]] Obesity tempts you to a GLP-1 agonist, but the exam's first line remains metformin; the GLP-1 agonist is added on.
- [[4]] "Obese" pulls you toward liraglutide; the first-line answer is still metformin.
- [[5]] If the stem says UTIs occur after intercourse, choose postcoital single-dose antibiotic rather than continuous prophylaxis.
- [[6]] Options that forbid or ignore the patient's wishes are rarely right; refer to the qualified professional.
- [[7]] Whether the stem says 'stopped' or 'adjusted', the interacting drug is amlodipine; warfarin trumps it if present.
- [[7]] ⚠ Rifampicin reduces amlodipine levels, so the expected action is to increase its dose or switch to a non-CYP3A4 agent, not to stop it; the compiler himself flags 'stopped' as a likely bad recall (the 'adjusted' version SWR-0785 is more defensible).
- [[8]] Rifampicin weakens amlodipine; if you cannot raise amlodipine, raise the other antihypertensive, never stop the TB drug.
- [[9]] 'Confirm' adrenal insufficiency = stimulation test; 'confirm' Cushing = suppression test.

idea:
- [[0]] Check the interval as well as the start age: every 5 years means high risk, every 10 years means average risk.
- [[0]] ⚠ Current guidance (and a reference question keyed to start at 45) begins average-risk screening at 45, so for a 45-year-old the ideal answer is now, colonoscopy every 10 years, which is not among the recalled options. C is keyed because it has the correct interval, but it delays the start by 5 years; B has the right start with the wrong interval. The full recall may contain a 'now, every 10 years' option.
- [[1]] Hb alone does not decide: hypotension in active bleeding means blood, whatever the Hb.
- [[2]] Hypotension in a bleeding patient means blood, even if the Hb sits above 7.
- [[3]] Obesity is the classic distractor: it protects bone, low BMI is the risk factor.
- [[4]] A low A1C on a sulfonylurea means watch for hypoglycemia, never escalate.
- [[5]] The clue 'drinks little water' flips the answer from cranberry to hydration.
- [[6]] If TSH or B12 appears in the options, choose it before MRI.
- [[7]] Every shock except distributive raises SVR; only septic and neurogenic shock lower it.
- [[8]] If your version says sepsis, the author picks CVP 8; otherwise pick the normal lactate.
- [[9]] Beyond 4.5 hours, never tPA; a large vessel occlusion within 24 hours still earns thrombectomy.

idea:
- [[0]] Initial management of hypercalcemia is always fluids; a diuretic before rehydration is a trap.
- [[1]] Brisk reflexes plus a sensory level equals cord; areflexia points to GBS or cauda equina.
- [[2]] Left-right confusion plus cannot write = dominant parietal, even if speech is affected.
- [[3]] Automatisms do not make it focal: the 3 Hz spike-and-wave EEG decides absence.
- [[4]] Agnosia for objects or faces is a temporo-occipital sign; pair it with a visual field defect, not with weakness.
- [[5]] Left right confusion plus a writing or calculation problem is the parietal signature; neglect means the non dominant side.
- [[6]] Excision is for a suspicious lesion; a request for screening in a high-risk patient goes to a dermatologist.
- [[7]] Hypertension plus deep bleed = basal ganglia; elderly normotensive with lobar bleed = amyloid angiopathy.
- [[8]] Horner plus pain and temperature loss on the face is the combination that points to the LATERAL medulla; motor weakness and tongue deviation point medial.
- [[9]] When the choice is CT versus MRI for the cord, discs or nerve roots, MRI wins; CT wins for bone and acute trauma.

idea:
- [[0]] 'Best evidence' means antibiotics; cranberry is the answer only when antibiotics are absent from the options.
- [[1]] Low CrCl alone points to UFH; low CrCl plus BMI over 40 points to the higher-dose UFH option, not 5000 twice daily.
- [[1]] ⚠ The stem (sudden severe dyspnoea, tachycardia, SpO2 88%) suggests acute PE, which needs therapeutic anticoagulation (IV UFH infusion when CrCl is below 30), not a prophylactic dose. The key is defensible only if the question is really asking for VTE prophylaxis in a morbidly obese patient with CrCl below 30.
- [[2]] Diffuse concave ST elevation plus heart failure signs = myocarditis (Coxsackie B), not pericarditis alone.
- [[3]] If a postcoital or prophylactic antibiotic option appears, it beats cranberry juice.
- [[4]] Trigeminal neuralgia under 40 or with other neurological signs is MS until proven otherwise: MRI, not CT.
- [[5]] Do not stop the beta blocker for ordinary HF decompensation; stop it only when there is shock or hypoperfusion.
- [[6]] Young woman + hypertension + bruit = fibromuscular dysplasia; start with Doppler ultrasound.
- [[7]] Hypokalemia + urine pH above 5.5 = distal; hyperkalemia = type 4; glycosuria with normal glucose = proximal/Fanconi.
- [[8]] Normal resting ECG never excludes paroxysmal AF; prolonged monitoring does.
- [[9]] Brisk reflexes put the lesion in the cord; absent reflexes with saddle anaesthesia put it in the cauda equina.

idea:
- [[0]] Facial palsy with hearing loss means AICA (pons, labyrinthine artery); hoarseness and dysphagia mean PICA.
- [[1]] 'Initial test' = duplex US; 'confirm' = CT or MR angiography; 'gold standard' = catheter angiography.
- [[2]] Antibody positive in an adolescent is type 1; the same antibody in a 40 year old not needing insulin is LADA.
- [[3]] Low glucose plus lymphocytes = TB; normal glucose plus lymphocytes = viral; low glucose plus neutrophils = bacterial.
- [[4]] Per the compiler, if the options include the Clinical Dementia Rating, pick it; otherwise the on-road driving evaluation is the most direct assessment of driving safety.
- [[5]] Age of the relative at diagnosis decides it: before 60 means colonoscopy every 5 years, 60 or older means every 10 years.

- [[7]] Liver history decides the drug: no cirrhosis means PPI, cirrhosis means octreotide; either way, transfuse the hypotensive patient first.
- [[8]] Deep, tender or discharging after shaving changes the answer to furuncle; multiple openings on the nape means carbuncle.
- [[9]] If pseudofolliculitis barbae appears in the options for bumps after close shaving in coarse curly beard hair, it is the more specific answer.

- [[1]] Time window kills tPA here; whether thrombectomy is right hinges entirely on basilar vs non-basilar occlusion.
- [[2]] Mucus without blood is allowed in IBS; add blood, anemia or weight loss and the answer flips to IBD or cancer workup.

- [[4]] Whipple disease is chosen only when joints, heart or brain are involved; malabsorption after tropical travel with negative stool studies is tropical sprue.
- [[5]] If tachycardia is listed, it beats cold extremities as the earliest sign; hypotension is always late.
- [[6]] HBeAg tells you how infectious, IgM anti-HBc tells you how recent.

- [[8]] Disease-modifying drugs prevent relapses; they never treat the relapse in front of you.
- [[9]] If the options include medulla and the stem adds dysphagia, hoarseness or crossed sensory loss, think lateral medullary syndrome rather than isolated cerebellar lesion.

idea:
- [[0]] TB is airborne, not droplet: the precaution is an N95 respirator and a negative pressure room.
- [[1]] Do not pick 'droplet' or 'contact' precautions for TB; airborne precautions are always added to standard ones.
- [[2]] Signs and lab findings of the infection (suprapubic tenderness, RBC, nitrites) are never "predisposing"; only host factors such as age-related prostatic obstruction or male anatomy can be.
- [[3]] Read the lead-in: "what makes this a complicated UTI" points to male sex as a classification, while "what increases complications" in a medically free 41-year-old is genuinely debated, which is why the recall is left open.
- [[4]] The word "urgent" flips it: urgent endoscopy is wrong for a stable patient with blood streaks after vomiting.
- [[5]] The same "hematemesis" stem flips to octreotide the moment it mentions jaundice, ascites, spider naevi or known cirrhosis.
- [[6]] A healthy 40 year old with no family history is told to wait until 45; a positive family history changes everything.
- [[6]] ⚠ Current USPSTF (2021) and ACS guidance start average risk screening at 45, so a 45 year old should begin now; option B (start immediately) matches current practice, while A reflects the older age 50 threshold.
- [[7]] If the level is at the upper limit or the patient has diplopia and ataxia, the answer flips from increasing the dose to adding or switching.
- [[8]] Homans sign is unreliable on its own; it raises suspicion, but duplex ultrasound confirms DVT before treatment.
- [[9]] Inflammatory markers and procalcitonin never replace blood cultures in febrile neutropenia; culture first, then antibiotics immediately.

idea:
- [[0]] "Initial step" usually means bedside cognitive testing; "investigation" or "to rule out structural cause" means brain imaging.
- [[1]] When both 'lifestyle modification' and 'exercise' appear, choose the broader lifestyle option: exercise is part of it.
- [[2]] Screening age is set by the youngest affected relative, not by the average-risk start; always subtract 10 years from the relative's age at diagnosis.
- [[3]] If the stem's only twist is "high K" and the choice is NS vs RL, the traditional key is the potassium-free fluid; know that modern data favour balanced fluids being safe.
- [[4]] Low sodium in an edematous HF patient means too much water, not too little salt: diurese, do not give saline.
- [[5]] Hydroxyurea is the answer for preventing ACS; transfusion is the answer for treating it.
- [[5]] ⚠ Reference management order lists oxygen and hydration first, then analgesia and antibiotics, with transfusion as the definitive step; if the lead-in strictly means the first action, supportive care (oxygen, cautious fluids) is favoured, while transfusion is the key treatment.
- [[6]] "Can only move eyes up and down" is the locked in sign of the ventral pons.
- [[6]] ⚠ As worded here, preserved vertical eye movement only is the locked in pattern of a pontine hemorrhage, which favours Pons; the basal ganglia key comes from the fuller recall and should be checked against it.
- [[7]] Steroids treat the attack; biologics and interferon prevent the next one.
- [[8]] Read the age first: adult ITP → steroids; child ITP needing treatment → IVIG.
- [[9]] If Doppler ultrasound or CT angiography appears as an option, that is usually the expected choice for suspected renal artery stenosis.
- [[9]] ⚠ Standard guidance uses duplex Doppler ultrasound as the initial test and CT or MR angiography (contrast) to confirm renal artery stenosis; a non-contrast CT does not show the stenosis itself, so the US option could be favoured if it meant Doppler.

q: -MS patient relapsing remitting presented with one week history of left arm weakness Wbc normal Lymphcytes normal MRI with gadolinium showed something and T6 hyperintense lesion | C. High dose methylpredinose @SWR-3346
idea:
- [[0]] Calcium is the trap: it answers "prevent fractures in osteoporosis", not "decrease falls".
- [[1]] Trigeminal neuralgia in a young patient is MS until proven otherwise, so image with MRI plus gadolinium, not CT.
- [[2]] One acute boil = furuncle; recurrent bilateral fold lesions with sinuses and scars = hidradenitis.
- [[3]] Rash and flushing alone = allergic, prevented by antihistamine; add fever and hypotension and it becomes a hemolytic emergency.
- [[4]] Young woman of childbearing age with any seizure type: avoid valproate; this is what flips the answer in most exams.
- [[4]] ⚠ Current guidance (e.g. NICE) favours lamotrigine or levetiracetam (or carbamazepine) first line for focal seizures, and valproate should be avoided in women of childbearing potential because of teratogenicity; levetiracetam would usually be preferred here.
- [[5]] Furosemide is the classic trap: it belongs only after rehydration and only for fluid overload.
- [[6]] 'New finding, no organ damage' points to lifestyle; 'usually high' or 'already tried lifestyle' points to drugs.
- [[6]] ⚠ A near-identical question (older man, repeated BP 147 to 150/84 to 86, BMI 31, no organ damage) is keyed to lifestyle modification and weight loss as stage 1 hypertension. The key of starting an antihypertensive holds only if 'usually 150/90' means lifestyle has already failed, or under ACC/AHA staging (≥140 systolic = stage 2). The recaller was unsure of the values, so check the exact stem.
- [[7]] Age 65 plus smoking = AAA ultrasound; if the options also include low-dose chest CT, read the age and pack-years carefully.
- [[8]] Separate the two frameworks: the traditional one counts sex as a risk factor, while age counts only at the extremes (elderly or young children).
- [[9]] New symptoms on MS treatment = steroids now; DMT change is a later, preventive decision.

idea:
- [[0]] Read the stem: active symptoms means treat (culture + antibiotics); asking how to prevent recurrence means hydration/cranberry first.
- [[0]] ⚠ The stem is a partial recall. If the woman presents with an active symptomatic episode, culture plus antibiotics is the standard answer (cranberry/hydration does not treat an established UTI). Cranberry plus hydration is correct only for prevention of recurrence.
- [[1]] In a confused marathon runner who drank a lot of water, check sodium first: exercise-associated hyponatremia changes the plan.
- [[2]] Even back pain alone with a normal neuro exam in a cancer patient gets steroids plus urgent MRI; do not wait for deficits.
- [[3]] If prophylactic antibiotics appear in the options, they beat cranberry.
- [[4]] Excision is for a suspicious or changing lesion; a high-risk patient with benign moles needs surveillance by dermatology, not surgery.
- [[5]] Intercourse-triggered UTI = postcoital single dose, not 6 months of daily antibiotics.
- [[6]] A single mildly high fasting glucose with an A1c under 7% is not a reason to escalate treatment.
- [[7]] Serology cannot confirm eradication because antibodies stay positive; use urea breath test or stool antigen after treatment.
- [[8]] If rasburicase is among the options for a bulky high risk lymphoma it beats allopurinol; with no rasburicase or hydration listed, pick allopurinol.
- [[9]] Low CO with a high PCWP points to cardiogenic; high JVP with a low PCWP points to obstructive (massive PE).

q: Electrocardiogram shows ST depression in leads II, III, and aVL. What is the considerable risk factor (written like this in the exam)? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-4395

q: A woman with uncontrolled diabetes mellitus has a BMI of 45 kg/m2. She is a smoker and has a sedentary lifestyle. Which of the following lifestyle modifications is most important for improving her diabetes? | A. Weight reduction @SWR-4469
q: CT of SAH asking about target BP : | A. 140-160 @SWR-4486
idea:
- [[0]] Facial palsy + deafness = AICA; hoarseness + dysphagia = PICA.
- [[1]] The hike is a distractor; concave diffuse ST elevation plus S3 means viral myopericarditis, think Coxsackie B.
- [[2]] Sodium channel blockers (phenytoin, carbamazepine) aggravate myoclonic epilepsy. Choose levetiracetam.
- [[3]] Trigeminal neuralgia under about 40 years, especially with other deficits, is MS until proven otherwise; the answer is MRI, not CT.
- [[4]] If the stem stresses a woman planning pregnancy, valproate is the trap and lamotrigine or levetiracetam is chosen.
- [[4]] ⚠ Current guidance (MHRA, ILAE) restricts valproate in women and girls of childbearing potential because of teratogenicity; for a 19-year-old woman levetiracetam or lamotrigine is generally favoured, with valproate only if others fail and a pregnancy prevention programme is in place.
- [[5]] Low BP trumps the sodium value: hypotension means isotonic saline first even when Na is high.
- [[6]] Read the stem for age and the BP value first; those two numbers decide this whole family of recalls.
- [[7]] When the dementia diagnosis is already clinically made and the question asks for the initial diagnostic TEST, brain imaging becomes the answer; when the patient just presents with forgetfulness, cognitive assessment comes first.
- [[8]] Read the outcome in the lead-in: 'improve diabetes' = weight loss; 'reduce mortality or cardiovascular events' in a smoker = quit smoking.
- [[9]] Bleeding (SAH, ICH) = lower targets; ischemic stroke tolerates high pressure (220/120 or 185/110 with tPA).

idea:
- [[0]] IgM anti-HBc is the only marker that says "acute"; HBeAg says "infectious", not "new".
- [[1]] If the stem gives a test score already, do not choose 'cognitive assessment' again: move to imaging.
- [[2]] Untested memory complaint = cognitive assessment first; already abnormal score = brain imaging next.
- [[3]] Affected relatives of the carrier parent do not raise the baby's risk; only the other parent's carrier status does.
- [[4]] IgM anti-HBc is the only marker that says ACUTE; total anti-HBc only says the person has met the virus.
- [[5]] IgM anti-HBc is the only marker that says "acute"; HBeAg says "infectious", not "new".
- [[6]] New symptoms on MS treatment = steroids now; DMT change is a later, preventive decision.
- [[7]] Decide by volume status first: shock gets isotonic resuscitation, then the sodium is lowered slowly with hypotonic fluid.
- [[8]] Read the lead-in: "modifiable" points to smoking; an unqualified "most considerable" in the bank version was keyed to age.
- [[9]] One scan with old lesions = space; a second scan with NEW lesions = time, and that confirms MS.

q: A patient with MS develops urinary retention and lower-limb hyperreflexia, with a new lesion on spinal MRI. What is the next step? | A. IV methylprednisolone @SWR-5023
idea:
- [[0]] "One lesion last year, three lesions now" = dissemination in time on MRI.
- [[1]] Add one red flag (dysphagia, weight loss, bleeding, or age above 60) and the answer flips to EGD.
- [[2]] Strictly, anti-HCV proves exposure; chronic infection is confirmed by HCV RNA, which is the next step for this donor.
- [[3]] Fecal occult blood is never the answer for established IDA in an adult man; a negative result would not stop endoscopy.
- [[4]] Healthy young non-pregnant woman = uncomplicated; any man = complicated.
- [[5]] If the stem says tPA will be given, the threshold flips to 185/110; if CT shows bleeding, aim for systolic around 140.
- [[5]] ⚠ Keyed option is the best listed choice, but guideline threshold without thrombolysis is 220/120 rather than 220/110; the option likely reflects imperfect recall.
- [[6]] If the stem gives a test score already, do not choose 'cognitive assessment' again: move to imaging.
- [[7]] Ethosuximide is for absence only, and lamotrigine, carbamazepine and phenytoin can worsen myoclonus: for brief arm jerks the classic key is valproate.
- [[7]] ⚠ Valproate is the classic answer, but current guidance (e.g. NICE, MHRA) advises against valproate in women of childbearing potential because of teratogenicity; for myoclonic seizures in a 28-year-old woman levetiracetam (part of option D) is favoured, with valproate only if others fail and under a pregnancy prevention programme.
- [[8]] Contrast plus kidney makes students reflexively choose hydration; a raised JVP flips the answer to furosemide.
- [[9]] A disease-modifying drug is never the answer for an acute MS relapse; give steroids first.

q: An old man is brought by his family due to a history of progressive behavioral changes (I can't remember what exactly), there were no changes in memory, brain MRI reveals frontal lobe atrophy. Which of the following features is most specific for establishing the diagnosis? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-5138
q: Female with jerky movements in the upper limb, given valproic acid with no improvement. What to do? | — no firm answer in the source ⚠ @SWR-5148
idea:
- [[0]] Liver clues (high ammonia, encephalopathy, stigmata) switch the drug from PPI to octreotide; and lactulose is never IV.
- [[1]] Hypernatremia with shock: isotonic saline first for perfusion; hypotonic fluid only after the patient is hemodynamically stable.
- [[2]] "Now asymptomatic" and "resolved in a few days" make this a workup question for an MRI, not an emergency CT or LP.
- [[3]] Normal labs already rule out provoked causes; the next step in a first unprovoked seizure is imaging, not EEG or LP.
- [[4]] Read the lead-in: a 'distinguishing clinical feature' points to behavior change with spared memory, whereas a 'supporting or confirming finding' points to frontotemporal atrophy on imaging.
- [[5]] Female + myoclonic seizures + valproate failed or unsuitable = levetiracetam; never a sodium channel blocker.
